**Summary.** The study experimentally investigates superradiant emission from cold atomic clouds using Fourier imaging to map out the spatial structure of emitted light. By comparing these measurements with theoretical models, the authors successfully demonstrate that collective spontaneous decay is governed by specific, measurable jump operators. This work advances our understanding of how mode competition dictates the macroscopic dynamics in interacting quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial experimental validation for advanced theoretical frameworks describing open quantum systems, specifically demonstrating how collective decay dynamics can be mapped onto measurable spectral properties (eigenvalues/decay rates) of the system's environment coupling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to experimentally measure and theoretically characterize the collective spontaneous emission modes associated with superradiant decay in cold atomic clouds.

**Main result.** The authors successfully measured a highly directional, ring-shaped emission pattern corresponding to the most superradiant mode, confirming theoretical predictions regarding the dominance of specific collective jump operators.

**Method.** The research combines Fourier imaging of emitted light with advanced quantum optical modeling using master equations and diagonalization of decay matrices.

**Model / system.** The system involves cold $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms in an optical dipole trap, whose superradiant emission is governed by a master equation incorporating collective jump operators derived from the vacuum Green's function. Theoretical analysis models mode competition using bosonic and Lindblad approximations.

**Key observables.** Spatial pattern of emitted light in the far field (Fourier plane), superlinear scaling of intensity with atom number ($N$), and decay rates ($\Gamma_l$) associated with collective modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cloud sizes ($\sigma_x, \sigma_r$), Rabi frequencies ($\Omega$), and critical atom numbers ($N_c$). The Fresnel number $F$ governs the dependence on cloud geometry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the system using master equations and diagonalizing decay matrices; limitations include discrepancies between bosonic model predictions (e.g., peak intensity scaling) and experimental observations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the measured ring-shaped emission pattern, compare calculated vs. initial probability distributions of superradiant modes, and show how the spectral gap ($\Gamma_1/\Gamma_3$) depends on cloud geometry parameters like $\sigma_r$ and $\sigma_x$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from measuring the spatial patterns of emission to analyzing mode competition using bosonic approximations, refining the model with a detailed Lindblad operator, and finally deriving theoretical conditions for superradiance based on eigenvalue analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We measure the spatial pattern associated with the superradiant emission from a cloud of cold 87Rb atoms using Fourier imaging. We observe a highly directional, ring-shaped emission structure, which corresponds to a single collective jump operator associated to the most superradiant mode of the ensemble. Using spatial filtering, we isolate this channel and find the typical superradiant burst with superlinear scaling of the intensity with atom number. We compare our results to two models that describe the competition between the various decay channels, finding good agreement. Our work shows that the collective jump operators introduced by Carmichael et al. [Optics Communications 179, 417 (2000)] can be measured and manipulated.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys the use of advanced quantum sensors, specifically Optically Pumped Magnetometers and NV centers in diamond, to revolutionize chemical and materials analysis. It contrasts OPMs' high bulk sensitivity with NVs' superior nanoscale resolution for applications ranging from battery monitoring to detecting free radicals. The paper argues that these technologies are poised to solve complex real-world analytical challenges.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum physics (spin dynamics, coherence) directly into applied chemistry and materials science diagnostics. The detailed comparison between ensemble averaging (OPMs) and localized defects (NVs) provides a rich case study in translating fundamental quantum measurements to macroscopic engineering problems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is overcoming the inherent limitations of conventional analytical tools—specifically regarding sensitivity, spatial resolution, and throughput—in modern chemistry and materials science.

**Main result.** Quantum sensors, particularly OPMs and NV centers, are shown to be transformative platforms capable of performing highly sensitive molecular and material diagnostics in real-world settings.

**Method.** The review contrasts two main quantum sensing modalities: Optically Pumped Magnetometers (OPMs) for bulk measurements and Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond for nanoscale, localized probing.

**Model / system.** The core physical systems are alkali-metal vapor ensembles used in OPMs and point defects ($	ext{N}$ and vacancy) within the diamond lattice forming NV centers. Sensing relies on monitoring spin states via light absorption/fluorescence (ODMR for NVs).

**Key observables.** Magnetic field strength, concentration of free radicals ($	ext{ROS}$), $	ext{pH}$, temperature, and spectroscopic signatures (e.g., $	ext{NMR}$ signals) at various spatial scales.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic sensitivity reaching femtotesla levels; atomic-scale resolution ($\sim 10 	ext{ nm}$) for NVs; operation in zero-to-ultralow magnetic fields ($	ext{ZULF}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The field is maturing toward commercial viability, allowing quantum sensors to routinely address complex analytical challenges previously deemed impossible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the distinct setups for OPM sensing (using alkali vapors) and NV center sensing (showing energy levels and ODMR measurements), alongside physical examples of their deployment.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a review, first introducing the limitations of current analytical techniques, then detailing the physics and capabilities of OPMs, followed by an in-depth comparison with the multi-modal sensing capabilities of NV centers, concluding with diverse applications like *operando* monitoring.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The advancement of chemistry and materials science relies on transformative analytical tools which can overcome the sensitivity, spatial resolution, and throughput limitations of conventional techniques. This review explores the application of quantum sensors - specifically optically pumped magnetometers (OPMs) and nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond - as robust platforms for molecular and materials analysis. We contrast the extreme magnetic sensitivity of macroscopic OPM ensembles with the atomic-scale resolution and multimodal capabilities of solid-state NV centers. We highlight their deployment in zero- to ultralow-field and nanoscale NMR spectroscopy, real-time reaction monitoring, and transient radical and pH detection. Furthermore, we discuss their integration into high-throughput chemical assays and non-destructive materials diagnostics, such as operando battery monitoring. With the ongoing commercialization of these technologies and advances in quantum-enhanced sensitivities, quantum sensors are poised to routinely address complex real-world analytical challenges.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the symmetries governing the Bosonic Kitaev Chain and related quadratic pairing Hamiltonians. It demonstrates that two distinct types of effective symmetries—sublattice symmetry and particle-hole symmetry—govern different topological features like skin effects. The findings provide a comprehensive classification scheme for these 1D bosonic systems, extending to interacting models via mean-field theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed symmetry analysis provides a powerful theoretical toolkit for understanding topological phases in bosonic quantum systems, which is crucial for designing novel superconducting or cavity QED circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how symmetries dictate non-trivial topological phenomena, such as skin effects, in bosonic pairing Hamiltonians beyond the basic Kitaev model.

**Main result.** It establishes that two distinct symmetries—sublattice (chiral) symmetry and effective particle-hole symmetry from quadrature decoupling—can independently be used to construct generalized, stable bosonic pairing models with non-trivial topology.

**Method.** The analysis relies on deriving the dynamics via a dynamical matrix in the quadrature basis and applying topological diagnostics like winding numbers or specialized invariants for bosonic systems.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Bosonic Kitaev Chain (BKC), a quadratic pairing Hamiltonian for bosons. The study extends to general 1D translationally-invariant quadratic bosonic Hamiltonians, including interactions treated at mean-field level.

**Key observables.** Non-Hermitian Skin Effect (NHSE), topological invariants derived from symmetries, and the spectral stability of the system's dynamical matrix eigenvalues.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping ($w$), pairing ($\Delta$), interaction strengths ($U$, $g$), and momentum ($k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on approximations (e.g., leading order in 1/N) or mean-field treatments when considering interacting terms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the BKC and highlights the two key symmetries: quadrature decoupling linked to particle-hole symmetry, and sublattice symmetry linked to an effective time-reversal symmetry.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the basic BKC model by identifying its inherent symmetries. It then develops generalized models based on preserving only one of these symmetries. The structure progresses through classifying all 1D quadratic systems and finally discusses extending the framework to interacting, non-quadratic Hamiltonians using mean-field theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The bosonic Kitaev chain (BKC) model is a deceptively simple looking quadratic pairing Hamiltonian. Despite being purely Hermitian, it exhibits a number of striking non-Hermitian topological phenomena, including skin effects. We show here how symmetries play a key role in this model, and how identifying these allows one to develop generalized BKC-like models. We emphasize the surprising fact that any quadratic bosonic pairing Hamiltonian with a sublattice (chiral) symmetry necessarily has a dynamical matrix with an effective time reversal symmetry. This symmetry is unrelated to physical time-reversal, but enables non-trivial topological invariants. We also discuss how this symmetry is unrelated to another key property of the BKC, the decoupling of quadrature dynamics. This feature can instead be connected to a distinct symmetry, namely an effective particle-hole symmetry of the dynamical matrix. We discuss non-trivial generalized BKC models that only keep one of these two effective symmetries intact. We also provide a classification of all translationally-invariant 1D pairing Hamiltonians, and show connections between the BKC and a well-studied non-Hermitian fermionic system, the symplectic Hatano-Nelson model.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an effective field theory description using the Keldysh-replica NLSM for entanglement dynamics in a monitored BCS superconductor. By analyzing the RG flow of this model, the authors predict that the system settles into a critical phase characterized by super-logarithmic entanglement scaling ($\ln^2 L$). This result explains numerical findings without invoking topological terms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a field-theoretic understanding of non-equilibrium steady states in superconducting systems under continuous monitoring, linking concepts from critical phenomena (RG flow) to quantum information measures (entanglement entropy).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates entanglement dynamics in a monitored superconducting chain, focusing on characterizing the critical phase established by the interplay between unitary evolution and projective measurements.

**Main result.** The system exhibits super-logarithmic steady-state entanglement scaling, $S(L) \sim \ln^2 L$, driven by a weak-anti-localization flow in the effective field theory description.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Keldysh-replica non-linear sigma model (NLSM) formalism combined with one-loop renormalization group (RG) calculations to analyze the low-energy fluctuations.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional spinful s-wave BCS chain subjected to rare, spin-resolved projective measurements ($\gamma \ll J, \Delta$). The effective dynamics are mapped onto an SO(R) target manifold in replica space.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy scaling $S_s(L)$, and the running coupling constant $g(\ell)$ derived from RG flow.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rare-measurement regime ($\gamma \ll J, \Delta$), Replica limit ($R	o 1$), and the system size $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a rare-measurement limit and relies on projecting fluctuations onto an SO(R) manifold after applying saddle point approximations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes, but the core results concern scaling laws derived from RG flow equations.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Keldysh-replica NLSM for entanglement dynamics. It analyzes how measurement backaction constrains the system to an SO(R) manifold. Finally, a one-loop RG analysis of this model yields the super-logarithmic scaling result.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a Keldysh-replica non-linear sigma model (NLSM) for the entanglement dynamics of a monitored one-dimensional spinful $s$-wave BCS chain in the rare-measurement regime, $γ\ll J,Δ$. Although the clean spinful $s$-wave BCS Hamiltonian belongs to symmetry class CI, spin-resolved measurements and projection to a conserved $f$-sector reduce the effective problem to class C. Starting from the corresponding parent symplectic saddle, we show that measurement backaction and the pairing amplitude impose complementary mass constraints that gap out different fluctuation channels. Their interplay dynamically projects the surviving massless modes onto an $\textrm{SO(R)}$ target manifold in replica space. A one-loop renormalization group analysis of this $\textrm{SO(R)}$ NLSM shows that, in the replica limit $R\to1$, the beta function becomes negative, producing a weak-anti-localization flow. This flow yields a super-logarithmic steady-state entanglement scaling $S(L)\sim \ln^2 L$ in the rare-measurement regime. Our field-theoretic result explains the numerical evidence reported in the companion Letter [arXiv:2604.04375] and shows that a topologically trivial monitored $s$-wave superconductor can realize an $\textrm{SO(R)}$ weak-anti-localizing critical phase without relying on a Wess-Zumino-Witten term.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the critical physics of quantum circuits subjected to measurements that break a global U(1) symmetry. By mapping the dynamics to a classical exclusion process in the large dimension limit, the authors prove that this symmetry breaking drives the system to a universal critical point identical to non-symmetric cases. This contrasts sharply with symmetry-preserving measurements and provides insights into how measurement protocols control quantum criticality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium quantum dynamics in open systems, showing how measurement back-action dictates universal critical behavior, which is highly relevant for understanding decoherence and information scrambling in physical platforms like superconducting qubits or trapped ions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how local projective measurements that explicitly break a global U(1) symmetry affect the critical properties of random quantum circuits.

**Main result.** Symmetry-breaking measurements act as relevant perturbations, driving the system to the same universal critical behavior as generic monitored circuits. Furthermore, this process prevents a charge-sharpening transition by keeping the charge correlation length finite.

**Method.** The analysis uses the large local Hilbert-space limit ($d 	o \infty$) to map the quantum dynamics onto an exactly solvable classical statistical mechanics model (a symmetric simple exclusion process).

**Model / system.** The system is a monitored random quantum circuit with U(1) symmetry, involving two-qubit gates and local projective measurements. The analysis compares this to circuits with non-symmetric unitary dynamics.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy, charge correlation length ($\xi_{charge}$), and the universality class of the Measurement-Induced Phase Transition (MIPT).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Measurement rate ($p$), local Hilbert-space dimension ($d 	o \infty$ limit), and the symmetry-breaking nature of the measurement operator.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary analytical tool relies on taking the large local Hilbert-space dimension limit to map the problem onto a classical statistical model; numerical support is given for finite dimensions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches the monitored random quantum circuit structure, illustrating how symmetry breaking leads to the generic MIPT universality class, while preserving symmetry leads elsewhere. Other figures detail the mapping to the SSEP and numerical scaling results.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model (U(1)-symmetric circuits with local measurements). It then analyzes the large $d$ limit by mapping entanglement entropy to a classical process. The core result proves the relevance of symmetry breaking, contrasting it with symmetry-preserving cases and providing numerical verification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the critical properties of random quantum circuits with a $U(1)$ symmetry subject to local projective measurements that explicitly break this symmetry. We find that, at the measurement-induced phase transition, symmetry-breaking measurements act as a relevant perturbation at large scales, leading to the same universal critical properties as the corresponding monitored random circuit with non-symmetric unitary dynamics. In particular, we consider monitored $U(1)$-symmetric Haar-random circuits in the limit of large local Hilbert-space dimension, where the trajectory-averaged entanglement entropy can be exactly obtained in terms of a classical statistical mechanics model. In this model, the charge associated with the conservation law follows a symmetric simple exclusion process, in which symmetry-breaking measurements correspond to disordered defects that create and destroy charges. We prove that the charge correlation length remains finite for any measurement rate, ruling out a charge-sharpening transition, in contrast to the case of symmetry-preserving measurements. We further support our predictions at finite local Hilbert-space dimension through numerical finite-size scaling analyses of the entanglement transition in monitored $U(1)$-symmetric Haar and stabilizer random circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents novel computational techniques to solve large-scale differential equations by mapping them onto tensor network representations. It overcomes memory bottlenecks in operator construction using Pauli decomposition and significantly accelerates convergence during imaginary time evolution. These advancements allow accurate computation of eigenstates for high-dimensional quantum systems previously deemed intractable.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides advanced numerical tools for simulating quantum Hamiltonians derived from continuous physics (PDEs), which is highly relevant for studying open quantum systems or lattice approximations of continuum theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Solving Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) numerically, which becomes computationally expensive as the dimension of the discretized operator increases.

**Main result.** The authors developed techniques to efficiently construct Matrix Product Operators (MPOs) via Pauli-string decomposition and accelerated convergence using a multistage state-refinement heuristic.

**Method.** The core methods involve representing differential operators as MPOs, constructing these MPOs by expanding the operator in the Pauli basis with memory reduction techniques, and accelerating imaginary time evolution through multi-stage refinement.

**Model / system.** The work applies to solving eigensystems arising from Sturm–Liouville problems (e.g., Laplacian on a 1D lattice or 2D anharmonic oscillator), mapping them onto quantum systems solvable via tensor networks.

**Key observables.** Eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the differential operator; fidelity of computed eigenstates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the discretized operator ($N$), the number of qubits ($n=\log_2 N$), and the bond dimension ($\chi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the underlying operators can be efficiently decomposed into a polynomial number of Pauli strings; accuracy degrades for disordered systems due to increased entanglement.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but mathematical propositions (e.g., Proposition 1-7) and an algorithm (Algorithm 1) are central to the derivation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the computational bottleneck of large PDEs, proposing MPO construction via Pauli decomposition with memory optimization, accelerating imaginary time evolution, and validating these techniques on specific physical models like the Laplacian.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical numerical techniques for solving partial differential equations (PDEs) become computationally expensive as the dimension of the discretized differential operator increases. For PDEs giving rise to Sturm--Liouville problems, tensor network (TN) methods can be highly productive: an operator of dimension $N\times N$ can be represented as a matrix product operator (MPO) using only $n=\log_2(N)$ qubits, enabling computation of eigenvalues and eigenvectors via imaginary time evolution (ITE). However, this remains computationally challenging. First, most methods for generating MPOs of large operators without explicit tensor-product structure require prohibitively large memory. Second, the number of Trotterization steps for convergence in conventional ITE increases rapidly with $n$. We present techniques to mitigate both challenges for certain sparse, structured differential operators. To address the first, we construct the MPO by expanding the operator in the Pauli-string basis, enabled by an analytical expression for the Pauli basis coefficients that reduces the memory requirement from $\mathcal{O}(2^{n+1})$ to $\mathcal{O}(2n)$. To address the second, we propose a multistage state-refinement heuristic that accelerates ITE convergence, reducing convergence time by up to two orders of magnitude. Using this TN framework, we compute the first 32 eigenstates of a Laplacian of dimension exceeding $10^6$ with fidelity above $0.95$ using a 20-qubit MPO. We further validate the method on the 2D anharmonic oscillator and investigate disordered systems, where increasing random potential strength degrades accuracy and limits the approach.

</details>

**Summary.** The research addresses the critical challenge of stray electric fields corrupting measurements on highly sensitive Rydberg atoms. The authors introduce and experimentally validate a novel method that uses photo-ionization to generate an in-vacuum plasma capable of neutralizing these detrimental fields. This technique restores spectral coherence, enabling reliable quantum control experiments on trapped Rydberg arrays.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, practical technique for mitigating environmental noise (static/DC electric fields) in advanced Rydberg quantum simulations, directly impacting the feasibility of high-fidelity quantum control experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Uncontrolled stray electric fields severely degrade quantum control experiments involving highly sensitive Rydberg atoms.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a universal method using in-vacuum plasma generation to efficiently eliminate detrimental stray electric fields, restoring stable and coherent excitation of individual Rydberg states.

**Method.** They employ photo-ionization by laser-cooled atoms to create an in-vacuum plasma source that neutralizes the ambient electric field, which is then verified by monitoring spectroscopic shifts.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses Strontium (Sr) Rydberg atoms trapped in an optical tweezer array platform. The system's sensitivity arises from the large dipole moments of highly excited Rydberg states ($n \gg 1$).

**Key observables.** Rydberg state resonance frequency shifts, Rabi oscillation frequencies, and spectral homogeneity across the atom array.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Stray electric field strength (initially $>10 	ext{ V/cm}$), ionization rates ($\sim 10^3$ ion-electron pairs per ms), and coherence time constants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial stray fields are attributed to material properties of the vacuum cell, and the plasma generation mechanism is robustly controllable for field neutralization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup (Rydberg laser ionizing atoms in a MOT), show resonance frequency shifts confirming field dependence, and finally display uniform site-resolved spectra after successful field elimination.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the problem of stray fields causing spectral distortion to presenting the novel plasma neutralization protocol. It then validates this by showing recovery of coherent Rabi oscillations and achieving highly homogeneous excitation across the array.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rydberg atoms are highly sensitive to external electric fields due to their exaggerated electronic properties. This unique feature lays the foundation for many of their applications in quantum science. However, an uncontrolled stray electric field can be detrimental, severely degrading their quantum control. In this work, we demonstrate a universal scheme that relies on the efficient creation of an in-vacuum plasma source by photo-ionizing laser-cooled atoms to eliminate detrimental electric fields in a Rydberg-atom tweezer array platform, requiring only readily available resources. With this method, we began with a Stark-ionized Rydberg continuum spectrum caused by a large, unknown stray electric field and ultimately recovered stable, coherent excitation of an individual Rydberg state after fully eliminating the field. Our method is directly applicable to existing Rydberg-atom platforms and can also be useful in other experiments sensitive to stray electric fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper shows that by engineering collective enhancement mechanisms in a superconducting circuit heat engine, one can circumvent the classical power-efficiency trade-off. The authors demonstrate that the engine can approach Carnot efficiency while still producing finite power, confirming theoretical predictions using an implementable quantum platform.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly connects abstract thermodynamic bounds in finite-time physics to a concrete, experimentally realizable platform (circuit-QED), providing a blueprint for designing quantum devices based on enhanced dissipative processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research addresses the fundamental question of whether a quantum heat engine can approach Carnot efficiency while operating at finite power, overcoming limitations faced by classical Markovian engines.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that using a superconducting-circuit platform and exploiting collective enhancement mechanisms allows the engine to achieve $\eta = \eta_C - O(1/N)$ while maintaining $P = O(N)$.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical derivation of thermodynamic quantities (heat, work) with numerical simulation on an effective quantum master equation describing the working medium.

**Model / system.** The system is a superconducting-circuit heat engine utilizing two coupled cavities and a qubit, emulating collective enhancement via a coupler-assisted swap (CAS-$c$) interaction. The dynamics are modeled using the GKSL equation under various approximations.

**Key observables.** Efficiency ($\eta$), Power ($P$), Heat current ($J$), Work rate ($\dot{W}$), and Entropy production ($\Sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling parameter $N$ (total photon number) is crucial, as the result holds in the limit where $\eta - \eta_C = O(1/N)$ and $P = O(N)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include the Born–Markov approximation, adiabatic elimination of auxiliary cavities, and operating within specific dispersive parameter regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (cavities, qubit), the two-stroke cycle protocol in phase space, and numerical confirmation showing $P \propto N$ and $\eta_C - \eta \propto 1/N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the classical power-efficiency trade-off to proposing a quantum mechanism (CAS-$c$), deriving an effective master equation for the working medium, and finally demonstrating analytically and numerically that this mechanism achieves the desired scaling limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Whether a heat engine can approach Carnot efficiency while maintaining finite power is a fundamental question in finite-time thermodynamics. For classical Markovian heat engines with local interactions, the power-efficiency trade-off forbids an asymptotic approach to Carnot efficiency at finite power. In quantum systems, by contrast, degeneracy, symmetry, and collective jumps have been theoretically predicted to enable such an asymptotic attainment by enhancing activity. It has remained open, however, whether this mechanism can be realized in an experimentally implementable heat engine. In this Letter, we propose a superconducting-circuit heat engine that emulates the collective enhancement, thereby enabling an asymptotic approach to Carnot efficiency at finite power. This result demonstrates that, in an implementable model, such an enhanced dissipative mechanism circumvents the power-efficiency trade-off of classical Markovian engines. Our work connects abstract bounds in finite-time thermodynamics to a concrete circuit-QED platform and suggests a route toward quantum-device design based on collectively enhanced dissipative processes.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors experimentally demonstrated Entanglement Sudden Death (ESD) for two photonic qubits using superconducting circuit resonators. By monitoring entanglement decay under natural dissipation from leaky environments, they confirmed that entanglement can vanish in finite time even when the underlying photon loss is asymptotic. This work validates a key theoretical prediction regarding decoherence in realistic quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial experimental confirmation of a non-trivial quantum phenomenon (ESD) in a realistic, naturally dissipative environment, advancing the field of open quantum systems beyond idealized artificial channels.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to experimentally demonstrate Entanglement Sudden Death (ESD) induced by natural environmental dissipation in a quantum system.

**Main result.** The experiment successfully demonstrated that the entanglement between two photonic qubits decays completely to zero within a finite time due to coupling with natural leaky resonators, confirming ESD.

**Method.** The researchers used a circuit QED platform involving superconducting qubits and leaky microwave resonators to prepare entangled states and monitor their decay dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two photonic qubits stored in leaky resonators coupled to superconducting circuits. The evolution is governed by a Lindblad master equation, modeling interaction with a Markovian reservoir.

**Key observables.** Concurrence ($C$), which quantifies entanglement; density matrix elements ($ho_{10,10}, ho_{01,01}$); and the time-dependent decay of these quantities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photonic decay rates ($\kappa_1, \kappa_2$); resonator frequencies ($\omega_{r1}, \omega_{r2}$); and the initial entanglement strength parameter ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Born-Markov approximation for modeling environmental coupling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the setup, pulse sequences, measured concurrence decay showing a sudden drop to zero (ESD), and time evolution plots of density matrix elements.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical setup using superconducting circuits, describes state preparation via controlled coupling, models the dynamics using master equations, measures entanglement decay over time, and compares results against theoretical predictions for ESD.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Any quantum system inevitably interacts with its natural environment, which can be modeled as a Markovian reservoir consisting of a continuum of electromagnetic field modes. The quantum coherence of qubits in a zero-temperature natural reservoir decays asymptotically, whereas the quantum entanglement of two qubits coupled to such reservoirs may disappear in a finite time. This phenomenon, referred to as entanglement sudden death (ESD), has been simulated with artificially engineered dissipative channels, but ESD induced by natural dissipative channels has not been confirmed. We here present the first demonstration of natural-dissipation-induced ESD for two photonic qubits, each stored in a leaky resonator of a superconducting circuit. The disentanglement dynamics of the two photonic qubits is monitored with two ancilla superconducting qubits, which can be controllably coupled to the corresponding leaky resonators. The techniques developed in our experiment pave the way for experimental exploration of entanglement dynamics in natural environments.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that decoherence, usually viewed as detrimental, can actually induce robust topological phases in interacting many-body systems. By analyzing the evolution of correlation matrices under a Lindblad master equation, they show that the noise generates spectral features like point gaps and winding numbers. This leads to observable physical consequences, such as asymmetric diffusion whose direction is dictated by the system's topology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical mechanism showing that non-Hermitian physics and topological invariants can emerge purely from open system dynamics (stochastic noise) rather than being imposed by an effective Hamiltonian, which is highly relevant for understanding realistic quantum devices subject to dissipation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate whether decoherence, conventionally seen as an obstacle to topology, can instead induce topological phenomena in interacting many-body systems.

**Main result.** The noise-averaged dynamics realize a topological phase characterized by a winding number and the non-Hermitian skin effect. This is manifested dynamically through asymmetric diffusion fixed by the system's interactions.

**Method.** Analysis involves solving the equation of motion for two-particle correlation matrices derived from the Lindblad master equation, utilizing momentum-space representations and spectral analysis (winding numbers).

**Model / system.** The model is a 1D lattice system of spinless fermions whose time evolution is governed by a Lindblad equation incorporating both coherent Hamiltonian dynamics and environment-induced dephasing jumps. The complexity arises from the interaction terms in the noise operators.

**Key observables.** Asymmetric diffusion, winding number ($
u$), non-Hermitian skin effect, and the spectrum of the superoperator $\hat{C}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength (related to $B$ parameter), lattice hopping ($t$), and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on tracking correlation matrices via a linear evolution equation in the superoperator space, requiring various truncations of the infinite-dimensional problem.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral structure (point gaps) in both single-particle and two-particle sectors, demonstrating how non-zero winding numbers guarantee boundary modes (skin effect) and asymmetric diffusion in time evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general framework using the Lindblad equation for correlation matrices to analyzing symmetries and spectral properties in momentum space. It then details how breaking inversion symmetry opens a point gap, leading to the prediction of skin effects and finally demonstrating the resulting asymmetric diffusion in long-time dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Decoherence is conventionally regarded as an obstacle to realizing topological quantum phases. This has motivated extensive efforts to suppress noise in candidate topological materials and devices. Here, we show that decoherence can instead induce topological phenomena. We demonstrate this in a lattice system subject to environment-induced dephasing. The noise-averaged dynamics, governed by an interacting quantum master equation, realize a topological phase characterized by a winding number and the non-Hermitian skin effect. The dynamical consequence is striking: the correlated nature of the stochastic noise yields asymmetric diffusion, whose direction is fixed by the winding number and is reversible only through a topological phase transition. This effect is induced purely by interactions, distinguishing it from previous studies of free, effectively single-particle systems. It also disappears upon postselecting measurement outcomes, confirming that it is a genuinely open-system phenomenon with no effective Hamiltonian description. Remarkably, the model remains analytically tractable. Our results establish correlated quantum noise as a route to topology in open many-body systems, beyond free-particle and non-Hermitian Hamiltonian paradigms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the complex stochastic dynamics of particles in correlated fluctuating environments, showing that simple linear models fail due to memory effects. By deriving generalized Langevin equations, the authors characterize anomalous diffusion regimes—including superdiffusion and run-and-tumble motion—that arise from both external correlations and self-generated fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides advanced theoretical tools for modeling open quantum systems where dissipation and memory effects are crucial, linking concepts from condensed matter (viscoelasticity) to particle transport phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses how the effective dynamics of mesoscopic particles deviate from simple linear models when immersed in complex, fluctuating, or correlated media.

**Main result.** The effective dynamics are generally non-linear and non-Markovian, exhibiting anomalous diffusion (e.g., superdiffusive behavior) depending on dimensionality and whether the field is self-generated or external.

**Method.** Theoretical derivation using projection operator formalisms (like Mori-Zwanzig) and analyzing spectral densities $S(\omega)$ to characterize memory effects in generalized Langevin equations.

**Model / system.** A probe particle interacting with a fluctuating, correlated Gaussian field $\phi(x)$. The system can be modeled by coupling the particle's motion to the bath dynamics, leading to non-Markovian friction kernels.

**Key observables.** Mean Square Displacement (MSD), Power Spectral Density $S(\omega)$, and deviation from simple exponential decay in relaxation dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Correlation length $\xi$, dimensionality $d$, coupling strength $\lambda$ (for self-interaction), and the memory kernel $\Gamma(t)$ or $F(t, x)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The field is treated as Gaussian in some derivations; approximations are made regarding the bath dynamics to derive effective equations of motion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows a probe particle interacting with a correlated field $\phi$ confined by a trap. Figure 6 illustrates particle trajectories and associated field heatmaps for chemotaxis analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from reviewing the failure of simple linear Langevin models to deriving generalized, non-Markovian dynamics in coupled systems, culminating in specific analyses of self-chemotactic motion exhibiting dimension-dependent anomalous diffusion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The effective dynamics of a colloidal particle immersed in a complex medium at equilibrium is usually described in terms of a linear overdamped Langevin equation, possibly with memory. However, numerical simulations and experiments have shown that this linear model fails, suggesting that the effective dynamics of the probe is actually nonlinear. Focusing on the case in which the medium is described by a fluctuating and correlated Gaussian field, linearly coupled to the colloid, we derive this effective dynamics and discuss its various consequences, including those on the stochastic thermodynamics of a driven particle. When the field is generated by the particle itself, with negligible fluctuations, the resulting self-chemotactic dynamics turns out to display anomalous diffusion and run-and-tumble motion in low spatial dimension, which we characterise analytically.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper compares the Input-Output (IO) approach with the Quantum Langevin equation formalism for optomechanical systems. It finds that while both methods describe dispersive coupling, the standard Langevin treatment incorrectly predicts dissipative coupling when decay rates depend on mechanical position. The authors conclude that a model-free IO analysis is necessary to correctly account for phase factors and the true physical origin of dissipation in these quantum optical setups.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical check on established methods in quantum optics, highlighting where simplified or model-dependent approaches (like standard L-formalism) break down when moving to more complex physical regimes involving dissipation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To critically assess the validity and range of applicability of the quantum Langevin equation formalism for describing optomechanical systems, especially when dissipative coupling is present.

**Main result.** The Input-Output (IO) approach reveals that standard Langevin formalisms incorrectly predict the generation of dissipative optomechanical coupling when only cavity decay rate dependence on length is considered. Furthermore, L-formalism may miss a crucial phase factor when dissipative coupling is active.

**Method.** The authors compare results derived from the model-free Input-Output (IO) relations approach (based on classical wave equations) against those obtained using the standard quantum Langevin equation formalism for various cavity setups.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on optomechanical systems, specifically utilizing an optomechanical Fabry-Perot cavity and a modified Michelson-Sagnac interferometer where one mirror acts as a mechanical oscillator. The comparison involves analyzing coupling mechanisms (dispersive vs. dissipative).

**Key observables.** Intracavity field ladder operator, stochastic back-action force spectral power density S(Omega), and the relation between input/output fields.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipative optomechanical coupling strength ($g_\gamma$), cavity decay rate ($\gamma$), mechanical oscillator position dependence of $\gamma$, and phase factors.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison relies on simplifying the IO-approach results to match the structure of the Langevin formalism, though discrepancies are found when dissipative effects are included.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem by comparing L-formalism and IO-formalism. It then details the application of both formalisms to specific cavities (Fabry-Perot, MMSI), deriving equations for field operators and backaction forces in each framework before drawing comparative conclusions regarding dissipative coupling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The description of the optomechanical system is commonly based on the quantum Langevin equation formalism. This framework is introduced phenomenologically or based on a model Hamiltonian. However, once dealing with the optomechanical Fabry-Perot cavity or the modified Michelson-Sagnac interferometer with a semitransparent mechanically active membrane inside, a model-free consideration is also possible by using an alternative approach. Such an approach, which is based on the classical wave equations in the systems, is popular in the gravitational-wave community where it is termed as input-output relations approach. In this work, using the aforementioned approach, we derived the equations for the ladder operator of the intracavity field, stochastic back-action force, and the relation between the fields at the input mirror. Then we simplified the obtained results down to the range of applicability of the Langevin equation formalism and compared these with the corresponding predictions of the latter formalism. This enabled us to critically assess the validity of the Langevin equation formalism and rectify its range of applicability. In the case where the dissipative optomechanical coupling is involved we identified appreciable problems with this formalism. We found that, disregarding the fact that decay rate of the optomechanical Fabry-Perot cavity depends on its length, no dissipative optomechanical coupling is generated. This is in contrast with the prediction of the standard Langevin-equation based treatment. We found that, staying inside the range of applicability of the Langevin equation formalism, the relation between the fields at the input mirror may not be correct. We found that the Langevin equation formalism misses a phase factor at the input field, this factor turns out to be important for the situation involving the dissipative optomechanical coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general, diagonalization-free method to calculate the Adiabatic Gauge Potential (AGP) required for Shortcuts to Adiabaticity in non-Hermitian quantum systems. By mapping the problem onto Krylov space using bi-Lanczos and Arnoldi algorithms, it reduces complex calculations to sparse matrix equations. This provides an accurate and practical route for controlling dynamics and diagnosing phase transitions in challenging many-body models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful computational tool to analyze non-adiabatic dynamics and phase transitions in open or non-Hermitian quantum systems, which are central topics in modern AMO physics and condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a general, computationally feasible framework for engineering Shortcuts to Adiabaticity (STA) in complex non-Hermitian quantum systems.

**Main result.** The authors successfully recast the difficult-to-compute Adiabatic Gauge Potential (AGP) into a sparse matrix equation solvable via Krylov space methods, applicable across various non-Hermitian models.

**Method.** They utilize an integral representation of the counterdiabatic control and generate the necessary basis using bi-Lanczos and Arnoldi algorithms to reduce the AGP calculation to manageable linear algebra problems.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to general non-Hermitian quantum systems, demonstrated on specific models like decaying two-level atoms, the interacting Hatano-Nelson model, and PT-symmetric Heisenberg chains.

**Key observables.** Adiabatic Gauge Potential (AGP) norm, which serves as a diagnostic tool for detecting phase transitions like PT-symmetry breaking.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tuning parameter $\lambda$ governing the system's evolution; convergence relies on only a small fraction of the full Krylov space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that representing the AGP in the Krylov basis provides a practical and accurate alternative to direct diagonalization, even for complex many-body Hamiltonians.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the method involves deriving recurrence relations and solving matrix equations derived from bi-Lanczos/Arnoldi procedures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the challenge of STA in non-Hermitian settings, developing the general Krylov space framework using integral representations, detailing the algorithmic implementation (bi-Lanczos/Arnoldi), and concluding with physical demonstrations on multiple model systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Shortcuts to adiabaticity (STA) reproduce adiabatic dynamics in finite time, but their counterdiabatic implementation relies on the adiabatic gauge potential (AGP), which is difficult to compute and implement in many-body systems and whose extension to open and non-Hermitian settings has remained largely model-specific. Here, we develop a general, diagonalization-free framework for engineering STA in non-Hermitian systems by representing the AGP in Krylov space. Starting from an integral representation of the counterdiabatic control, we recast the AGP as a nested-commutator series with controlled locality and generate the associated Krylov basis using the bi-Lanczos and Arnoldi algorithms. This reduces the exact or truncated AGP to a sparse tridiagonal or upper-Hessenberg matrix equation that generalizes the Hermitian construction. We demonstrate the method on a decaying two-level atom, where it recovers the exact drive and signals the exceptional point; on the interacting Hatano-Nelson model, where truncated controls rapidly suppress nonadiabatic excitations; and on a PT-symmetric Heisenberg chain, whose AGP norm detects the PT-symmetry-breaking transition. Throughout, the expansion converges with only a small fraction of the full Krylov space, offering a practical route to fast, accurate control of many-body non-Hermitian systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops the concept of using the 'Markov gap' as a quantitative measure for irreducible tripartite entanglement (IrTE) in quantum many-body systems. By analyzing free-fermion chains and extending to interacting models, the authors show that this gap reveals distinct scaling behaviors ($t \sim L^2$) indicative of long-range correlation buildup. It offers a transparent physical interpretation by linking the gap's magnitude directly to the singular values of an associated tripartite null matrix.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable diagnostic tool (the Markov gap linked to ETF null matrices) for quantifying complex multipartite entanglement in non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, bridging abstract information theory concepts with observable physical scaling laws ($L^2$ vs $L$).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the operational meaning of the Markov gap as a diagnostic tool for irreducible tripartite entanglement (IrTE) emerging from short-range quantum dynamics far from equilibrium.

**Main result.** The Markov gap exhibits distinct dynamical behaviors, showing volume-law saturation at $t \sim L^2$, which is significantly slower than ballistic bipartite correlation spreading. The gap's evolution is shown to track the singular values of a tripartite null matrix associated with essential tripartite fermions (ETFs).

**Method.** The analysis uses concepts from quantum information theory applied to free-fermion systems, calculating entanglement measures like reflected entropy and mutual information via two-point correlation matrices. The concept of ETFs and their associated null matrix $N_3(t)$ provides an operational interpretation.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the free-fermion chain, initially analyzed using a simple hopping Hamiltonian ($H_1$). Extensions are made to interacting systems like the XXZ spin chain. The dynamics are studied in quantum quench scenarios across three contiguous regions (A, B, C).

**Key observables.** Markov gap ($M$), Irreducible Tripartite Entanglement (IrTE), onset time of IrTE ($t^*$), and the singular value spectrum of the tripartite null matrix $N_3(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$), initial state type (e.g., crystal, domain-filled, random filling), and anisotropy parameter ($J_z$) for the XXZ chain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially relies on free fermion approximations, though results are extended to interacting systems like the XXZ chain. The interpretation links Gaussian IrTE directly to the number of small singular values in $N_3$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Markov gap dynamics for various initial states (e.g., $|D_Bangle$), showing distinct early-time growth patterns (linear vs. jumps) and long-time volume-law scaling ($t \sim L^2$). Comparisons are made between the gap evolution and the singular value spectrum of $N_3$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes entanglement emergence by first establishing the Markov gap definition for free fermions, demonstrating its distinct dynamical signatures (quasi-linear/staircase jumps) based on initial states. It then introduces ETFs to provide an operational link between the gap and matrix singular values before extending these findings robustly to interacting models like the XXZ chain.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how irreducible multipartite entanglement, a long-range correlation by nature, can emerge from short-range dynamics far from equilibrium. Focusing on the Markov gap as a probe of irreducible tripartite entanglement (IrTE) in free-fermion chains, we uncover qualitatively distinct dynamical behaviors: the Markov gap grows either quasi-linearly or in staircase-like jumps depending on the initial state. We also propose attainable upper and lower bounds for the onset time of IrTE based on the Lieb-Robinson bound. Strikingly, the Markov gap saturates to a volume-law value on a timescale $t\sim\! L^2$, much slower than the ballistic spreading of bipartite correlations. To understand what information about the wavefunctions is revealed by the Markov gap calculation, we introduce the concept of essential tripartite fermion (ETF) and an associated tripartite null matrix. The value of Markov gap closely tracks the number of small singular values of this tripartite null matrix, yielding a transparent, operational physical interpretation of the measure. We further demonstrate that several dynamical signatures persist in the interacting XXZ chain.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores the persistence of quantum correlations in hyperon pairs produced in $e^+e^-$ collisions when subjected to environmental decoherence. It demonstrates that memory effects are vital, preventing complete loss of quantum information seen in Markovian models. The research establishes a robust hierarchy among different quantum resources, making polarized hyperons a unique laboratory for testing open quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges high-energy particle physics (hyperons) with fundamental concepts of open quantum systems and quantum information theory, providing testable predictions for future colliders.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how correlated dephasing environments (environmental memory effects) affect various quantum resources within polarized hyperon-antihyperon systems.

**Main result.** Environmental memory is crucial, leading to information backflow in the non-Markovian regime; furthermore, a clear hierarchy of quantum resources exists: Coherence > Geometric Quantum Discord > Logarithmic Negativity.

**Method.** The analysis uses an open quantum system framework, comparing Markovian and non-Markovian dynamics by calculating time evolution using specific noise models.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is the production of hyperon-antihyperon pairs (Y∗∗) in $e^+e^-$ annihilation. The dynamics are modeled using a correlated dephasing channel incorporating memory effects.

**Key observables.** Logarithmic negativity, geometric quantum discord, l1-norm quantum coherence, entanglement, and Bell nonlocality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Beam polarization (longitudinal/transverse), environmental memory parameter ($\mu$), and the Markovian vs. Non-Markovian regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The noise is modeled as correlated dephasing noise; the analysis utilizes experimental inputs from BESIII for validation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the entire process: Polarized $e^+e^-$ beams leading to $Yar{Y}$ pairs, incorporating polarization and memory effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes quantum resources under varying conditions, comparing different quantifiers to establish a resource hierarchy while modeling open-system dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hyperon--antihyperon pairs produced in $e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow J/ψ\rightarrow Y\bar{Y}$ ($Y=Λ,Σ^{+},Ξ^{-},Ξ^{0}$) constitute a unique high-energy platform for probing quantum correlations through experimentally accessible spin observables. We investigate the impact of correlated dephasing environments on the stationary and dynamical properties of logarithmic negativity, geometric quantum discord, and $l_{1}$-norm quantum coherence under both longitudinal and transverse beam polarizations. Our analysis reveals that environmental memory plays a crucial role in preserving quantum resources. In the non-Markovian regime, information backflow generates recurrent revivals of quantum correlations and significantly delays decoherence, whereas Markovian evolution drives the system toward asymptotic stationary states through an irreversible loss of quantum information. The influence of beam polarization is found to be strongly channel dependent and can substantially enhance the amount of accessible quantum correlations. A comparative investigation of different quantifiers uncovers a clear hierarchy of quantum resources. Quantum coherence remains robust over the widest parameter range, geometric quantum discord survives even in regions where entanglement is strongly reduced, while logarithmic negativity is the most sensitive to environmental degradation. This hierarchy persists for all considered hyperon channels and under both polarization configurations. The dependence of quantum resources on the production angle, azimuthal angle, polarization degree, and memory parameter is examined using experimental inputs from BESIII. The predicted effects are found to be compatible with the precision expected at BESIII and future high-luminosity facilities such as STCF and CEPC.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a scalable Non-Gaussian Matrix Product State (NGS-MPS) method to simulate strongly correlated electron-phonon systems. By applying this technique to Hubbard-Holstein models, the authors map out complex phase diagrams, revealing how phonon interactions stabilize novel electronic phases like bipolaronic stripes crucial for understanding high-temperature superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the use of advanced tensor network methods like MPS/NGS for simulating coupled bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom is highly relevant to open quantum systems modeling in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates strongly correlated electron-phonon (e-ph) systems to decode the complex interplay between electronic correlations and lattice vibrations, which is crucial for understanding superconductivity.

**Main result.** In 2D models, they map a phase diagram showing a metallic phase emerging from competition effects, and in doped regimes, stabilize novel bipolaronic stripe phases via non-local retardation effects.

**Method.** They employ a Non-Gaussian Matrix Product State (NGS-MPS) method, which combines NGS with MPS to efficiently characterize the entanglement inherent in these complex e-ph systems.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on generalized Hubbard–Holstein (HH) models in 1D and 2D. The Hamiltonian includes electronic hopping ($H_e$), phonon propagation ($H_{ph}$), electron-phonon coupling ($H_{e-ph}$), and electron-electron repulsion ($H_{e-e}$).

**Key observables.** Phase diagram mapping, identification of metallic, antiferromagnetic (AFM), charge-density-wave (CDW), and bipolaronic stripe phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping concentration, phonon frequency ($\omega$), electron hopping ($t$), Hubbard repulsion ($U$), and coupling strength ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is designed to overcome limitations of QMC sign problems and standard TNS methods by using a variational ansatz that preserves key symmetries.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the Hamiltonian components, presents the NGS-MPS methodology (including the variational ansatz and optimization workflow), and then systematically applies this method to analyze 1D and 2D HH models across different doping and filling regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate strongly correlated electron-phonon (e-ph) systems via a non-Gaussian matrix product state method. By combining non-Gaussian states with matrix product states, our method efficiently characterizes the intractable entanglement between strongly correlated electrons and phononic modes of unbounded Hilbert space, enabling scalable simulations across broad parameter regimes. In one-dimensional generalized Hubbard--Holstein (HH) models, we identify a pronounced tendency toward phase separation (PS), an instability relevant to recent angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy observations on doped cuprate chain. In two-dimensional HH models, we construct the phase diagram at half-filling featuring a metallic phase emerging from the competition between non-local phonon-mediated attraction and local Hubbard repulsion. Upon doping, we elucidate the role of soft phonons in stabilizing stripe phases. In the antiferromagnet, the stabilization of the fully filled stripe is attributed to a local retardation effect, wherein the charge order is pinned by phonons, leading to a diminished response to spin fluctuations. In the doped charge-density-wave regime, a novel bipolaronic stripe phase with an enlarged unit cell is stabilized via a non-local retardation effect, where long-range phonon-mediated interactions suppress PS. Our work establishes a systematic route to decoding the e-ph interplay that is crucial for superconductivity.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 5.</b> Figure 3(b) presents the results of rearranging atoms from a randomly loaded array of 24 optical tweezers into a target zone of N = 10 atoms. Following the 150ms MOT load- ing step, single atoms are stochastically loaded into the 24 tweezers, giving an initial single-atom filling fraction of ap- proximately 0.6 in the target region (blue bars). After rear- rangement, the single-site filling fraction increases to approxi- mately 0.96 (red bars), substantially enhancing the probability of generating defect-free arrays; this confirms that, in neutral- atom systems, rearrangement substantially mitigates the limi- tations imposed by stochastic loading.</figcaption>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an FPGA-based electronic control system designed to overcome the slow data processing bottlenecks in large-scale neutral atom quantum computing experiments. By integrating all necessary functions onto a PXIe architecture, they achieved microsecond-level feedback latency. This breakthrough enables high-fidelity, iterative rearrangement protocols essential for building complex, defect-free quantum registers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical engineering bottleneck—the control electronics—for scaling neutral atom quantum processors. The low latency achieved is crucial for implementing mid-circuit measurement and real-time Quantum Error Correction (QEC) protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The scalability of neutral atom quantum computing requires integrated electronic control systems with low latency, modular architecture, and real-time feedback capability.

**Main result.** The developed FPGA-based system achieves a total feedback latency of $282\,\mathrm{\mu s}$ and successfully boosts the success probability for generating a 10-atom defect-free array from $65.7\%$ to $95.4\%$.

**Method.** The authors designed and implemented an FPGA-based electronic control system that integrates photon counting, real-time decision-making, and waveform generation into a unified PXIe architecture, eliminating the slow PC bottleneck.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a 1D array of neutral atoms trapped in optical tweezers. The system uses fluorescence detection via Optical Channel Mapping (OCM) to Single Photon Detectors (SPDs) for real-time state readout and feedback control.

**Key observables.** Total feedback latency ($282\,\mathrm{\mu s}$), single-round filling fraction ($\sim 96\%$), success probability of defect-free array assembly ($95.4\%$ after five rounds).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trap spacing ($4.5\,\mathrm{\mu m}$), trap depth ($\sim 0.8\,\mathrm{mK}$), state preparation/gate timescales ($\sim 1-2\,\mathrm{\mu s}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary bottleneck addressed is the latency of external PC control loops; the remaining single-round limit is set by atomic physics, not electronics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup detailing the optical path and electronic feedback loop components (AOD, SPDs, FPGA cards). Timing sequences show the total electronic latency, and histograms/plots demonstrate success probability improvements through iterative rearrangement protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the necessity of low-latency control for fault tolerance, presents the integrated FPGA architecture, characterizes the timing overheads of each subsystem (readout, calculation, transmission), and validates the system by demonstrating high-fidelity, multi-round assembly of defect-free atom arrays.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The scalability of neutral atom quantum computing demands integrated electronic control systems with low latency, modular architecture, and real-time feedback capability. Here, we present an FPGA-based electronic control system that eliminates the PC from the feedback loop, integrating photon counting, real-time decision-making, and waveform generation within a unified PXIe architecture. The system achieves a total feedback latency of $282\,\mathrm{μs}$ and is validated in practical experiments by assembling defect-free atom arrays from 24 stochastically loaded optical tweezers. A single-round rearrangement achieves a filling fraction of $\sim96\%$, while feedback-controlled iterative rearrangement over five rounds boosts the success probability for generating a 10-atom defect-free array from $65.7\%$ to $95.4\%$. This system establishes the electronic infrastructure necessary for mid-circuit measurement and real-time quantum error correction on neutral-atom platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using a Hamiltonian Encoding Framework for Quantum Reservoir Computing, allowing data inputs to drive the system's dynamics. This method bypasses common training pitfalls like barren plateaus while proving its efficacy across both analog superconducting processors and digital gate models. The work highlights that controlled environmental dissipation can constructively stabilize quantum learning processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly connects concepts from open quantum systems (dissipation, Hamiltonian evolution) to machine learning theory by showing how controlled environmental interaction can stabilize and enhance computational utility in a physical quantum hardware setting.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is developing a quantum learning paradigm that is robust, hardware-efficient, and trainable while overcoming issues like barren plateaus.

**Main result.** The Hamiltonian Encoding Framework (HEF) successfully establishes itself as a compact, expressive, and hardware-efficient method for quantum learning, demonstrating comparable performance on both analog superconducting arrays and digital gate circuits.

**Method.** Input data are mapped onto a fixed Hamiltonian ($H$), and feature extraction is achieved through controlled quantum dynamical evolution ($	ext{e}^{-iHt}$), bypassing variational optimization landscapes.

**Model / system.** The framework utilizes Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) implemented via an HEF. The dynamics are governed by the von Neumann equation, allowing for cross-platform testing on superconducting arrays and digital circuits.

**Key observables.** MNIST test accuracies ($\approx 98\%$ vs $\approx 97.5\%$), Principal Component Analysis explained variance ratios, mean local nonlinearity $\langle N angle$, and Fisher ratio ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Evolution time ($	au$), number of qubits/components ($K$), dissipation strength (finite environmental coupling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the dynamics generated by a fixed Hamiltonian provide sufficient nonlinear feature mapping, and it investigates the constructive role of finite dissipation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the HEF schematic, PCA results showing high dimensionality retention, local nonlinearity strength evaluation, and class separability diagnostics.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the HEF for QRC, details its construction using input-dependent and base Hamiltonians, validates its expressivity via feature space analysis (PCA/nonlinearity), compares performance across analog and digital platforms, and discusses the role of environmental coupling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum learning provides a versatile paradigm for information processing by exploiting the intrinsic representational capacity of high-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Here, we investigate a Hamiltonian-encoding framework for quantum reservoir computing that simultaneously addresses three key challenges in quantum learning: trainability, hardware efficiency, and information stability. In this framework, input data are directly mapped onto a fixed Hamiltonian and transformed into expressive nonlinear features through quantum dynamical evolution. By employing the reservoir-computing paradigm, the approach naturally circumvents the barren plateau problem in quantum learning landscapes. We validate the framework across two complementary platforms: an analog superconducting array processor and a digital gate-based quantum circuit implementation. Despite their fundamentally different realizations, both platforms exhibit comparable representational power and achieve competitive learning performance, establishing a unified framework for cross-platform quantum learning. While both implementations achieve comparable performance, the analog processor may offer a more hardware-efficient realization by bypassing the temporal overhead of gate-based decomposition and thereby making more effective use of finite coherence times, albeit at the expense of universality. Furthermore, we find that finite dissipation suppresses quantum-scrambling-induced instabilities at long evolution times and can enhance learning performance, revealing a constructive role for environmental coupling in stabilizing quantum learning dynamics. Collectively, these results establish Hamiltonian-encoded reservoir computing as a compact, expressive, and hardware-efficient paradigm for quantum learning on current-generation quantum platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the fundamental challenge of defining temperature for isolated quantum systems evolving away from equilibrium. It introduces a novel mathematical structure based on the minimum-variance foliation to parameterize these non-equilibrium states. The result provides a generalized, physically meaningful definition of temperature applicable even when coherence effects dominate over simple energy population distributions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for defining thermal concepts in non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, which is crucial for understanding quench dynamics or open system approximations where true equilibrium assumptions fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is defining a meaningful concept of 'temperature' for isolated quantum many-body systems when they are evolving unitarily and far from thermodynamic equilibrium.

**Main result.** The authors propose replacing the standard definition with one based on the minimum-variance foliation, which defines a canonical flow parameter acting as an out-of-equilibrium temperature in the thermodynamic limit.

**Method.** The approach involves reinterpreting energy fluctuations by separating population contributions from coherence contributions and utilizing geometric tools like state space foliations (specifically the minimum-variance foliation).

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on isolated quantum many-body systems, modeled using quantum spin chains with local interactions governed by a Hamiltonian H(I). The theory is developed for both finite and infinite (thermodynamic) systems.

**Key observables.** Energy fluctuations (population vs. coherence), Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), correlation functions K_O(I), and the effective subsystem Hamiltonian H_A.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), system size ($L 	o \infty$ limit), and the separation parameter $\Deltaeta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on establishing analytic properties of correlation functions in small neighborhoods around equilibrium, and for subsystems, it assumes that local observables can be characterized by an effective Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares two foliations (commutant vs. minimum-variance) of the state space; Figure 2 contrasts how these two foliations characterize thermal manifolds for both equilibrium and non-equilibrium states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the failure of standard temperature concepts out of equilibrium, then introducing the mathematical framework using state space foliations (minimum-variance foliation), establishing the resulting canonical flow parameter as the generalized temperature, and finally extending this definition to subsystems via effective Hamiltonians.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Temperature is one of the central concepts of thermodynamics, yet its meaning away from equilibrium remains elusive. This problem is particularly acute in isolated quantum many-body systems: their states evolve unitarily, need not be close to equilibrium, and can retain energy coherence, a feature with no classical thermodynamic analogue. A non-stationary quantum state contains two kinds of energy fluctuations. One is associated with energy populations and has the usual thermodynamic interpretation; the other arises from coherence between energy sectors and drives time dependence. We propose that temperature, also out of equilibrium, locates the state within the family of regular states compatible with its energy-coherence structure. This leads to a natural definition of temperature for regular nonequilibrium states. The resulting inverse temperature is not generally the derivative of thermodynamic entropy with respect to energy. Indeed the principle of maximum entropy does not extend in its usual form; it is replaced by a principle of minimum discrimination information. We also develop the corresponding theory for subsystems, where temperature cannot in general be inferred from the reduced state alone. Instead, it is determined by the induced local thermodynamic structure, with boundary ambiguities removed in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>
