**Summary.** The paper analyzes how the complex eigenvalue statistics of a partially leaky quantum baker map transition between different spectral regimes. By using the complex spacing ratio and comparing results to Random Matrix Theory benchmarks, the authors demonstrate that this crossover is smoothly controlled by tunable parameters like leakage amplitude ($ho$). This has implications for understanding wave chaos in realistic, dissipative open quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for characterizing spectral rigidity in open quantum systems where standard Hermitian assumptions break down, linking chaos diagnostics to non-Hermitian random matrix theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex eigenvalue statistics of a partially open, asymmetric quantum baker map to understand spectral fluctuations in non-Hermitian systems.

**Main result.** A smooth crossover is observed between quasi-1D and 2D Ginibre-like spectral regimes, controlled universally by both the number of openings ($M$) and the reflectivity parameter ($ho$).

**Method.** The analysis focuses on the joint distribution of the complex spacing ratio $z$, comparing results against the Partially Truncated Circular Unitary Ensemble (PTCUE) benchmark.

**Model / system.** The system is based on the classical asymmetric baker map, quantized and modified by introducing partial leakage via 'openings' in position space. The dynamics are controlled by a reflectivity parameter $ho$ interpolating between fully open ($ho=0$) and closed ($ho=1$) limits.

**Key observables.** Complex spacing ratio $z$, joint probability density $P(|z|, 	heta_z)$, classical escape rate ($\gamma$), and fractal dimension ($d_I$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $M$ (number of open channels), $ho$ (reflectivity parameter), and the spectral regime (quasi-1D vs. 2D Ginibre).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The complex spacing ratio metric is used because standard unfolding techniques fail for non-Unitary operators; convergence to universal statistics is conjectured.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate classical survival probabilities, eigenvalue spectra, and the joint density plots of $z$ across different opening geometries and reflectivity values, showing crossover behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model (baker map with openings) and diagnostics ($z$, PTCUE). It then analyzes classical dynamics ($\gamma, d_I$) before focusing on the quantum spectral statistics, demonstrating the smooth crossover controlled by $ho$ and $M$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the complex eigenvalue statistics of the asymmetric quantum baker map with partial projective openings. The classical asymmetric baker map, with its discontinuity at $q=2/3$, is fully chaotic, has no reflection symmetry, and provides a clean setting with tunable escape rate and fractal repeller dimension. We consider three distinct opening geometries in position space: localized (contiguous channels), random, and uniform (equispaced channels), all controlled by a tunable amplitude reflectivity parameter $ρ$ that interpolates between the fully open ($ρ=0$) and the closed ($ρ=1$) limits. We use the partially truncated circular unitary ensemble (PTCUE) as the random matrix theory benchmark. The main focus is on the joint distribution of the complex spacing ratio $z$, defined as the ratio of the distances from an eigenvalue to its nearest and next-nearest neighbors in the complex plane. We find a smooth crossover from a quasi-1D spectral regime, where eigenvalues cluster near the unit circle and the phase distribution of $z$ is peaked, to a two-dimensional Ginibre-like regime, where the distribution becomes nearly uniform and level repulsion is fully developed. Both the number of open channels $M$ and the reflectivity $ρ$ modulate this crossover, and $ρ$ provides an additional continuous control even at fixed opening size. All three opening models converge to PTCUE statistics at large $M$, while differences are most pronounced for the localized model at small $M$. No evidence of an abrupt transition is found. This crossover which suggests a universal behavior, has deep consequences for open quantum and wave-chaotic experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates whether a generalized 'quantum focusing' inequality holds for Rényi divergences in quantum field theory. By constructing a counterexample using specific divergence calculations, the authors prove that such an off-diagonal focusing statement does not hold universally for a broad class of information measures. This constrains the mathematical scope of generalizations of fundamental physical conjectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QFT/Gravity analogies, the mathematical machinery involving divergences and information flow (like relative entropy generalizations) is highly relevant to understanding entanglement structure in many-body quantum systems far from equilibrium.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors investigate whether an off-diagonal quantum focusing inequality, analogous to those derived for diagonal Rényi divergences (RQNEC), holds universally for general Rényi-type divergences.

**Main result.** They show that for a broad class of Rényi divergences satisfying standard axioms, the required off-diagonal focusing statement fails in general, providing a negative answer to the question.

**Method.** The analysis constructs a counterexample using free quantum field theory on Minkowski spacetime and calculating the mixed variation ($\Delta_{12}D_\gamma$) of the Rényi divergence across different null generators.

**Model / system.** The model is a regulated Quantum Field Theory (QFT) setup involving spectator degrees of freedom and two active null pencils. The focus is on how entanglement/information divergences behave when considering correlations between distinct spatial regions.

**Key observables.** Off-diagonal variation $\Delta_{12}D^\gamma$, Rényi divergence $D_\gamma(\Psi\|\Omega)$, and the general structure of the focusing inequality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Rényi parameter $\gamma$ (or $\alpha$), which dictates the specific form of the divergence used in the analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are restricted to divergences satisfying faithfulness, data processing inequality (DPI), tensor additivity, and matched classical-quantum conditioning rules.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the focus is on deriving algebraic counterexamples for the divergence variation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews the context of quantum focusing conjectures, then develops calculations using specific block decompositions of states to compute the mixed variation $\Delta_{12}D_\gamma$ for various Rényi parameters, culminating in the explicit violation of the inequality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum focusing conjecture is a mathematical expression of the idea that semiclassical gravity remains universally attractive. Its off-diagonal part is a monotonicity condition on the double null shape variation of relative entropy on distinct null generators, and has been argued to follow from strong subadditivity of entanglement entropy. Recent proof of a diagonal Rényi quantum null energy condition raises the question: does a full Rényi focusing statement also hold? We answer this question negatively for any Rényi-type divergence satisfying data processing, tensor additivity, and matched classical--quantum conditioning.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a method to extract universal Conformal Field Theory data from real-time Loschmidt echoes following quantum quenches. By mapping the time evolution onto a boundary-CFT partition function, the authors show that ratios of spectral splittings encode fundamental CFT parameters like scaling dimensions and central charge. This provides a robust theoretical framework for analyzing out-of-equilibrium dynamics in critical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful theoretical tools to probe non-equilibrium quantum dynamics using measurable quantities (echoes) that bypass the need for complex state preparation, making it highly relevant for characterizing critical points in physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a method to extract universal Conformal Field Theory (CFT) data from real-time quantum dynamics, specifically using Loschmidt echoes after critical quenches, without needing to prepare the difficult-to-access critical low-energy states.

**Main result.** Ratios of spectral splittings derived from finite-size dependence of Loschmidt echoes are shown to be universal and directly encode differences in boundary scaling dimensions ($\Delta_i - \Delta_j$), allowing for a practical extraction protocol.

**Method.** The analysis involves mapping the time evolution to a boundary-CFT partition function on a strip geometry, utilizing transverse transfer matrices whose spectra yield CFT data. Finite-size scaling protocols are developed by analyzing ratios of decay rates.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on one-dimensional quantum systems undergoing a quench from an initial product state $|\psi_0angle$ to a critical Hamiltonian $H$. The dynamics are diagnosed using the Loschmidt amplitude, $A(T) = \langle\psi_0| e^{-iHT} |\psi_0angle$, which is related to boundary-CFT partition functions.

**Key observables.** Loschmidt amplitude/echo ($L(T)$), transverse transfer matrix spectrum (eigenvalues $\lambda_i$), generalized temporal entropies, and ratios of dynamical exponents ($\Delta_{ii}/\Delta_{11}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size dependence ($N_X$), quench time ($T$), central charge ($c$), and boundary scaling dimensions ($\xi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on analytic continuation from imaginary time (Euclidean) to real time, and the extraction protocols assume that finite-size effects can be systematically removed through ratios.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the tensor network representation of the Loschmidt amplitude via transverse contraction. Plots show linear relationships between logarithms of observables versus scaled time/temperature, confirming power-law decays predicted by CFT theory (e.g., slope $-1/8$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the connection between Loschmidt echoes and boundary-CFT partition functions on a strip. It then details how spectral properties of the transverse transfer matrix encode universal data like central charge and scaling dimensions, culminating in proposing finite-size scaling protocols for experimental/simulation extraction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conformal field theory provides universal predictions for Loschmidt amplitudes following quenches from product states to critical Hamiltonians. Building on this observation, we develop a route to extracting conformal data from real-time dynamics without preparing critical low-energy states. After analytic continuation, the Loschmidt amplitude is described by a boundary-CFT partition function on a strip, whose transverse transfer matrix encodes both the boundary operator spectrum and the central charge. Local space-time perturbations of the amplitude are governed by equilibrium correlation functions, and therefore provide access to critical exponents. In parallel, generalized temporal entropies exhibit scaling with time analogous to the equilibrium scaling of spatial entanglement entropy. We show that the low-lying boundary spectrum can be reconstructed from the system-size dependence of finite-chain Loschmidt echoes, whose damped oscillations encode differences of boundary scaling dimensions. Finally, we propose a finite-size scaling protocol that can extract these quantities from simulations or experiments on state-of-the-art quantum platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an efficient Monte Carlo simulation technique using the NKSOS model to study the multichannel Kondo effect. It achieves a unified description, successfully modeling the transition between weak and strong electron-impurity coupling regimes. This work provides crucial quantitative predictions for transport properties in complex correlated quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous numerical treatment of crossover physics in a highly correlated impurity model provides advanced computational techniques applicable to open quantum systems where tunneling barriers or coupling strengths vary widely.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is establishing a unified theoretical description that connects different coupling regimes (weak vs. strong) for the Kondo effect, which is crucial for understanding strongly correlated electron systems.

**Main result.** The authors successfully use their model to describe the crossover between weak and strong tunneling regimes in the multichannel Kondo problem, predicting universal low-frequency conductance values.

**Method.** They employ cluster Monte Carlo algorithms on a discrete N-channel Kondo solid-on-solid (NKSOS) model, which efficiently simulates dynamics while eliminating critical slowing down.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the multichannel Kondo model using the NKSOS model, derived from an anisotropic Kondo Hamiltonian. This system generalizes to arbitrary channel numbers ($N$) and incorporates features like anisotropy and interacting leads.

**Key observables.** Zero-frequency conductance ($G^*$), universal crossover functions, and transport properties across different coupling regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of channels ($N$), temperature ($T$), and various coupling strengths defining the weak/strong tunneling limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial assumption of identical microscopic transparencies for all channels is noted as a potential limitation, though the method handles general asymmetry terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare simulated conductance values (e.g., 2CK and 3CK) against established theoretical solutions (like Emery-Kivelson), illustrating universal scaling and RG flow in parameter space.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the NKSOS model, details the cluster Monte Carlo simulation method to calculate transport properties, presents results showing agreement with known limits (1CK, 2CK), and finally extends the analysis to higher channel numbers ($N \geq 3$) to unify weak and strong coupling descriptions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Kondo model is a paradigmatic quantum impurity problem realized in a wide variety of experimental platforms and central to the study of strongly correlated electrons. We introduce a discrete model that exactly reproduces the multichannel Kondo model and demonstrate that it can be simulated efficiently. Using cluster Monte Carlo algorithms, we completely eliminate critical slowing down, providing direct access to universal crossover functions and transport properties across a broad range of parameters. Remarkably, the same model captures both the weak- and strong-coupling regimes, unifying descriptions traditionally derived in complementary limits and revealing their common origin. Our method naturally accommodates large channel numbers, anisotropy, interacting one-dimensional leads, and channel asymmetry, yielding predictions for transport properties in charge-Kondo devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study explores five potential polymorphs of AgBeF4 as candidates for high-temperature superconducting materials. Using DFT calculations, the authors determined that these phases exhibit strong antiferromagnetic coupling, particularly in structures forming linear chains analogous to those found in cuprates. The work provides a computational roadmap for synthesizing and understanding novel magnetic fluorides.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state chemistry, the methodology of calculating strong magnetic exchange interactions ($J$) in novel fluorides directly parallels theoretical work done on cuprates and other strongly correlated Mott insulators relevant to AMO physics research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to find novel silver(II) fluorides that could serve as electronic and magnetic analogues to high-temperature cuprate superconductors.

**Main result.** Five polymorphs of AgBeF4 were identified, with two exhibiting exceptionally strong antiferromagnetic superexchange interactions (J values up to -460 meV), suggesting potential superconducting pathways.

**Method.** The research combines global structure prediction algorithms with first-principles calculations (DFT+U) to determine structural stability and magnetic coupling constants.

**Model / system.** The system under investigation is the Be–Ag(II)–F chemical space, specifically focusing on various polymorphs of AgBeF4. The magnetic interactions are modeled using a Heisenberg Hamiltonian approach based on calculated superexchange couplings (J).

**Key observables.** Antiferromagnetic ground state confirmation, strong exchange coupling constants (J), electronic band gaps, and formation energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exchange coupling constants ($|J|$ in meV), Band Gaps (eV), Reaction Enthalpies ($	ext{kJ/mol}$), and structural parameters like bond lengths and angles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The phases are computationally found to be metastable, requiring synthesis via fluorine radicals; the calculations rely on DFT+U approximations for electronic structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate coordination environments, ground state magnetic models comparing results to cuprates, and convergence checks for energy and k-point sampling. Tables list lattice parameters, band gaps, and calculated J values.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically explores five polymorphs using structure prediction, followed by detailed electronic (band structure, DOS) and magnetic (superexchange coupling calculation) analyses to characterize their potential as superconducting precursors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The search for novel silver(II) fluorides is driven by their potential as electronic and magnetic analogues to high temperature cuprate(II) superconductor precursors. Here, we explore the previously uncharted Be Ag(II) F chemical space using global structure prediction algorithms combined with first principles calculations. Focusing on the AgBeF4 stoichiometry, we identify the five lowest enthalpy polymorphs crystallizing in the C2, P minus 1, and P 21/c space groups. All polymorphs show an antiferromagnetic ground state, with AgBeF4_4 and AgBeF4_5 exhibiting unprecedented strong superexchange interactions of J equal circa to minus 460meV and J equal circa to minus 359meV respectively. Those high J values are due to the presence of either [Ag2F7] for AgBeF4_4, or related infinite [AgF2/2+2/1]2 minus chains for AgBeF4_5. Although the phases are found to be metastable with respect to binary difluorides, the thermodynamic analysis suggests that they could be targeted via synthetic routes employing fluorine radicals, with reaction enthalpies reaching minus 370 kJ/mol.

</details>

### [Quantifying randomness with measurement incompatibility](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08697v1)

**Authors:** Sebastian Schlösser, Pauli Jokinen, Martin Plávala, Leevi Leppäjärvi, Leonardo S. V. Santos, Roope Uola  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08697v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `quantum measurements` **4/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **3/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work rigorously connects the concept of measurement incompatibility in quantum measurements to the ability to generate secret random bits. By formulating security bounds using semidefinite programming based on generalized robustness, the authors provide a powerful tool for quantifying device-independent randomness generation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, information-theoretic tools (SDPs) to quantify fundamental resource relations—namely, how much non-classicality (incompatibility) can be extracted into usable randomness against an adversary.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a quantitative trade-off between the observed measurement incompatibility of quantum measurements and the capabilities of an eavesdropper (Eve) in a prepare-and-measure setting.

**Main result.** Measurement incompatibility is shown to be equivalent to the security against Eve's knowledge, providing a direct link between geometric measures of incompatibility and randomness generation protocols.

**Method.** The authors utilize semidefinite programming (SDP) formulations involving generalized robustness witnesses to bound Eve's guessing probability ($P_g$) based on the joint measurability of Bob's measurements.

**Model / system.** The model is a prepare-and-measure quantum communication scenario where an eavesdropper intercepts the message and performs operations described by instruments, analyzed using POVMs and concepts like quantum steering.

**Key observables.** Measurement Incompatibility (quantified by generalized robustness), Eve's guessing probability ($P_g$), and the minimum entropy $H_{\min}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rank of the input state ($\varrho$) is crucial; the analysis extends to scenarios with quantum memory for Eve.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial results rely on assuming full-rank input states, and later extensions involve bounding violations against qubit simulability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates the quantitative trade-off between Eve's guessing probability and observed incompatibility robustness for various measurement settings (MUBs).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the core equivalence (JM $\iff$ security) in simple cases, to developing SDP bounds using generalized robustness, extending this to quantum steering, and finally proving the mathematical equivalence between the initial SDP formulation and a steering-based SDP.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a trade-off between the amount of observed measurement incompatibility and the capabilities of a classical Eavesdropper in a prepare-and-measure scenario. The result is based on a qualitative connection between measurement incompatibility and randomness generation together with the utilization of incompatibility witnesses as randomness certificates. This allows one to use a geometric measure of incompatibility, the generalised robustness, to bound Eve's strategies through a semi-definite program, while providing an explicit protocol for generating randomness from any set of incompatible measurements. By translating the result to quantum steering, we find a tight connection between steerability and randomness generation in a setting using any finite number of measurement inputs. We further show how our techniques can be generalised to scenarios where Eve has a quantum memory by using a dimensional generalisation of joint measurability.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Code-Compiled Quantum Circuits (CCQCs) that allow the construction of deep quantum circuits exhibiting signs of classical hardness. By mapping these complex logical operations onto simple physical interactions using error-correcting codes, they show that simulation cost can be bounded by the encoder's bond dimension rather than entanglement growth. This provides a powerful theoretical benchmark for assessing the capabilities and limitations of near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool to benchmark quantum hardware by creating complex logical circuits whose ideal reference state is efficiently computable classically, which is crucial for testing fault tolerance in AMO or quantum optics settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to determine when a quantum circuit becomes classically hard to simulate, challenging existing resource measures that define simulation boundaries.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that certain code-compiled circuits can exhibit indicators of classical hardness (high entanglement, magic) while remaining efficiently simulable via Matrix Product States (MPS). This shows standard resource measures are individually insufficient to predict simulation difficulty for some circuit families.

**Method.** They construct Code-Compiled Quantum Circuits (CCQCs) using high-rate CSS codes and simulate them by exploiting structural properties: diagonal transversal gates preserve the MPS bond dimension, and permutations are tracked classically. They use both MPS simulation and an exact phase-polynomial backend.

**Model / system.** The model involves mapping complex logical quantum circuits onto physical qubits via error-correcting codes (specifically polar CSS codes). The resulting physical evolution is shown to be equivalent to a simple, onsite interaction structure after encoding.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy, magic content, non-Gaussianity, and the MPS bond dimension ($\chi_E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The peak bond dimension of the encoder ($\chi_E$), which controls the simulation cost regardless of circuit depth for certain codes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the encoded state retains an efficient MPS representation, and the structural properties regarding diagonal transversal gates and classical permutation tracking hold.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the code-compilation framework, showing how logical nonlocal gates are compiled into simple onsite physical operations. Specific examples use the $[[16, 11]]$ polar code to show matched pairs of complex logical circuits versus their physically simulated counterparts.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the CCQC concept, details the simulation mechanism relying on structural properties (diagonal gates/relabeling), presents two complementary simulation backends (MPS and PhasePoly), demonstrates results using the infinite polar CSS code family, and concludes with a near-term application for fidelity estimation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a family of quantum circuits that possess standard indicators of classical simulation hardness including high entanglement entropy, magic, and non-Gaussianity, yet admit efficient classical simulation via matrix product states (MPS). Our construction uses logical circuits of high-rate Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes with enhanced symmetries. Using code automorphisms and transversal diagonal gates from higher levels of the Clifford hierarchy, we realize nonlocal logical Clifford and non-Clifford gates, showing how error-correcting codes can compile complex logical circuits into simple physical operations. Simulation efficiency rests on two properties: (i) diagonal transversal gates do not increase bond dimension, and (ii) permutations are tracked classically via on-the-fly relabeling, avoiding costly SWAP networks. Unlike Clifford or matchgate simulation, our method accepts a broad class of initial states, including dense entangled, magic, and non-Gaussian inputs, provided the encoded state retains an efficient MPS representation. We also release an exact phase-polynomial backend for monomial subfamilies, whose cost is set by higher-degree phase terms rather than entanglement growth. We demonstrate the method on an infinite polar CSS code family, showing bond dimension stays bounded by the encoding cost regardless of circuit depth. These results show that for some circuit families, standard resource measures are individually insufficient to indicate simulation hardness. As a near-term application, we use the compiled MPS as a classical reference for direct fidelity estimation of a quantum device running nontrivial logical circuits. Pauli sampling on the encoded reference, with a Clifford pushback through the known encoder, provides the ideal expectation values, so the logical output fidelity can be estimated from local Pauli readout alone, without costly state tomography.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper describes the operational framework for performing hybrid continuous-discrete variable quantum computation on the QSCOUT trapped-ion system. It details how to use specific laser pulse sequences and a custom language, Jaqal, to implement advanced gates like Conditional Beamsplitter and Squeeze. The work is crucial for advancing hardware realization by providing rigorous protocols for gate execution and state verification.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges theoretical quantum control techniques (Hamiltonian engineering) directly to a physical hardware platform (trapped ions), providing actionable recipes for implementing complex quantum circuits in the CV-DV regime.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper details how to implement a comprehensive qubit-boson gate set for hybrid continuous-discrete variable (CV-DV) quantum computation on the QSCOUT platform.

**Main result.** It provides specific pulse sequences and language commands (Jaqal) necessary to execute advanced gates like Conditional Beamsplitter and Conditional Squeeze, alongside methods for state preparation and measurement.

**Method.** The implementation relies on precisely timed laser pulses driving resonant sidebands (JC/AJC) and utilizing four-tone interactions to engineer complex quantum operations.

**Model / system.** The system is a trapped-ion quantum processor (QSCOUT) using Yb-171 ions, where qubits are encoded in internal spin states ($\sigma$) and motional modes ($a, a^\dagger$) act as bosonic degrees of freedom. The coupling allows for hybrid CV-DV operations.

**Key observables.** Fock state occupation probabilities ($p_n$), the characteristic function $\chi(eta)$, and the Wigner representation $W(\gamma)$ are key diagnostic tools used to verify gate fidelity and characterize motional states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate parameters include rotation angles ($	heta$) and phases ($\phi$); squeezing is controlled by the complex parameter $\zeta$; detunings ($\Delta\omega, \delta$) govern sideband resonance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that motional modes can be truncated in the Fock basis (e.g., $|n=10angle$), and state preparation relies on controlled sequential application of specific pulse sequences.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate fitting beating oscillations from AJC gates to extract Fock occupancies, and show circuits for probing $\chi(eta)$ using conditional displacement gates, alongside examples of tomography data.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the platform (QSCOUT), defines fundamental gates (JC, AJC, CD, CR), details advanced gate implementations (BS, Squeeze) via specific Hamiltonians and pulse sequences, and concludes with measurement protocols for state characterization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Quantum Scientific Computing Open User Testbed (QSCOUT) has developed a qubit-boson gate set for hybrid continuous-discrete variable (CV-DV) quantum computing. This document outlines how to utilize these gates on QSCOUT using Just Another Quantum Assembly Language, Jaqal\textsuperscript{TM}.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes 'correlator geometry' as a theoretical tool for analyzing families of higher-order correlators in quantum many-body physics. By decomposing the geometry into reducible and irreducible parts, it quantifies genuinely novel information encoded in the system dynamics. This framework offers a systematic way to interpret complex data from programmable quantum simulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, systematic geometric language to interpret complex, high-dimensional data generated by modern quantum simulators studying strongly correlated systems, moving beyond simple correlation functions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a structured framework for the collective analysis of higher-order correlator families, as these are increasingly accessible from quantum simulators but their physical meaning is difficult to infer due to redundancy and interdependence.

**Main result.** The authors introduce 'correlator geometry' which allows quantifying irreducible information via volume profiles, revealing hidden structures in many-body dynamics inaccessible from individual measurements.

**Method.** The framework represents correlators as inner products between operator words in an abstract 'operator space,' decomposing the resulting geometry using conditioning subspaces to separate reducible and irreducible components.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to general quantum many-body systems, analyzing families of higher-order out-of-time-ordered correlators (OTOCs) relevant for studying dynamics in condensed matter contexts like spin chains or lattice models.

**Key observables.** Correlator families, irreducible volume profiles ($\pi_q$), Gram matrix determinant ($\det G(\Omega)$), and the residual geometry $Q(W)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The conditioning rank ($r$) used to define the resolved sector; system size ($L$); and comparison between different dynamical regimes (e.g., free-fermion vs. chaotic).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that correlator families can be systematically organized into a geometric structure amenable to subspace decomposition via conditioning.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the construction of correlator families as inner products and demonstrate the decomposition of the geometry into reducible (explained) and irreducible (residual) components.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by first defining the geometric representation using the operator Gram matrix, then introducing various conditioning schemes (canonical, targeted, Krylov/cross), and finally applying this framework to diagnose differences between known quantum dynamics regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Programmable quantum simulators are beginning to access correlators of increasing complexity, ranging from four-point out-of-time-ordered correlators to even higher-order many-body correlators. The theoretical framework for interpreting such data, however, remains comparatively underdeveloped. Although a variety of higher-order correlators can be constructed straightforwardly, their physical meaning is often difficult to infer. A further complication is that different correlators are generally not independent: some may be mutually redundant, while others may encode genuinely distinct information. These features make it necessary to analyze correlators not as isolated quantities, but as a structured family. In this work, we develop a geometric framework for the collective analysis of higher-order correlator families. By representing correlators as inner products between operator words, we recast each family as a geometry in operator space. The key idea is to introduce conditioning subspaces that separate this geometry into reducible information, already explained by a chosen resolved sector, and irreducible information, encoded in the residual correlator geometry. Focusing on the latter component, we define irreducible volume profiles that quantify how broadly the unexplained part of a correlator family spreads over independent geometric directions. This perspective leads to several complementary forms of conditioning. Canonical conditioning optimally explains a correlator family. Targeted conditioning fixes the resolved sector to isolate a chosen physical feature. Krylov and cross conditioning extend the framework from a single correlator family to comparisons among correlator geometries. Our framework reveals irreducible structures hidden at the level of individual correlator values and establishes correlator geometry as a higher-level description of quantum many-body dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates an entanglement-preserving method to convert photonic qubits from polarization encoding to the more stable time-bin basis using trapped ${}^{88}	ext{Sr}^+$ ions. By implementing this conversion, the authors show that the resulting entangled state fidelity remains high and is robust against significant depolarizing noise, which is crucial for practical quantum communication networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical bottleneck in quantum networking—maintaining coherence over long distances where fiber polarization drift is common. The successful demonstration of robust entanglement transfer via time-bin encoding is highly relevant to building scalable quantum repeaters and networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming polarization instability inherent in quantum communication links when distributing entanglement between matter qubits (trapped ions) and photons.

**Main result.** The implementation of a polarization-to-time-bin conversion successfully preserves entanglement fidelity, proving robust against depolarizing noise even at full depolarization strength.

**Method.** The authors use an asymmetric Mach-Zehnder interferometer (MZI) as an encoder to convert the initial polarization encoding into a time-bin basis for the photonic qubit.

**Model / system.** The system involves trapped ${}^{88}	ext{Sr}^+$ ions acting as matter qubits, emitting photons at $1092 	ext{ nm}$. The process requires two interferometers (encoder and decoder) to map polarization states ($|Hangle, |Vangle$) onto time-bins ($|Eangle, |Langle$).

**Key observables.** State fidelity bounds ($\approx 0.91 - 0.93$), conversion error ($< 0.028$), and the robustness of fidelity against depolarizing noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time-bin separation (60 ns), ion transition wavelength (1092 nm), and depolarization strength parameter ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that background counts are negligible for post-selected fidelity, and the phase error estimation is a conservative upper bound due to shared common-mode drift.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the MZI encoder/decoder setups (Fig. 1), show population measurements and coherence fringes for time-bin encoding (Fig. 2), illustrate the phase stabilization scheme (Fig. 4, Fig. 5), and compare fidelity vs. noise strength for both encoding methods (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for robust entanglement distribution, detailing the physical implementation of the polarization-to-time-bin conversion using MZIs, quantifying the resulting state fidelity and error sources through detailed measurements, and finally demonstrating the superior noise resilience of the time-bin encoding.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Time-bin photonic qubits are well-suited for quantum network applications due to their robustness to polarization instability in fiber links and potential for heterogeneous networks. In this work, we implement the first entanglement-preserving polarization-to-time-bin conversion of a photon qubit in an entangled state with a matter qubit. Photons initially generated with polarization encoding are converted to the time-bin basis through a polarization-discriminating asymmetric Mach-Zehnder interferometer. The photonic qubits are generated via the $1092$ nm transition of a $^{88}$Sr$^{+}$ ion. We measure state fidelity bounds of $0.906 \pm 0.011 \le \mathcal{F} \le 0.934\pm 0.011$, with conversion error $< 0.028$, and find this fidelity is unaffected by depolarizing noise even at full depolarization strength.

</details>

**Summary.** This work quantifies the typicality of quantum steering—a measure of genuine quantum influence—in two-qubit systems subjected to multiple random measurements. By analyzing various state ensembles, the authors show that the probability of observing steering increases significantly with the number of measurements, suggesting that non-classical correlations are robust features of generic mixed states.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous quantitative bounds on how robustly non-classical correlations like steering manifest in realistic random quantum systems, which is crucial for designing entanglement-based quantum protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the 'typicality' of quantum steering—a non-classical correlation effect—for generic two-qubit states subjected to random projective measurements.

**Main result.** The probability of observing steering ($P_S$) increases systematically with the number of measurements ($m$), approaching genuine typicality ($P_S=100\%$) asymptotically for many random state ensembles.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical derivations, analytical expressions (for specific states like Werner states), and extensive numerical simulations employing Semi-Definite Programming (SDP) to calculate the steering probability integral over measurement settings.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-qubit state ($ho_{AB}$), analyzed in terms of its entanglement properties. The study focuses on mixed-state ensembles generated via partial tracing from larger random states, contrasting this with pure Haar-random states.

**Key observables.** Steering probability ($P_S$), which quantifies the likelihood that observed conditional quantum states cannot be explained by a Local Hidden State (LHS) model.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of measurements ($m$), visibility parameter ($v$) for Werner states, and the structure/ensemble type of the initial two-qubit state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between finite $m$ detection and the asymptotic limit ($m 	o \infty$) required to claim typicality; specific calculations rely on assumptions about measurement settings (e.g., uniform choice on the Bloch sphere).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the systematic growth of $P^S$ versus $m$ for different state ensembles (like Bell-diagonal and depolarized states), showing how steering becomes more probable with more measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining quantum steering and its typicality, deriving analytical results for simple cases (Werner states), expanding to general mixed-state ensembles using numerical methods, and finally comparing the findings across various state families.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phenomena that slip beyond the grasp of our classical intuition reveal uniquely quantum effects that deepen our understanding of the physical world and enable advances in information processing, particularly in quantum communication and computation. One such phenomenon is quantum steering, whereby measurements performed by one party influence the conditional states of another when the two share an entangled quantum system. If the observed correlations cannot be explained by a local hidden state model, the state is said to be steerable. In this work, we investigate the typicality of this behavior: given a generic two-qubit state and $m$ Haar-random projective measurements, what is the probability of observing steering? We derive analytical expressions for the steering probability $\mathcal{P}_S$ of Werner states in two- and three-setting scenarios, the latter restricted to coplanar projective measurements on the Bloch sphere. For larger numbers of settings and various random states ensembles, we perform numerical analyses showing that $\mathcal{P}_S$ increases systematically with the number of measurements and substantially exceeds the corresponding probabilities associated with Bell nonlocality. Our results demonstrate that random states with minimal environmental coupling exhibit a high probability of steering for finite $m$ and approach genuine typicality, $\mathcal{P}_S=100\%$, as the number of settings increases. We provide a detailed characterization of $\mathcal{P}_S$ across different state ensembles and specific families, including Bell-diagonal and Werner states, identifying those with the greatest non-classical potential and highlighting their relevance for protocols in which steering serves as a key resource.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers investigated the nature of a phase change in moiré rhombohedral graphene between two topological states: an FCI and an anomalous Hall crystal. By using bulk-sensitive quasiparticle charging measurements, they definitively ruled out edge effects and established that the transition is a genuine thermodynamic phase transition occurring at very low temperatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides experimental confirmation of competing correlated ground states in moiré systems, which is highly relevant for understanding emergent topological order and fractionalized excitations in strongly interacting electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to resolve whether the observed transition from a Fractional Chern Insulator (FCI) to an extended quantum anomalous Hall background in moiré rhombohedral graphene is due to edge state equilibration failure or a genuine bulk phase transition.

**Main result.** Quasiparticle charging measurements and temperature-dependent bulk resistance strongly suggest that the system undergoes a thermodynamic phase transition from an FCI to a generalized anomalous Hall crystal at low temperatures, ruling out the edge state scenario.

**Method.** The research employs mesoscopic transport measurements, including quasiparticle tunneling spectroscopy (measuring diagonal resistance) and bulk longitudinal/Hall resistance mapping across varying filling factors and temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is moiré rhombohedral graphene (mRG), fabricated into a mesoscopic antidot device structure. The physics explores competing topological ground states, specifically FCI versus generalized anomalous Hall crystal phases.

**Key observables.** Quasiparticle charge carried during tunneling ($e^*$), quantized resistance plateaus ($R_{xx}, R_{xy}$), and the temperature dependence of bulk resistance near critical filling factors ($
u=2/3$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (down to 12 mK) are crucial for observing clear signatures; the transition occurs below $T \approx 150$ mK.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of bulk-sensitive antidot measurements is necessary to overcome ambiguities inherent in edge-state dominated Hall bar measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate device schematics, resonant tunneling peaks (Coulomb oscillations) at specific fillings ($
u=1, 2/3$), and phase diagrams showing the competition between FCI and GAHC states as temperature changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents evidence from quasiparticle charging measurements to establish charge quantization, followed by bulk resistance mapping that reveals a temperature-dependent transition between two distinct topological phases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré materials realize a versatile platform for exploring the physics of fractional Chern insulators (FCIs). The recently observed evolution from FCIs to an extended quantum anomalous Hall background upon lowering the electronic temperature in moiré rhombohedral graphene (mRG)8 raises a fundamental question: Is it caused by a failure to equilibrate the edge states of an FCI or by a genuine phase transition in the bulk from an FCI to a generalized anomalous Hall crystal? Here we address this question by probing quasiparticle charging in a mesoscopic mRG antidot device and by bulk resistance measurements, both of which are bulk-sensitive and free from complications from edge states. Tunneling to the mRG antidot reveals quasiparticles carrying one electron charge for both Chern states at filling factors ν=1 and 2/3 at low temperatures. Temperature dependence measurements of the bulk resistance near ν=2/3 further suggest a thermodynamic phase transition from an FCI to a generalized anomalous Hall crystal at temperatures below about 150mK. The results clearly exclude the edge state equilibration scenario and favor the phase transition scenario. Our work establishes mesoscopic probes as a powerful approach to uncover competing ground states in moiré materials and provides a basis for probing fractionalized excitations in FCIs.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate Measurement-Based Quantum Computing using four-photon graph states generated on an integrated silicon photonic chip. They successfully implemented fundamental universal gates and tested algorithms like Grover's search, achieving high fidelities that confirm the feasibility of scaling this technology for future quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a concrete experimental realization of fault-tolerant quantum computation principles (MBQC) on a scalable solid-state platform, advancing the field of photonic quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to demonstrate Measurement-Based Quantum Computing (MBQC) on a scalable photonic platform, circumventing the need for deterministic photon-photon interactions.

**Main result.** The experiment successfully demonstrated reconfigurable four-photon MBQC using integrated silicon photonics, achieving high fidelities for graph state generation and implementing universal quantum gates like T, CZ, and CNOT.

**Method.** MBQC is implemented by performing adaptive measurements on highly entangled photonic graph states generated on an integrated chip. Quantum algorithms (Grover's, Deutsch-Jozsa) were also demonstrated using these resource states.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an integrated silicon photonic chip utilizing dual-rail encoding across eight modes to encode four photonic qubits. The computation relies on generating and measuring specific entangled graph states ($	ext{|Star4}angle$, $	ext{|Lin4}angle$).

**Key observables.** Fidelities for graph states ($F_{	ext{Star}} \approx 83.5\%$, $F_{	ext{Lin}} \approx 75.6\%$), gate fidelities ($F_{	ext{CNOT}} \approx 90.3\%$), and violation of Bell inequalities ($	ext{|}\langle B_{II} angle	ext{|}_{	ext{Star}} = (3.21 \pm 0.09)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system operates with four photonic qubits, utilizing integrated silicon photonics components like thermo-optic phase shifters and grating couplers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work establishes feasibility for four-photon MBQC; limitations include noise sources from losses, non-perfect indistinguishability, and the need for deterministic single-photon sources for scaling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup (SPDC sources coupled to the chip), confirm entanglement via stabilizer measurements and Bell inequality violation, and detail the implementation of quantum gates (T, CZ, CNOT) using MBQC principles.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical platform diagnostics (waveguide loss, coupling efficiency), characterizes the single-photon source quality (HOM visibility), demonstrates graph state generation and entanglement verification via Bell tests, and finally implements fundamental universal quantum gates and algorithms using these states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Integrated photonics provides a scalable platform for quantum information processing. In this context, measurement-based quantum computing (MBQC) offers an attractive approach in which quantum computation is realised by adaptive measurements on highly entangled graph states, circumventing the need for deterministic photon-photon interactions. Here, we demonstrate MBQC on an integrated silicon photonic chip capable of generating photonic graph states with up to four qubits. We achieve fidelities of $F_{Star} = (83.5 \pm 1.8)\,\%$ and $F_{Lin} = (75.6 \pm 1.1)\,\%$ for four-photon star and linear graph states, respectively. We use these resource states to implement MBQC-based single- and two-qubit gates and to demonstrate Grover's search algorithm and the Deutsch-Jozsa algorithm. These results establish the feasibility of reconfigurable four-photon MBQC on an integrated photonic platform and provide a foundation for future larger-scale implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper extends concepts from finite-dimensional quantum information theory—specifically smoothing exponents and decoupling bounds—to the general setting of semifinite von Neumann algebras. By developing new operator-algebraic tools, the authors establish exact formulas showing that these fundamental physical limits depend only on the underlying algebraic structure, not on artificial dimension truncations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to analyze quantum resource theories (like entanglement or coherence) in infinite-dimensional settings relevant to continuous variable systems or large quantum memories, which is highly relevant for advanced quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the smoothing exponent of the max-relative entropy and develops a framework for catalytic quantum information decoupling within the mathematical setting of semifinite von Neumann algebras.

**Main result.** They derive exact exponent formulas showing that these quantities are governed by the underlying algebraic structure rather than finite matrix dimension estimates, generalizing known results to infinite dimensions.

**Method.** The methodology involves developing operator-algebraic replacements for finite-dimensional tools and utilizing noncommutative L^p-spaces associated with semifinite von Neumann algebras.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework is built upon general quantum systems described by semifinite von Neumann algebras (VNA), extending beyond simple matrix models. Concepts from quantum information theory, such as Rényi relative entropies and divergences, are central to the analysis.

**Key observables.** Smoothing exponent, max-relative entropy ($D_\infty$), decoupling reliability exponent, and sandwiched Rényi mutual information.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameter $s$ (related to the order of the divergence) is crucial for optimization; limits as dimension/index go to infinity are key.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary technical challenge overcome is moving from finite-dimensional arguments to the general semifinite setting, requiring intrinsic algebraic tools.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing foundational $L^p$-space definitions on VNA, defining generalized information divergences, proving key continuity lemmas (like for Legendre transforms), and finally applying these results to derive the exponent formulas for decoupling and smoothing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the smoothing exponent of the max-relative entropy in semifinite von Neumann algebras. Our main result gives an exact exponent formula in this setting. The proof develops operator-algebraic replacements for the dimension-dependent tools used in finite-dimensional arguments. These ingredients show that the smoothing exponent is governed by the underlying von Neumann algebraic structure rather than by matrix dimension estimates. As an application, we formulate catalytic quantum information decoupling with a semifinite von Neumann algebraic reference system. We prove an intrinsic layer-cake lemma for von Neumann algebras, which removes the countable spectrum assumption in the finite-dimensional proof and yields the corresponding semifinite estimate. Consequently, the decoupling reliability exponent is described by the same sandwiched Rényi mutual information formula as in the finite-dimensional theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the center-of-mass dynamics in interacting 1D many-body quantum systems. It derives a sharp upper bound showing that the ground-state fluctuation of the center-of-mass is limited by the system's active spectral gap. Crucially, it proves that this maximum rigidity (saturation) is achieved if and only if the external trapping potentials are harmonic, establishing harmonic confinement as the unique static extremizer.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful spectral tools—relating ground-state variances to energy gaps—that are highly relevant in AMO physics for understanding how trapping potentials dictate collective excitations and rigidity in systems like trapped atoms or quantum chains.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper extends the harmonic-rigidity mechanism, traditionally known for one-body systems, to analyze the center-of-mass observable in interacting many-body quantum systems.

**Main result.** It establishes a sharp upper bound on the ground-state center-of-mass fluctuation ($	ext{Var}_0(X)$) proportional to the active spectral gap ($\Delta X$), showing that equality holds if and only if the external trapping potentials are harmonic with a common frequency (under positivity assumptions).

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on commutator identities involving the Hamiltonian ($H$) and the center-of-mass operator ($X$), leading to rigorous bounds derived from spectral properties.

**Model / system.** The system consists of one-dimensional many-body quantum particles with masses $m_i$ interacting via translation-invariant potentials, subject to external trapping potentials $V_i(x_i)$. The Hamiltonian is explicitly defined in terms of kinetic and potential energy terms.

**Key observables.** Center-of-mass fluctuation ($	ext{Var}_0(X)$), Active Spectral Gap ($\Delta X$), Structural Defect ($G$), Variance Deficit ($D_X$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $\hbar^2/(2M)$ linking the variance bound to the gap, and the common frequency $\Omega$ characterizing harmonic confinement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary results hold generally without assuming ground state positivity ($\psi_0 > 0$), but the complete characterization of the equality case requires this positivity assumption.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing fundamental commutator identities for $X$ and $H$, deriving a general sharp bound on $	ext{Var}_0(X)$, and then characterizing the necessary and sufficient conditions (especially under positivity) for this bound to be saturated, linking saturation directly to harmonic confinement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the center-of-mass observable in one-dimensional many-body systems with translation-invariant interactions and extend the harmonic-rigidity mechanism from the one-body setting to an interacting many-body problem. We prove a sharp upper bound on the ground-state center-of-mass fluctuation in terms of the active spectral gap associated with the center-of-mass probe, and show that this bound does not require any positivity assumption on the ground state. In the positivity class, we characterize the equality case completely. Exact saturation occurs if and only if the external one-body traps are harmonic with a common frequency, while the interaction may remain arbitrary within the translation-invariant class. We also identify a natural rigidity defect measuring deviation from the harmonic extremal situation and prove quantitative near-saturation estimates controlling both the variance deficit and the spectral weight outside the first active shell. In this way, the paper establishes harmonic confinement as the unique static extremizer for the rigid interacting center-of-mass mode at fixed active gap.

</details>

### [Distributed Monogamy of Entanglement limits Quantum Channel Simulation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08591v1)

**Authors:** Rabsan Galib Ahmed, Graeme Smith  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08591v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

In the asymptotically many uses of C (n →∞), we require a vanishingly small error.</figcaption>

<img src="2607.08591_figures/2607.08591_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. A (3, 2)-extension, σAB[5]. Any two Bi’s can be jointly processed to output the (3, 2)-extendible state, ρAB.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced entanglement measures, such as fractional extendibility, and applies them alongside the concept of distributed monogamy of entanglement. Using these tools, the authors establish strict impossibility theorems showing that certain noisy quantum channels cannot simulate others if their noise parameters fall within specific ranges.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (fractional extendibility) to quantify resource limitations in quantum communication protocols, which is central to understanding achievable rates and bounds in quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates fundamental limits on quantum channel simulation, specifically determining when one noisy channel cannot be simulated by another.

**Main result.** They prove that a quantum erasure channel $N_\gamma$ with noise parameter $\gamma$ cannot simulate an erasure channel $N_\lambda$ if $\gamma < \lambda \le 1/2$, establishing quantitative bounds on simulation feasibility.

**Method.** The analysis relies on introducing and utilizing 'fractional extendibility' as a refined measure of quantum correlation, alongside the established 'distributed monogamy of entanglement'.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the quantum erasure channel $N_\lambda$, which transmits input perfectly with probability $\lambda$ and erases it otherwise. The analysis uses concepts from multi-partite entanglement on tensor product states.

**Key observables.** Fractional extendibility, Quantum Capacity ($Q(N_\lambda)$), and the average probability of extracting an EPR pair from random subsets of systems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The erasure channel parameter $\lambda$ (and related noise parameters $\gamma$), and the ratio $k/n$ derived from distributed monogamy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof for irrational parameters assumes that if simulation were possible, it would contradict results proven for rational numbers; also relies on vanishing trace distance implying entanglement constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the concept of simulating one quantum channel using another via the trace distance between Choi states. Figure 2 depicts a specific $(3, 2)$-extension state $\sigma_{AB}[5]$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining fractional extendibility and proving its invariance properties; it then establishes the quantitative bound of distributed monogamy of entanglement; finally, these tools are combined to prove the impossibility of simulating noisier channels with less noisy ones.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement is monogamous: if it is shared among more than two parties, the entanglement between any pair cannot be very strong. For an integer $k\geq 2$, $k$-extendibility of a state $ρ_{AB}$ quantifies this as the number of copies of $B$ that can be simulated by the state's environment. We introduce fractional extendibility, which gives a finer characterization of the quantum correlation that is leaked to the environment, and prove that it is invariant under tensor products and monotonic under local processing. We also establish the distributed monogamy of entanglement: for any state on $AB_1B_2\dots B_n$, the maximum average probability of extracting an EPR pair from a random subset of $k \leq n/2$ systems among the $B_i$'s is the fraction $k/n$. With these tools we show that any quantum erasure channel with erasure probability more than a half cannot simulate a less noisy erasure channel, even with asymptotically many uses of the more noisy channel.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates a functional metropolitan quantum network link by distributing entanglement between an atom and a photon over $24 	ext{ km}$ of standard optical fiber. This was achieved by employing specialized quantum frequency converters to shift the photon's wavelength between the atomic resonance (visible) and the telecom band. The success proves a crucial step toward integrating local quantum processors with existing long-distance communication infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the critical engineering hurdle—wavelength conversion—necessary to connect atomic quantum memories (which operate at visible wavelengths) with existing, long-haul fiber infrastructure used for quantum communication networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is overcoming the wavelength mismatch between quantum network nodes (operating at visible/near-visible wavelengths) and the telecom band required for long-distance fiber transmission to distribute entanglement.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a metropolitan quantum link capable of distributing atom-photon entanglement over 24 km by implementing bidirectional frequency conversion, maintaining high fidelity ($F \geq 86.9\%$).

**Method.** The experiment utilizes quantum frequency converters (QFCs) based on periodically-poled lithium niobate to convert the photon wavelength between the atomic resonance ($\lambda = 780 	ext{ nm}$) and the telecom S-band ($	ext{1514 nm}$), enabling transmission through deployed fiber.

**Model / system.** The system is a metropolitan quantum link connecting two laboratories via $24 	ext{ km}$ of commercial optical fiber. It involves an atomic qubit (e.g., $	ext{Rb}$) entangled with a photon, which undergoes conversion at the ends of the fiber segment.

**Key observables.** Entanglement fidelity ($F$), visibility ($ar{V}$), Bell parameter ($S$), and overall entanglement distribution efficiency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fiber length ($24 	ext{ km}$), atomic wavelength ($780 	ext{ nm}$), telecom wavelength ($	ext{1514 nm}$), and achieved photon transfer efficiency ($1.7\%$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system relies on the functionality of quantum frequency converters and assumes stable operation despite environmental drifts, which are actively corrected using classical polarization monitoring.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup showing entanglement generation at $780 	ext{ nm}$, conversion to $	ext{1514 nm}$ for fiber propagation, and back-conversion. Results show state correlations confirming entanglement violation of Bell's inequality both before and after full link transmission.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup involving frequency conversion stages ($	ext{QFC}1$ and $	ext{QFC}2$) to bridge wavelength gaps, followed by long-distance propagation through fiber. Key results confirm high fidelity entanglement distribution over $24 	ext{ km}$, concluding with a discussion of future applications in quantum networking.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement distribution is the overarching purpose of quantum networks. While communication over long distances can use deployed fiber infrastructure, it requires photons in the telecom band. However, advanced quantum network nodes do not operate at such wavelengths. Here we overcome this limitation with two tailor-made low-noise quantum-frequency converters to distribute entanglement between a single atom and a resonant photon over 14km line-of-sight via 24km of a deployed commercial fiber. The photon at wavelength 780nm is first entangled with the atom, then converted to the telecom S-band, and finally back-converted after propagation through the fiber. This link enables a photon transfer efficiency of 1.7% while affecting the atom-photon entanglement fidelity by less than 1%. This brings integration of atomic quantum nodes with existing long-distance fiber networks into reach, enabling novel applications in quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details the quantum-geometric design of novel lattice models intended to host generalized Landau levels. By analyzing these structured systems using exact diagonalization and entanglement spectroscopy, the authors predict and confirm the realization of exotic correlated phases, including fractional Chern insulators and anomalous Hall crystal states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a concrete theoretical framework—using engineered lattice Hamiltonians—to model exotic many-body phenomena (like fractional quantum Hall states) that are central to condensed matter theory, bridging geometric design with strong correlation physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research focuses on designing lattice models with tailored quantum geometry to realize physics analogous to Landau Levels (LLs) and higher-Chern bands, while also studying emergent fractionalized and charge-ordered states.

**Main result.** The authors successfully predict and confirm the existence of Abelian Fractional Chern Insulators (FCIs), Moore-Read states, and various anomalous Hall crystal phases in these engineered lattice models using exact diagonalization techniques.

**Method.** The primary methods involve constructing generalized LLs via quantum geometry analysis and then employing Exact Diagonalization (ED) coupled with Particle Entanglement Spectrum (PES) analysis to study interacting electron physics on the resulting flat bands.

**Model / system.** The models are lattice generalizations of Landau levels, constructed using $N=2, 3,$ and $4$ sublattices on lattices like honeycomb or kagome. The Hamiltonian is studied by projecting interactions onto selected bands derived from these engineered quantum geometries.

**Key observables.** Key observables include the Chern number ($C$), the quantum weight ($W$), the many-body average Chern number ($C_{	ext{avg}}$), and the analysis of entanglement gaps ($\xi_{AHC}$) to diagnose topological phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Important parameters include filling fractions $
u$ (e.g., $1/3, 1/2, 1/5$), the number of sublattices ($N=2, 3, 4$), and hopping decay rates (Gaussian or exponential).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumptions include the linear independence of basis states used in construction and that quantum geometry can be tailored to enforce specific topological properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures for different $N$ values, ED spectra showing quasi-degenerate ground states at fractional fillings (e.g., $
u=1/2$), and PES plots confirming gaps associated with charge ordering.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from designing the lattice models based on quantum geometry to analyzing the resulting flat bands via ED for various filling fractions, culminating in the identification of specific correlated topological phases like FCIs and AHCs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We design lattice models with tailored quantum geometry, including generalized Landau levels (LLs) satisfying the integrated trace condition and higher-Chern bands with ideal quantum geometry. Our models with $N=2$, $3$, and $4$ sublattices include a generalized Haldane model ($N=2$ honeycomb lattice model) with Gaussian-decaying hoppings realizable in twisted bilayer MoTe$_2$, and $N \geq 3$ models with exponentially decaying hoppings. Exact diagonalization reveals fractional Chern insulators in the generalized zeroth LL bands of all three models, a Moore-Read state in the generalized first LL band of the $N=4$ model, and various interaction-driven topological phases$\unicode{x2013}$including integer and fractional anomalous Hall crystals and a multicomponent Halperin state$\unicode{x2013}$in the ideal higher-Chern band of the $N=3$ model. Informed by quantum geometry, our work provides a pathway for lattice realizations of Landau-level and beyond-Landau-level physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes Entanglement Wedge Reconstruction within AdS/CFT, arguing that the strong interpretation requiring Holographic Quantum Error Correction (HQEC) is incompatible with ordinary local QFTs. By using locality arguments based on low-energy observables, the authors conclude that only a weaker, region-specific reconstruction (subregion complementarity) remains valid.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly theoretical and related to quantum gravity, the core argument about local observables detecting differences in structure (the failure of global consistency) echoes themes relevant to understanding entanglement structure in complex many-body systems or open quantum systems where locality constraints are paramount.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper distinguishes between two interpretations of Entanglement Wedge Reconstruction (EWR) in AdS/CFT: the strong Holographic Quantum Error Correction (HQEC) interpretation versus a weaker, region-by-region reconstruction statement.

**Main result.** The authors argue that for ordinary local CFTs, the strong HQEC structure fails due to locality arguments involving low-energy observables. What remains is EWR without HQEC, where each boundary region has its own independent bulk interpretation.

**Method.** The analysis uses simple locality arguments and compares predictions from toy models (like HaPPY codes) against the expected behavior of local QFT observables, such as smeared stress tensors.

**Model / system.** The framework is AdS/CFT correspondence. The physics involves reconstructing bulk operators in Anti-de Sitter space from boundary Conformal Field Theory (CFT) algebras at low energies ($	ext{H}_{	ext{low}}$).

**Key observables.** Smeared stress tensors, local observables, and the structure of overlapping subalgebras $R(A) \cap R(B)$ vs. $R(A \cap B)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The large N limit ($N=\infty$) versus finite N corrections (interaction order $g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The argument relies on the assumption that low-energy observables (like smeared stress tensors) are capable of detecting physical support and gravitational dressing, which is violated by HQEC codes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically contrasts the strong HQEC claim with the weaker algebraic statement. It uses locality arguments to show that ordinary QFTs cannot support a common logical operator across multiple regions, leading to the conclusion of region-specific reconstruction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bulk reconstruction is a central problem in AdS/CFT, and entanglement wedge reconstruction is its subregion version. We argue that this subregion statement should be separated from the stronger holographic quantum error correction interpretation, in which one region-independent logical bulk operator has code-preserving representatives in several boundary regions. A simple locality argument shows that such a common reconstruction must commute with the code-preserving local algebras in the complementary regions. This is the mechanism realized in HaPPY-type codes: the erased regions are blind to a protected logical algebra. An ordinary finite $N$ holographic CFT does not have such a protected invisible sector for supergravity fields. Its low-energy local observables, in particular, suitably smeared stress tensors, detect the physical support and gravitational dressing of ordinary bulk operators, up to possible center or superselection data. Thus, there is no such holographic quantum error correction and the $N=\infty$ agreement of global and subregion HKLL formulae is a free-theory statement. What remains is entanglement wedge reconstruction without holographic quantum error correction, or subregion complementarity: each boundary region has its own code-preserving low-energy algebra and its own region-adapted bulk interpretation, rather than a shared logical operator.

</details>
