**Summary.** This paper introduces the GroverFigureOfMerit (FoM), an advanced metric designed to holistically characterize and compare performance across diverse Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware platforms. By using Grover's algorithm as a stress test, it quantifies performance by accounting for dynamic noise, transpilation overhead, and topological limitations in a single, unified score.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial benchmarking tool for assessing algorithmic feasibility on noisy hardware, which is vital for theorists designing complex quantum circuits that must account for physical constraints beyond simple gate fidelity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The NISQ era suffers from hardware heterogeneity, where proprietary metrics and static characterizations fail to provide a unified way to select or compare quantum backends.

**Main result.** A novel Figure of Merit (GroverFigureOfMerit) based on Grover's algorithm provides a standardized, comparable score that captures dynamic noise, transpilation overhead, and topological constraints across different hardware architectures.

**Method.** The authors propose an algorithmic stress test using Grover's search, defining the FoM as a combination of success probability penalized by target state imbalance and leakage to non-marked states.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes various NISQ quantum backends (e.g., IBM, IonQ) across different physical architectures, utilizing the Qonscious framework for agnostic execution flow control.

**Key observables.** GroverFigureOfMerit score, probability of success on target states, non-uniform amplification penalties, and leakage to non-marked states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Search space size (N), number of qubits (n), coherence times ($T_1/T_2$), and gate error rates are compared against the dynamic FoM score.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that an algorithmic stress test can provide a unified, hardware-agnostic measure of performance superior to static component metrics. Validation on physical QPUs is noted as a future step.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Grover Scores across different backends and scaling parameters (N), demonstrating score collapse due to decoherence or topological overhead on certain processors.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of static characterization metrics in NISQ hardware. It then proposes the GroverFigureOfMerit, details its mathematical formulation incorporating penalties, and validates it by systematically analyzing performance across multiple simulated and real-world quantum providers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era poses a challenge for developers: hardware providers expose capabilities through heterogeneous interfaces with proprietary metrics varying widely across providers, hindering informed backend selection. Static characterization metrics - coherence times T1/T2, gate error rates - exhibit limitations: they fail to capture dynamic variability across successive executions, overlook the impact of transpilation, and lack architectural comparability across physically distinct technologies. We propose a Figure of Merit (FoM) based on Grover's algorithm as an algorithmic stress test evaluating quantum backend performance holistically. The metric combines success probability on target states with penalties for non-uniform amplification and leakage to non-marked states, yielding a unified score across hardware architectures. Implemented via the Qonscious framework - a conditional execution platform using polymorphic adapters, it executes agnostically on IBM, IonQ backends, and simulators. Main contributions: (1) proposal and validation of GroverFigureOfMerit, incorporating uniformity and leakage penalties (adapted from GRADE) with emphasis on noise, transpilation, and topological constraints; (2) systematic analysis of heterogeneity across nine quantum providers motivating agnostic metrics; and (3) experimental demonstration via ideal simulators and real-processor noise models, confirming sensitivity to noise, topology, and transpilation overhead. Results confirm the metric distinguishes backend performance under a unified score, capturing intrinsic algorithmic limits. Validation on physical QPUs is identified as a natural next step.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel diagnostic tool by reformulating the calculation of 'non-local magic' using the Walsh–Hadamard autocorrelations of the entanglement spectrum. This representation makes the underlying harmonic structure explicit, allowing for systematic theoretical analysis. The results characterize how this non-local resource scales in different quantum regimes, linking it fundamentally to the organization of entanglement eigenvalues.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful new spectroscopic tool—the entanglement spectrum—to diagnose complex quantum resources like non-local magic, linking it directly to spectral structure rather than just global measures like entropy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a tractable method to characterize 'non-local magic,' a resource quantifying genuinely non-local correlations, as its direct calculation is computationally intractable.

**Main result.** Non-local magic can be systematically represented using the Walsh–Hadamard autocorrelations of the entanglement spectrum. The scaling behavior of this resource is shown to depend critically on the spectral organization of the entanglement eigenvalues.

**Method.** The authors derive an equivalent representation for non-local magic involving fourth moments of autocorrelation functions derived from the entanglement spectrum, allowing for systematic analytical study.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to pure quantum states $|\psiangle$ partitioned into two subsystems (A and B), examining various theoretical limits such as Haar-random states, product-structured modes, gapped 1D systems, and critical free-fermion models.

**Key observables.** Non-local magic ($M_{	ext{Sch}}^2$), the entanglement spectrum eigenvalues ($\lambda_x$), and the second-order Rényi entanglement entropy ($S_2(ho_A)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (L), spectral organization, and the nature of correlations (e.g., area law vs. logarithmic scaling regimes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping Schmidt vectors to $\mathbb{F}_2$ and uses approximations for random states; a key assumption is that spectral organization governs behavior more strongly than entropy alone.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical framework by relating non-local magic to Walsh autocorrelations, then applies this framework to analyze scaling laws across different physical regimes (Haar random, gapped, critical) while establishing an upper bound using entanglement entropy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-local magic has recently emerged as a fundamental resource for characterizing genuinely non-local non-stabilizer correlations. However, its direct calculation is an intractable numerical problem, except for small systems, and its understanding remains limited. We derive a representation of non-local magic in terms of the Walsh--Hadamard autocorrelations of the entanglement spectrum. Our representation makes the underlying harmonic structure explicit and enables a systematic analysis of its behaviors for various scenarios. We prove that non-local magic can be upper-bounded by an entanglement entropy and we derive exact analytical results for broad classes of quantum states, characterizing the scaling of non-local magic for volume-law states, as well as ground states of one-dimensional gapped and critical systems. Our results identify the spectral organization of the entanglement spectrum as the key ingredient governing non-local magic and provide a framework for further systematic analytical investigation.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates the observation of a coherent flux-charge interaction within a gate-tunable fluxonium qubit. By exploiting parity selection rules in two-tone spectroscopy, the authors successfully isolate this coupling from standard capacitive interactions. This breakthrough provides superconducting circuits with a vital missing primitive for advanced quantum control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a fundamental limitation in superconducting circuit design by engineering a previously inaccessible coupling term ($\hat{U}_{FC}$), which is crucial for building more complex quantum gates and readouts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is demonstrating a direct flux-charge coupling interaction ($\hat{U}_{FC}$) in superconducting circuits, as native couplings are typically restricted to charge-to-charge or flux-to-flux interactions.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated this unconventional flux-charge coupling using a gate-tunable Josephson junction, enabling coherent control that is robust and scales linearly with the driving amplitude.

**Method.** They employed two-tone spectroscopy measurements combined with parity selection rules to isolate and confirm the presence of the desired $\hat{U}_{FC}$ term from parasitic capacitive couplings.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid superconducting-semiconductor fluxonium qubit. The Hamiltonian includes terms for capacitance, Josephson energy (tunable by gate voltage), and external flux bias. The coupling mechanism relies on modulating the junction's critical current via an applied gate voltage.

**Key observables.** Cross-quadrature coupling between flux ($\hat{\Phi}$) and charge ($\hat{Q}$); detection of $|0angle 	o |2angle$ transitions using two-tone spectroscopy as a function of external flux and gate voltage.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling scales linearly with driving amplitude; the method identifies a 'Charge-Insensitive' sweet spot that protects the transition energy from first-order variations in gate voltage.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis critically relies on exploiting parity selection rules to distinguish between different types of couplings, and simulations neglect relaxation/dephasing during pulse evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup showing the tunable junction; spectroscopy results compare forbidden transitions (capacitive only) versus detectable transitions (flux-charge coupling active) at half-flux quantum.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the theoretical necessity of $\hat{U}_{FC}$ to detailing the experimental platform and measurement techniques. It uses spectroscopic measurements comparing different driving mechanisms to isolate and quantify the unique flux-charge interaction, concluding with robustness analysis (ChIVE point).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interactions that mix conjugate variables, such as the flux through a circuit element and the charge across it, lie outside the reach of the elementary couplings of superconducting circuits. Capacitors connect charge to charge, and inductors connect flux to flux, while no two-terminal element couples flux to charge directly. A native flux-charge coupling would thus serve as a circuit primitive in its own right, opening direct routes to non-reciprocity, protected modes, and unconventional readout. In this work, we demonstrate a flux-charge coupling by harnessing a voltage-tunable Josephson junction with parametrically modulated critical current, which mediates the interaction between a classical charge variable and a quantum flux operator. Relying on parity-selection rules in a hybrid superconducting-semiconductor fluxonium, we isolate the flux-charge coupling from other parasitic capacitive contributions and perform cross-quadrature-activated coherent control of states. Critically, we realize a flux-charge coupling that scales linearly with driving amplitude while keeping the transition energy first-order-insensitive to gate voltage. Such unconventional interaction broadens the toolbox of superconducting circuits with a critical missing component that enables the coherent coupling of conjugate variables.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Möbius-guided compiler designed to efficiently compile diagonal phase gates for neutral-atom quantum processors. It uses subset-lattice Möbius inversion to represent these complex phases as a 'phase hypergraph,' preserving high-order structure. This allows the scheduler to optimally choose between executing native, hardware-efficient multiqubit controlled-phase operations or resorting to standard gate decompositions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical bridge between abstract quantum circuit requirements (diagonal phases) and the specific physical capabilities of emerging hardware platforms like neutral atoms, which is crucial for advancing fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard quantum compilers fail to exploit native, high-order multiqubit controlled-phase gates available on neutral-atom hardware because they prematurely decompose diagonal gate structures.

**Main result.** A Möbius-guided compiler was developed that maps the phase function to a phase hypergraph, achieving improved estimated success and stage reduction for algorithms with exploitable three- or four-body phase terms.

**Method.** The method uses subset-lattice Möbius inversion to transform an arbitrary diagonal phase function into its irreducible projector-phase components, forming a phase hypergraph representation.

**Model / system.** The work is situated on neutral-atom processors utilizing Rydberg-mediated interactions. The core primitive is the occupation-projector phase $	ext{PS}(	heta) = \exp\left(i	heta \sum_{j\in S} n_jight)$.

**Key observables.** Estimated no-fault success, scheduled-stage count, and gate counts compared across compilation strategies (Möbius native vs. ZAP/ZX baselines).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The support set $S$ of the phase term, the angle $	heta$, and hardware constraints like atom motion and blockade feasibility.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The compiler assumes that if a high-order phase is mathematically recoverable via Möbius inversion, it can be physically routed as a native Rydberg-mediated controlled-phase gate when feasible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic basis for Rydberg interactions ($V_{ij} \propto C_6/R_{ij}^6$), the physical layout showing support sets $S$ on neutral atoms, and the mathematical structure of the Möbius inverse transform in a degree-graded ordering.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting compiler limitations, introduces the Möbius-guided compilation framework using subset-lattice inversion to generate a phase hypergraph, details how this maps onto native Rydberg gates, and validates the approach via benchmarks showing improved scheduling metrics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Diagonal gates are ubiquitous primitives in quantum algorithms, from phase oracles, hypergraph-state preparation, and multi-control logic to Hamiltonian simulation of spin models and digitized lattice field theories, where Ising interactions and local potential terms are diagonal in the encoded basis. Standard compilers, however, often lower diagonal structure into one- and two-qubit gates before neutral-atom hardware can exploit native Rydberg-mediated multiqubit controlled-phase operations. We propose a Möbius-guided compiler that maps a diagonal phase function to a phase hypergraph via subset-lattice Möbius inversion. The hypergraph retains the support and angle of each many-body phase term, allowing sparse or local high-order structure to be routed as native multiqubit controlled-phase candidates when feasible and decomposed otherwise. The neutral-atom scheduler accounts for atom motion, interaction-zone constraints, blockade feasibility, and error costs, enabling a direct comparison between native high-order execution and decomposed alternatives. Benchmarks against routed ZAP and ZX-calculus baselines show improved estimated success for algorithmic instances with exploitable three- and four-body phase terms, and comparable performance on predominantly two-body instances. These results provide a feasible compilation strategy for more fully exploiting the native capabilities of neutral-atom hardware, using atom reconfigurability and Rydberg-mediated multiqubit phase operations as practical resources for more efficient quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper uses neural networks to learn an overcomplete dictionary representation for highly complex, chaotic quantum eigenstates. By constraining this learning process with phase-space localization, the model forces its learned building blocks (atoms) to spontaneously organize around structures corresponding to classical periodic orbits. This shows that simple geometric constraints can unlock deep semiclassical physics from purely spectral data.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates a powerful data-driven method for extracting fundamental physical symmetries (like those dictated by classical periodic orbits) from raw quantum spectral data without explicit knowledge of the underlying Hamiltonian dynamics or symmetry operators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether unsupervised learning can reveal the underlying semiclassical structure of quantum chaotic eigenstates.

**Main result.** By imposing phase-space localization constraints on a neural network dictionary, the learned atoms spontaneously localize onto periodic orbits and develop scar-like structures, confirming their fundamental role in chaos.

**Method.** The authors train an overcomplete autoencoder ('quantum dictionary') to sparsely represent eigenstates, comparing its learned structure against independently constructed semiclassical dictionaries based on periodic orbits.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses the quantum baker map as a model for a quantum chaotic system. States are analyzed using phase-space representations like the Husimi density.

**Key observables.** Reconstruction fidelity ($F$), Hilbert-space overlap ($\Omega_{mr}$), and Effective Participation Number ($N_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dictionary size ($M$) relative to the state dimension ($D$), and regularization weights ($\lambda_G, \lambda_{loc}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that phase-space localization constraints are sufficient to recover nontrivial semiclassical organization from spectral data.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare learned atoms' Husimi densities against known scar functions, showing structural alignment and high overlap ($	ext{SH} \approx 0.9$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of characterizing chaotic eigenstates, details the autoencoder setup with localization constraints, presents the semiclassical dictionary construction, and concludes by comparing the learned structures to show emergent periodic orbit organization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Physics-informed neural networks and neural quantum states have consolidated a new paradigm to analyze and discover physical phenomena through constrained neural parametrizations. In this context, we investigate whether the semiclassical structure of the eigenfunctions of a quantum chaotic system can be unveiled through unsupervised learning. To this end, we train a "quantum dictionary", formulated as an overcomplete autoencoder, that sparsely represents the eigenstates of the system, using as an illustration the quantum baker map. The only explicit physical information imposed on the dictionary atoms is their localization in phase space, without providing any kind of information about the periodic orbits of the corresponding classical system. The model achieves high fidelity in reconstructing eigenstates not used during training. By comparing the learned atoms with an independently constructed "semiclassical dictionary", we find that they spontaneously localize on the periodic orbits and develop scar-like structures. This result is interesting in two ways: a localization constraint is sufficient to recover nontrivial semiclassical organization from spectral data and at the same time periodic orbits confirm their fundamental role in the structure of quantum chaotic eigenfunctions. More generally, our proposed architecture opens a new route to learning representations whose atoms optimize other chosen physical properties.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves a majorization relation concerning the sum of two operators, each being a tensor product of multiple positive semidefinite components. The proof utilizes advanced linear programming techniques applied to index set combinatorics. It advances the mathematical understanding of operator inequalities in high-dimensional quantum systems while also mapping out the precise limits where these relations break down.

**Why it may be interesting.** Majorization relations are fundamental tools for comparing quantum states or operators (e.g., entanglement measures). Establishing such structural inequalities for tensor products is crucial for understanding multipartite quantum correlations and resource theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove a separable version of Ky Fan's majorization relation for the sum of two operators, where each operator is constructed as a tensor product of multiple positive semidefinite (PSD) operators.

**Main result.** A specific majorization relation ($	ext{Theorem 1.1}$) is proven using linear programming techniques, although the authors also provide counterexamples showing that this relation fails for sums involving three or more tensor products.

**Method.** The proof heavily relies on linear programming methods and establishing inequalities related to the dimensions of intersections between subspaces defined by downward-closed and upward-closed sets in product index spaces.

**Model / system.** The mathematical framework involves operators acting on high-dimensional Hilbert spaces ($\mathbb{C}^{d_1 	imes \cdots 	imes d_n}$) that are structured as sums of tensor products of local PSD operators. This structure is relevant to analyzing quantum correlations or entanglement measures.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the eigenvalues ($\lambda$) of the sum of projected operators, and the dimension of intersections between subspaces defined by index sets $\Omega$ and $\Upsilon$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of tensor product factors ($n$), the number of summands (two in the main theorem), and the dimensions $d_i$ are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof technique is established for a specific case ($n=2$) using linear programming, and counterexamples demonstrate limitations when generalizing to more terms or factors.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the majorization problem ($	ext{Theorem 1.1}$), reduces it to an inequality involving projectors ($	ext{Theorem 1.2}$), and proves this latter theorem using combinatorial inequalities derived from properties of product spaces, culminating in counterexamples showing limitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We use linear programming to prove a separable version of Ky Fan's majorization relation for a sum of two operators that are each a tensor product of $n$ positive semidefinite operators. We give an example showing that such a relation does not hold in general for sums of three or more tensor products of three or more positive semidefinite operators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a powerful, non-perturbative mathematical framework to find exact solutions for the evolution operators of quantum systems with time-varying Hamiltonians. By combining $\langle\cdotangle$-algebra, path-sums, and Omega calculus, the authors overcome limitations inherent in standard approximations like Dyson series or RWA. This methodology offers a universal tool for analyzing complex dynamics in fields ranging from quantum spin batteries to general non-autonomous differential systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant for AMO physics as it provides rigorous mathematical tools to solve time-dependent quantum dynamics, which is crucial for understanding driven systems in quantum optics and superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is deriving analytical, exact, and assumption-free formulas for the evolution operators governing quantum systems driven by time-dependent Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** The authors provide these exact solutions using a novel combination of mathematical techniques that bypass traditional perturbative expansions or approximations.

**Method.** The methodology combines three advanced mathematical tools: the $\langle\cdotangle$-algebra, path-sums (related to graph walks), and Omega calculus for systematic evaluation of resulting resolvents.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on 2-level quantum systems relevant to quantum spin batteries, governed by time-dependent Schrödinger equations. The Hamiltonian can be generalized but includes specific forms involving coupling terms $\epsilon(t)$ and $\Gamma$.

**Key observables.** Transition probabilities between spin states ($P_{|0angle 	o |1angle}(t)$) and the stored energy ($\Delta E(t)$) are key observables derived from the evolution operator $U(t,s)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is general, claiming validity across all parameter regimes, including cases where standard approximations (like RWA) might fail.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary strength claimed is the avoidance of assumptions; however, specific appendices discuss simplified scenarios like commuting Hamiltonians or using initial transformations for tractability.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the provided notes. The discussion references mathematical derivations (Eqs. (31), (G1), etc.) rather than figure descriptions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the general problem and introducing the novel $\langle\cdotangle$-algebraic framework, then details how path-sums and Omega calculus evaluate the resulting resolvents. It applies this machinery to specific physical models like spin batteries (Bloch-Siegert) and Brownian motion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the analytical, exact, explicit, and assumption free formulas for the evolution operators corresponding to four instances of time-dependent Hamiltonians relevant to quantum spin batteries including two stochastic cases. We demonstrate how to recover and go beyond existing expansions and approximations directly from the exact solutions giving, for example, an explicit exact formula for Floquet Hamiltonians at all orders. The exact solutions are obtained through a completely novel combination of three mathematical techniques, the $\star$-algebra, path-sums and Omega calculus, which we briefly overview. These are widely applicable to other non-autonomous differential systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper explores how quasiperiodic order interacts with non-Hermiticity to govern the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect. By analyzing modulated Hatano–Nelson models, the authors show that critical states found under periodic boundary conditions can induce skin effects when open boundaries are imposed. The findings extend this understanding to complex systems like long-range and multi-band lattices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges concepts from topological physics (NHSE) with localization theory (quasiperiodicity), providing analytical control over how boundary conditions affect spectral properties in complex quantum systems, which has implications for photonic and cold-atom lattices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the complex interplay between quasiperiodic criticality, non-Hermiticity, and boundary conditions in condensed matter models.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that parameter regimes exhibiting quasiperiodic critical states under periodic boundary conditions can also manifest the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect under open boundary conditions.

**Method.** The study employs analytical techniques like non-unitary gauge transformations to map non-Hermitian systems to Hermitian ones, alongside numerical diagnostics such as Lyapunov exponents and Inverse Participation Ratios (IPR) for localization analysis.

**Model / system.** The primary model is a quasiperiodically modulated Hatano–Nelson Hamiltonian. Extensions include long-range hopping terms and a two-band Rice–Mele model, all incorporating nonreciprocal hopping.

**Key observables.** Lyapunov exponent ($\lambda$), Directional Inverse Participation Ratio (dIPR), Mean Inverse Participation Ratio (MIPR), and the fractal dimension ($eta$) derived from finite-size scaling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The modulation strengths ($\mu_R, \mu_L$), the irrational frequency $\alpha$, and the boundary conditions (PBC vs. OBC) are crucial parameters defining different physical regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping non-Hermitian systems to effective Hermitian forms via gauge transformations and analyzing the thermodynamic limit for analytical results.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of MIPR/dMIPR on system size (N) to determine fractal dimensions ($eta$) under PBC, and show schematic comparisons between nearest-neighbor, long-range, and two-band models regarding skin effect signatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the basic quasiperiodic Hatano–Nelson model using gauge transformations and Lyapunov exponents. It then extends the analysis to include long-range hoppings and multi-band systems (Rice-Mele), consistently applying PBC/OBC diagnostics to confirm the persistence of the NHSE in critical regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quasiperiodic lattices can host critical eigenstates, whereas nonreciprocal hopping in non-Hermitian lattices can induce non-Hermitian skin effect. In this work, we investigate localization phenomena in a Hatano--Nelson model with quasiperiodically modulated hopping amplitudes, where nonreciprocity arises from unequal modulation strengths of the right and left hoppings. Using a non-unitary gauge transformation, we map the non-Hermitian system into a Hermitian quasiperiodic system and obtain an exact analytical expression for the Lyapunov exponent in the thermodynamic limit. Under periodic boundary conditions, inverse participation ratios and finite-size scaling analysis are used to identify the quasiperiodic critical regimes. The comparison shows that parameter regimes hosting quasiperiodic critical states under periodic boundary conditions can exhibit the non-Hermitian skin effect under open boundary conditions. Furthermore, the non-Hermitian skin effect associated with quasiperiodic critical regimes is also observed in representative long-range hopping models and multiband extensions. Our results provide an analytically controlled perspective on how quasiperiodicity, modulated nonreciprocity, and boundary conditions jointly shape the non-Hermitian skin effect in critical regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends the concept of quantum complexity from ideal closed systems to real-world open systems subject to dissipation. It reformulates complexity as an optimal control problem on the space of mixed states, revealing that the resulting geometry is sub-Finslerian rather than Riemannian. This provides a powerful geometric tool for quantifying information processing limits in noisy quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the quantification of complexity in realistic, dissipative quantum systems, which is a major frontier connecting quantum information theory with open quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is extending the geometric definition of quantum complexity, originally for closed systems, to open quantum systems governed by dissipation.

**Main result.** The geometry in open systems is fundamentally sub-Finslerian rather than Riemannian due to irreversible dissipation, providing a new framework to quantify complexity in realistic noisy environments.

**Method.** Complexity is defined via an optimal control problem on the space of mixed states, analyzed using tools from Finsler geometry and incorporating costs for both unitary and non-unitary generators.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses open quantum systems described by density matrices evolving under the Lindblad equation. Specific models include a single qubit with depolarizing/amplitude-damping channels and the damped harmonic oscillator.

**Key observables.** Complexity, flag curvature (Finslerian analog of sectional curvature), and non-reversibility of geodesics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Penalty factors in the cost functional; dissipation rates ($\Gamma$); Hamiltonian parameters ($H$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad equation (CPTP maps). The geometry is generally sub-Finslerian because the evolution is constrained by physical dissipation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the known unitary case to the open system generalization, deriving the necessary optimal control formulation and analyzing how dissipation modifies the underlying geometric structure (Riemannian $	o$ sub-Finslerian).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We extend Nielsen's geometric approach for quantum complexity from closed to open quantum systems, whose dynamics is governed by Lindbladian evolution. In this framework, complexity is defined through an optimal-control problem on the space of mixed states, with a cost assigned to both unitary and non-unitary generators. We show that the resulting geometric structure differs fundamentally from the Riemannian geometry that emerges in the case of unitary evolution. In the open-system setting, the natural geometry is typically sub-Finslerian. Dissipation makes the geodesics non-reversible, while the admissible tangent directions are restricted by the physically allowed controls. We analyze several physically motivated examples, including a single qubit subject to depolarizing and amplitude-damping channels, as well as the damped harmonic oscillator. We show that, similarly to the unitary case, varying the penalty factors in the cost functional modifies the geometric properties through changes in the flag curvature, the Finslerian analog of sectional curvature. Our results provide a geometric framework for quantifying the abstract notion of complexity in dissipative quantum systems, with potential connections to experimentally realizable setups.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a comprehensive framework to ensure that nonlinear convection-diffusion models rigorously obey the second law of thermodynamics. It achieves this by defining thermodynamic consistency as an invariant property preserved across continuum modeling, subsystem coupling, linearization, and numerical discretization. The work provides a powerful structural tool for building physically consistent computational models in transport physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical transport, the rigorous mathematical machinery—especially the emphasis on structure preservation under approximation/decomposition—is highly relevant for developing consistent quantum field theories or open quantum system dynamics where approximations are unavoidable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a rigorous mathematical framework for nonlinear convection-diffusion systems that guarantees the preservation of thermodynamic balance (the second law) across all modeling stages.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the free-energy balance is invariant under key operations like restriction to subdomains, interface coupling, and numerical discretization schemes.

**Method.** The work employs a continuum-first framework built on defining open constitutive structures using free-energy functionals and analyzing structural preservation via categorical methods.

**Model / system.** Open nonlinear convection-diffusion systems governing transport phenomena (mass/heat transfer) in fluids or porous media, modeled around a free-energy balance equation.

**Key observables.** Free energy functional ($F$), Dissipation term ($D \ge 0$), Boundary fluxes ($B$), and Source terms ($S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system state field ($
u$) and the constitutive flux decomposition ($J = J_{	ext{rev}} + J_{	ext{diss}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence of an admissible state field $\psi$ and the requirement that the thermodynamic balance must hold exactly across structural transformations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Notes focus on theorems, definitions, and mathematical structures rather than figure content).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the continuous free-energy balance (Theorem 2.9), extends this to local/global reconstruction via category theory (Theorems 3.5, 4.5, 4.13), and finally applies these structural invariants to ensure consistency across semidiscrete and fully discrete numerical schemes (Theorems 7.4, 7.7).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinear convection--diffusion systems play a central role in transport phenomena, including mass transfer, heat transfer, porous-media transport, and coupled continuum processes with source, exchange, and interface effects. In such systems, the key question is often not only which governing partial differential equation is used, but whether the model preserves a consistent thermodynamic balance under the operations that arise naturally in transport analysis: restriction to subdomains, coupling across interfaces, linearization near equilibrium, and discretization for computation.   This paper develops a continuum-first framework for open nonlinear convection--diffusion systems in which thermodynamic consistency is formulated as a free-energy balance with nonnegative bulk dissipation and explicit boundary and source contributions. Within this setting, nonlinear transport systems are defined as structured objects built from admissible state fields, storage functionals, constitutive flux decompositions, sources, and boundary ports. We prove that the thermodynamic balance is preserved under exact structure-preserving transformations, restriction to subdomains, local-to-global reconstruction over compatible domain decompositions, and power-conserving interconnection of open subsystems. We then derive classical linear convection--diffusion models as tangent thermodynamic descendants at equilibrium and show that the same invariant survives weak formulation, semidiscretization, and fully discrete time stepping when the numerical design respects thermodynamic structure. Nonlinear drift--diffusion and porous-medium convection--diffusion are used as explicit examples. The resulting contribution is a compositional transport framework in which the second law remains visible across continuum modeling, subsystem coupling, linear approximation, and computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, hardware-efficient ansatz for variational quantum algorithms whose geometric metric is provably diagonal. This breakthrough eliminates the need for costly auxiliary measurements when calculating natural gradients or performing time evolution in quantum simulations. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on molecular and Hubbard models with significantly fewer gates than existing leading methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO physicists working on open quantum systems or many-body dynamics, this provides a highly efficient, geometrically sound framework to implement variational methods (like VQE) that can handle complex Hamiltonians while maintaining rigorous control over optimization landscapes and computational cost.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is overcoming the computational overhead associated with calculating the Fubini-Study metric for variational quantum algorithms used in time evolution and optimization.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a hardware-efficient ansatz whose Fubini-Study pullback metric is diagonal in closed form, enabling natural gradient calculations and time evolution without costly auxiliary measurements or matrix inversions.

**Method.** They utilize a binary tree parameterization for the quantum state, deriving an exact, diagonal expression for the metric tensor that simplifies complex geometric computations into simple weight/probability tracking.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to simulating electronic structure calculations for small molecules and Hubbard quench dynamics. The ansatz is designed to be spin-adapted and applicable to general quantum many-body problems.

**Key observables.** Fubini-Study metric, two-qubit gate count, natural gradient signal magnitude, leakage $L(\phi)$, and energy expectation values ($E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), the dimension of the active subspace ($k$), and the required accuracy (reference-level).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the target state is supported on a low-dimensional subspace $k \ll 2^n$ to enable parameter pruning; it also relies on the existence of structured dressings for quantum advantage.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the end-to-end pipeline, showing how the binary tree structure classifies parameters and leads to a pruned circuit whose complexity scales favorably with the active subspace size $k$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the metric bottleneck in VQAs, proposing the diagonal ansatz structure via tree parameterization, deriving the closed-form metric for time evolution/gradient calculation, demonstrating pruning efficiency, and finally validating its use on molecular and Hubbard dynamics simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational quantum algorithms depend on the geometry of their parametrised circuits: metric-aware optimisation and time evolution require the Fubini-Study metric, which has hitherto demanded costly auxiliary measurements and ill-conditioned inversions. This work introduces a hardware-efficient $n$-qubit ansatz, which parametrises states by a binary tree and whose Fubini-Study pullback metric is diagonal in closed form. Quantum natural gradient on the tree parameters, variational imaginary- and real-time evolution, and exact unitary-invariant (Haar) sampling on a symmetry sector run with no auxiliary metric circuits or matrix inversion. When the target state is supported on a subspace of $k$ computational-basis states, the redundant tree parameters carry a gauge freedom a pruning compiler converts into circuits whose two-qubit count provably grows linearly in $k$; a variant reaches near-optimal $O(nk/\log n)$ scaling with the closed-form metric intact. On electronic-structure calculations for small molecules and half-filled Hubbard quench dynamics, the method reaches reference-level accuracy with one to three orders of magnitude fewer two-qubit gates than leading alternatives. Interchangeable constructions (a Schur-transform dressing or internal reparameterisations) make the ansatz exactly spin-adapted, with fixed total spin at every parameter and no penalty terms. The bare ansatz is an exactly controllable, well-conditioned and barren-plateau-free primitive for preparing and sampling sector states: on its own, it is classically simulable in $k$ (a boundary proved for a general class of sector-sparse ansätze); composed with a classically hard dressing, it yields molecular ground states, sector-Haar benchmarking, thermal correlators, and exact effective Hamiltonians trained from energy measurements alone, with the composed circuit carrying the potential for quantum advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how magnetic skyrmions confined to a quasicrystalline lattice create an emergent, field-tunable gauge field. The unique quasiperiodic pinning landscape allows the skyrmion density—and thus the topological Hall conductivity—to be smoothly controlled by small changes in the external magnetic field. This suggests quasicrystals are promising platforms for advanced magnetoelectronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent gauge fields controlled by topological textures (skyrmions) in a geometrically constrained system like a quasicrystal is highly relevant to understanding tunable electronic properties in novel magnetic materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how magnetic skyrmions on a quasicrystalline lattice generate tunable emergent gauge fields, which can control the electronic Hall conductivity.

**Main result.** The quasiperiodic pinning landscape allows for a smooth, field-controlled suppression of skyrmion density near saturation, enabling continuous tuning of the topological Hall response to zero.

**Method.** Theoretical modeling using classical Heisenberg spin dynamics combined with linear spin-wave theory and effective particle models.

**Model / system.** The system is magnetic skyrmions governed by a classical Heisenberg model incorporating Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions on a 2D quasicrystalline lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), skyrmion density, magnon eigenstate localization (IPR), and topological charge ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic field ($H$), Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya coupling strength ($D$), and the lattice structure itself.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often treats skyrmions as point-like particles confined to metastable pinning centers, and approximations like LSWT are employed.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $\sigma_{xy}$ vs $H$ showing continuous tunability near saturation; IPR plots characterize magnon eigenstate localization (extended vs. critical); energy histograms show distinct skyrmion energy sectors.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the Hamiltonian on the quasicrystal, analyzing the resulting magnon spectrum and state localization via LSWT/IPR, to deriving an effective model for skyrmion interactions, and finally calculating the field-dependent Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study magnetic skyrmions in a two-dimensional quasicrystalline lattice using a classical Heisenberg model with Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions and an external magnetic field. The competition between the skyrmion-skyrmion repulsion and an emergent quasiperiodic pinning landscape gives rise to a sequence of distinct skyrmion lattice configurations as a function of field. The resulting hierarchy of quasiperiodic pinning potentials, characterized by closely spaced quasi-degenerate minima, enables a quasi-continuous suppression of the skyrmion density as the saturation field is approached, in sharp contrast to the strongly first-order collapse of skyrmion crystals on periodic lattices. This provides a direct mechanism for controlling the topological charge and, consequently, the emergent gauge field for itinerant electrons. As a consequence, the Hall conductivity can be strongly modified with small changes in the magnetic field and driven smoothly to zero near saturation. This field-controlled tunability, rooted in the underlying multistability, identifies quasicrystalline magnets as a platform for tunable topological textures, with potential applications in magnetic memory and magnetoelectronic response.

</details>

**Summary.** This lecture notes guide provides a comprehensive overview of Random Matrix Theory (RMT), detailing how its core results are derived using both elementary and advanced mathematical tools. It establishes RMT as a powerful framework for predicting spectral statistics across diverse fields, including condensed matter physics and finance. The work is valuable for practitioners needing to master the analytical techniques required for disordered systems research.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on random matrices, the techniques (e.g., spectral analysis, localization theory) are highly relevant to understanding energy spectra in disordered quantum systems or analyzing noise/fluctuations in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is determining the statistical properties of eigenvalues and eigenvectors for large random matrices without direct calculation.

**Main result.** Classic results, such as Wigner's semicircle law for eigenvalue density, are derived using various advanced analytical techniques.

**Method.** The paper details pedagogical derivations using the cavity method, followed by introducing advanced formalisms like diagrammatic, replica, path-integral, and supersymmetric methods.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on large square random matrices (e.g., Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble) whose entries are independent random variables scaled by $1/N$. Applications span nuclear physics, finance, spin glasses, and quantum chaos.

**Key observables.** Eigenvalue density $ho(\omega)$, eigenvalue spacing distributions, and spectral correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system size $N$ is taken to infinity ($N 	o \infty$), and the analysis often involves taking limits in the complex plane ($\epsilon 	o 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that ensemble averages are sufficient, and for spectral density extraction, the variance of matrix entries must scale as $1/N$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the convergence of eigenvalue histograms (for large N) towards a limiting distribution, such as the Wigner semicircle law.

**Paper structure.** The notes suggest a two-part structure: first, pedagogical derivation of classic results using simple methods; second, an advanced reference guide introducing and comparing complex analytical tools like replica and path-integral formalisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Random matrix theory has established itself as a theoretical cornerstone of the mathematical sciences over the past century. It has undeniable utility in areas of research as diverse as nuclear physics, finance, ecology and disordered systems. The purpose of these notes is twofold. First, the most famous and widely used classic results are derived in a pedagogical manner, mostly using the comparatively elementary and transparent cavity method. The significance of each result is then demonstrated in the context of a particular application. There are also some select exercises at the end of each section. In the second part of these notes, a reference guide of analytical techniques for the random-matrix/disordered-systems practitioner is provided. Introducing the diagrammatic, replica, path-integral, and supersymmetric formalisms from first principles, we rederive some of the aforementioned classic results, particularly focussing on the simplest one -- the semicircle law. Innovations such as the population dynamics method and the tools of free probability theory are also included. We discuss the merits of each analytical approach, and we highlight the contexts in which each becomes particularly useful.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that a toroidal superfluid containing multiple Josephson junctions can function as a highly sensitive gyroscope. Rotation causes a measurable splitting in the natural oscillation frequencies, providing a direct signal proportional to $\Omega$. This method offers superior sensitivity scaling ($\Delta\Omega \sim n^{-3/2}$) compared to standard measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies advanced many-body physics techniques (linearization of GPE) to create a novel quantum sensor concept, bridging fundamental superfluid dynamics with metrology.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a compact, scalable rotation sensor using superfluid dynamics.

**Main result.** Rotation lifts the degeneracy of Josephson modes, creating a frequency splitting that grows linearly with both the rotation rate ($\Omega$) and the number of barriers ($n$), leading to an estimation uncertainty scaling as $\Delta\Omega \sim n^{-3/2}$.

**Method.** Analytical derivation of normal mode spectra via linearization around equilibrium, confirmed by full numerical simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a toroidal superfluid (BEC) interrupted by $n$ tunneling barriers. Dynamics are modeled using the Gross-Pitaevskii equation in the rotating frame.

**Key observables.** Josephson-mode spectrum, frequency splitting ($\delta\omega_k(\Omega)$), and two-frequency beatings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rotation frequency ($\Omega$), number of barriers ($n$), tunneling coefficient ($J$), interaction strength ($U$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the small-amplitude oscillation regime; nearest-neighbor contributions are kept in the derivation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup, the Doppler splitting effect, and time evolution of density profiles showing both monochromatic ($\Omega=0$) and beating ($\Omega
eq 0$) oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model (GPE in a ring) to deriving the normal mode spectrum analytically. It then uses this framework to show how rotation lifts degeneracies, leading to measurable frequency splittings that scale favorably with $n$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that a toroidal superfluid interrupted by $n$ tunneling barriers realizes a compact Josephson gyroscope with an $n$-enhanced response to rotation. In the small-amplitude regime, we derive analytically the normal mode spectrum of the coupled population-phase oscillations. In the absence of rotation, pairs of modes are degenerate: a finite angular velocity $Ω$ lifts this degeneracy through a Doppler shift, producing a frequency splitting that grows linearly with both $Ω$ and $n$. Full numerical simulations confirm this prediction and reveal long-lived two-frequency beatings, in sharp contrast with the monochromatic Josephson oscillations of the nonrotating system. These beatings provide a direct rotation signal with estimation uncertainty scaling as $ΔΩ\sim n^{-3/2}$, while remaining robust against imperfections and dynamical excitations. These results identify multi-junction toroidal superfluids as scalable, micrometer-size rotation sensors compatible with current experimental platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper shows how the Dynamical Casimir Effect, usually seen as an error mechanism in cavity-qubit systems, can be deliberately harnessed. By applying optimal quantum control techniques, the authors design precise driving pulses to deterministically generate high-fidelity nonclassical states like Fock and cat states. This demonstrates a powerful new paradigm for resource engineering in superconducting circuit QED.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges fundamental cavity QED physics with advanced quantum control theory. Showing that a process typically viewed as decoherence (DCE) can be controlled to create useful resources (nonclassical states) is a major advance for quantum optics and circuit QED platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To utilize the Dynamical Casimir Effect (DCE) not as an error source, but actively as a resource to deterministically engineer nonclassical states of light.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated high-fidelity generation of various nonclassical states, including Fock and Schrödinger-cat states, by optimizing driving protocols in cavity-qubit systems.

**Method.** Optimal quantum control techniques (CRAB and GRAPE) were employed to design time-dependent driving fields that steer the system evolution toward target quantum states.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a single-mode electromagnetic cavity ultrastrongly coupled to a frequency-tunable qubit. The dynamics are modeled using Hamiltonians incorporating counter-rotating terms, and open-system evolution is treated via the GKLS master equation.

**Key observables.** State fidelity (F), control cost, and Wigner functions plotted in phase space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system operates in the Ultrastrong Coupling (USC) regime; key parameters include coupling strengths ($g/\omega_c$) and environmental spectral densities ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on optimal control theory, assuming that the GKLS master equation accurately describes dissipation under weak system-bath coupling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optimized driving protocols for generating specific states (e.g., $|n=6angle$), comparing Wigner functions across ideal, unitary, and open-system evolutions, and showing performance metrics versus noise levels or control parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of harnessing DCE, details the Hamiltonian and system setup in the USC regime, applies optimal quantum control methods (CRAB/GRAPE) to design protocols, and finally presents results demonstrating high-fidelity state preparation while analyzing robustness against various noise sources.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonadiabatic driving in ultrastrongly coupled light--matter systems is commonly regarded as a source of errors, as counter-rotating interactions convert vacuum fluctuations into real excitations through the dynamical Casimir effect (DCE). Here we show that, instead, the DCE can be harnessed as a resource for engineering nonclassical states of light. Considering a cavity mode ultrastrongly coupled to a frequency-tunable qubit, we employ optimal quantum control to design driving protocols that convert vacuum fluctuations into targeted states. Numerical optimization reveals a versatile and robust approach for the deterministic preparation of a broad class of nonclassical states, illustrated here through Fock states, squeezed states, and Schrödinger-cat-state superpositions.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study proposes using spacer-layer Cl doping as a chemical method to induce superconductivity in low-layer number nickelates. By calculating layer-resolved electronic correlations using DFT+DMFT, the authors show that this doping strategy successfully tunes the material's correlation strength to match known superconducting compounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the use of advanced many-body techniques like DFT+DMFT to map out phase diagrams based on tuning parameters (like doping) shares conceptual overlap with controlling quantum phases in artificial quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to design low-layer number ($n$) square-planar nickelates into superconducting candidates by tuning their electronic structure and correlation strength.

**Main result.** Spacer-layer Cl doping can drive low-$n$ compounds into a correlated regime comparable to higher-$n$ superconducting nickelates, suggesting it is a promising chemical strategy for material design.

**Method.** The research employs Density Functional Theory combined with Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DFT+DMFT) calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is multi-layer square-planar nickelates ($	ext{R}_{n+1}	ext{Ni}_n	ext{O}_{2n+2}$), focusing on the $	ext{Ni-}d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbital. The model uses Cl doping to chemically tune the Ni valence in low-$n$ systems.

**Key observables.** Layer-resolved electronic structure, correlation strength (quantified by mass enhancement $m^*/m$), and the imaginary part of the Matsubara self-energy ($	ext{Im}\Sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The layer number ($n=2$ to $6$) and the doping level achieved via Cl substitution are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that spacer-layer Cl substitution provides a chemically natural way to tune valence while keeping the active $	ext{NiO}_2$ planes largely intact.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare self-energies and mass enhancements across different $n$ values, demonstrating how doping shifts low-$n$ systems towards the correlation regime of high-$n$ superconductors.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the correlation hierarchy in undoped nickelates. It then introduces Cl doping as a tuning mechanism to compensate electrons into low-$n$ compounds, showing that this process successfully induces strong $	ext{Ni-}d$ correlations necessary for superconductivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multi-layer square-planar nickelates provide a rare platform in which the nominal Ni valence, dimensionality, and layer-resolved electronic structure can be tuned within the same structural family. Recent experiments have found superconductivity in $n=4$--8 $R_{n+1}Ni_nO_{2n+2}$ compounds, with the highest $T_c$ near $n=6$, whereas the more heavily hole-doped $n=3$ member remains nonsuperconducting. Here we propose spacer-layer Cl doping as a route to convert low-$n$ nickelates into superconducting candidates. Compared with changing the layer number $n$, Cl substitution on the spacer-layer oxygen sites offers a chemically natural way to continuously tune the Ni valence while leaving the NiO$_2$ planes largely intact; the lower-$n$ compounds may also be more accessible for synthesis. Using density functional theory combined with dynamical mean-field theory, we show that electron-compensated $n=2$ and $n=3$ La-based nickelates, targeted to the nominal Ni valence of superconducting $n=6$, develop Ni-$d$ correlations comparable to those of superconducting higher-$n$ compounds while preserving the characteristic low-energy Ni-$d$ electronic structure. These results suggest spacer-layer Cl doping as a promising strategy for designing low-$n$ square-planar nickelate superconductors.

</details>

### [Global Precision Limits in Critical Quantum Metrology: From Cramér-Rao to Ziv-Zakai](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08431v1)

**Authors:** Neng Zeng, Tao Liu, Yu-Ran Zhang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08431v1>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes precision limits in critical quantum metrology by comparing the Cramér-Rao bound to the Ziv-Zakai bound. The key finding is that while QFI suggests super-Heisenberg scaling at criticality, incorporating prior knowledge via the QZZB reveals this enhancement is merely a constant factor relative to the initial uncertainty. This establishes fundamental limits for quantum sensing in many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for quantum sensing in condensed matter systems near criticality. It refines existing understanding by quantifying the role of prior knowledge, which is crucial for designing practical quantum sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the fundamental limit on precision achievable in quantum metrology near a critical point, specifically distinguishing between genuine gains from criticality versus artifacts arising solely from prior knowledge.

**Main result.** By using the Quantum Ziv-Zakai Bound (QZZB), the authors show that for second-order phase transitions, the enhancement factor remains constant relative to the prior standard deviation, indicating that super-Heisenberg scaling is due to prior information rather than criticality itself.

**Method.** The study compares the standard Quantum Cramér-Rao Bound (QCRB) with the global QZZB, deriving lower bounds on the Mean-Square Error ($\Sigma$) by incorporating prior knowledge via a Gaussian distribution.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to quantum many-body systems undergoing second-order quantum phase transitions. Specific models analyzed include a 1D system with Stark localization and an anisotropic transverse-field XY chain.

**Key observables.** Mean-Square Error ($\Sigma$), Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), Enhancement factor ($\eta$), and the scaling exponent $G(N, W)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical exponents ($\alpha$, $
u$), system size ($N$), prior width ($W$ or $\sigma$), and the critical point location ($	heta_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The QZZB provides a more general limit than the QCRB, which assumes unbiased estimation; the analysis focuses on regimes where the prior information is significant.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how the inverse QZZB and the enhancement factor $\eta$ scale with system size $N$ for both studied models. They consistently show that $\eta$ approaches a constant value when analyzed using the QZZB, regardless of whether the tighter or looser bound is used.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the QZZB, applies it to two distinct physical models exhibiting second-order phase transitions, and analyzes the scaling behavior of the achievable precision limit ($\Sigma$) relative to the prior uncertainty ($W$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Critical quantum metrology with equilibrium states predicts quantum-enhanced sensitivity only in the vicinity of criticality, where large prior information about the parameter is required. By employing quantum Ziv-Zakai bounds, we derive a limit on the mean-square error in critical quantum metrology. For second-order quantum phase transitions, we show that the precision predicted by the Cramér-Rao bound offers no substantial improvement over the prior standard deviation. Thus, the critical quantum sensor's precision can only achieve a constant gain compared to the prior standard deviation, even without performing any measurement. We elucidate the fundamental limitation on the achievable precision in critical quantum metrology in the context of local sensing, even without considering state-preparation costs or noise. Thus, the super-Heisenberg-limited sensitivity at criticality arises from precise prior knowledge rather than a genuine gain due to criticality. Our work provides a practical framework for assessing critical quantum metrology and a routine for studying quantum sensing with many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an exactly solvable theoretical paradigm for non-Hermitian topology driven by momentum-dependent identity terms. By studying an SO-coupled Hatano-Nelson chain, the authors show that such terms actively deform the system's Brillouin Zone, leading to topological edge states and zero modes even when conventional symmetries are absent. This establishes a rigorous benchmark for testing topological invariants in complex open quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalizable framework for analyzing non-Hermitian systems where simple symmetry arguments fail due to complex momentum dependence in the identity term, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper challenges established topological criteria for non-Hermitian systems by showing that a momentum-dependent identity term can actively deform the Generalized Brillouin Zone (GBZ), invalidating fixed contour assumptions.

**Main result.** They establish an exactly solvable paradigm demonstrating that inter-cell spin-orbit coupling, combined with this identity term, induces robust topological edge states and zero modes even without chiral symmetry protection.

**Method.** The authors provide exact analytical solutions for the non-Hermitian eigensystem under open boundary conditions by solving characteristic polynomial equations derived from the Hamiltonian structure.

**Model / system.** The model is an SO-coupled 1D Hatano-Nelson (HN) chain, generalized to a fully generic two-band system. The Hamiltonian includes a momentum-dependent identity term $\epsilon_0(\mathbf{k})\mathbb{I}$ alongside spin-orbit coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Topological edge states, robust zero modes, and the deformation of the Generalized Brillouin Zone (GBZ).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-cell SO coupling ($	ilde{t}_2$), identity term parameters ($\epsilon_0(\mathbf{k})$), and system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving exact analytical solutions for the OBC spectrum, extending beyond previous criteria that required fixed GBZ contours or specific symmetries.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the SO-coupled HN chain showing GBZ deformation and induced edge states. Phase diagrams map out topological regimes based on coupling strengths, confirming bulk-boundary correspondence via energy band analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first introducing the physical model and identifying the failure point in standard topology theory. It then presents the exact analytical solution for the OBC spectrum using polynomial equations derived from the Hamiltonian. Finally, it analyzes the resulting topological phases and confirms robustness against disorder.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

An identity term in the Hamiltonian is conventionally regarded as spectrally inert-it shifts energies but does not alter eigenstate topology. We show that under non-Hermitian skin pumping, this paradigm fails: a momentum-dependent identity term actively deforms the generalized Brillouin zone, thereby challenging established topological criteria that rely on fixed complex contours. Here, by introducing spin-orbit coupling into a Hatano-Nelson chain, we present an exact analytical solution for the entire non-Hermitian eigensystem under open boundary conditions. Our solution reveals how inter-cell spin-orbit coupling, synergizing with this non-trivial identity term, induces topological edge states and robust zero modes in the complete absence of chiral symmetry. This work establishes an exactly solvable paradigm for non-Hermitian topology beyond symmetry protection, and provides a rigorous benchmark for testing topological invariants in systems with momentum-dependent identity terms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes how vacancies perturb the magnetic order in altermagnets. By using continuum field theory on lattice models, the authors predict that these defects induce unique, symmetry-dictated anisotropic magnetization textures around them. This provides a powerful, local diagnostic tool for identifying altermagnetic quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the use of low-energy field theory expansions, symmetry analysis ($\Theta_{\sigma_d}$), and defect response calculations mirrors techniques used in analyzing boundary effects or impurities in complex many-body systems relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how non-magnetic vacancies induce anisotropic magnetization textures within altermagnets, providing a local probe for detecting this specific magnetic order.

**Main result.** Vacancies generate symmetry-protected, anisotropic magnetization distortions in the surrounding spins, with the decay laws differing between classical and quantum regimes (e.g., $1/r^5$ decay for quantum case).

**Method.** The analysis employs a combination of microscopic simulations for classical models and low-energy field theory expansions (including continuum limits) for both classical and quantum spin systems.

**Model / system.** The system is an altermagnet, modeled on checkerboard or honeycomb lattices using Heisenberg Hamiltonians. The key feature is the symmetry that allows for finite spatial multipoles of uniform magnetization despite zero net magnetization.

**Key observables.** Vacancy-induced anisotropic distortion of local moments, the resulting magnetization texture $M(\mathbf{r})$, and its long-distance decay laws (e.g., $\sin(2	heta)$ dependence).

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic field strength ($h$), lattice couplings ($J_A$, $J_1$, etc.), and the spin magnitude ($S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on gradient expansions, assuming small deviations from homogeneous magnetization in the continuum limit, and treating the vacancy as a localized defect.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the checkerboard lattice model and expected textures near vacancies for both classical (transverse field) and quantum (zero field) cases. Other figures show scaling collapses of magnetization profiles against theoretical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theory by first establishing the altermagnetic nature, then applying continuum field theory to derive effective Hamiltonians incorporating vacancy terms. Results are presented for both classical (large-S limit) and quantum spin models on different lattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study magnetic textures induced by vacancies in altermagnets using microscopic simulations and low-energy field theory. We show that a vacancy generically produces a real-space anisotropic distortion of the magnetic order, whose structure encodes the symmetry of the underlying altermagnetic state. This impurity response offers a direct route to detecting altermagnetic order with locally resolved probes. We demonstrate this for both classical altermagnets, where vacancies generate anisotropic magnetization textures in a transverse magnetic field, and quantum models, where fluctuations induce longitudinal power-law decaying magnetic distortions even at zero field.

</details>
