**Summary.** The authors propose using triangulene-based diradicals as novel molecular quantum platforms, drawing parallels to robust defects like $	ext{NV}^-$ centers. Using advanced theoretical methods, they calculate spin coherence times and demonstrate that these molecules exhibit optically addressable triplet states suitable for qubit applications. The findings establish a theoretical roadmap for designing highly coherent, room-temperature molecular qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the theoretical design principles for solid-state molecular qubits by providing quantitative predictions for coherence times and optical control mechanisms based on first-principles calculations. It bridges fundamental quantum chemistry with applied quantum information science.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing molecular quantum platforms that combine long spin coherence times with efficient, optically addressable interfaces for quantum computation.

**Main result.** The study predicts that triangulene-based diradicals possess a triplet ground state resembling $	ext{NV}^-$ centers and can exhibit highly spin-selective optical readouts. Theoretical modeling suggests pathways to achieve long coherence times ($T_2$) approaching the molecular core limit (up to $27 	ext{ ms}$ at $300 	ext{ K}$).

**Method.** The research employs advanced first-principles methods, including multireference quantum chemistry and spin-vibronic coupling calculations. Dynamics are modeled using quantum master equations for phonon and nuclear spin interactions.

**Model / system.** The physical systems studied are triangulene (1), its aza-cation derivative (2), and the crystal of 2,6,10-tri-tert-butyl-4,8,12-trimesityl-triangulene (3). The theoretical framework incorporates electronic ($\hat{H}_e$), phonon ($\hat{H}_{ph}$), and electron-phonon coupling ($\hat{H}_{e-ph}$) Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Spin coherence time ($T_2$), spin relaxation time ($T_1$), energy separation between triplet ground state and singlet excited state ($\approx 0.5 	ext{ eV}$), and the rate/selectivity of inter-system crossing (ISC).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T_2 = 0.21 	ext{ ms}$ at $10 	ext{ K}$; estimated $T_1=27 	ext{ ms}$ at $300 	ext{ K}$; operation ideally at room temperature.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on expanding the vibronic coupling up to the quadratic term and modeling spin-spin dephasing via fluctuations in the effective magnetic field from nuclear spins.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates $	ext{NV}^-$ center transitions (ISC, IC) alongside molecular representations of Compounds 1, 2, and 3, including the crystal structure of compound 3.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the need for robust molecular qubits, analyzing the electronic structure of triangulene derivatives to show $	ext{NV}^-$ center analogies, calculating spin decoherence from phonons/nuclei, and finally demonstrating optically addressable, spin-selective processes like ISC at room temperature.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The identification of molecules that combine long spin coherence times and efficient spin-optical interfaces, ideally at room temperature, is pivotal towards the development of molecular quantum technology. By means of advanced first-principles methods, we here unravel the electronic structure for triangulene (1), its aza-cation derivative (2), and the crystal of 2,6,10-tri-tert-butyl-4,8,12-trimesityl-triangulene (3), and show that these organic diradicals possess a triplet ground state well separated from the first singlet excited state approaching 0.5 eV, closely resembling solid-state defects like nitrogen vacancy centers. In addition, we compute spin decoherence times due to the interaction with phonons and surrounding nuclear spins, showing that a deuterated molecule of 3 in a nuclear spin-free environment would support $T_2 = 0.21$ ms at 10 K. Importantly, we show that the engineering of specific low-energy vibrations could significantly improve $T_2$ toward the limit imposed by the molecular core spin relaxation, here estimated to be as long as $T_1=27$ ms at 300 K for 2. Finally, we compute two-phonon contributions to inter-system crossing at 300 K for2 as a luminescent prototype, and find that it is highly spin-selective, supporting the possibility to engineer optical read out and spin initialization. These results advance a unified first-principles theoretical foundation of spin decoherence and spin-selective excited-state processes and point to novel chemical design strategies for optically addressable, highly coherent molecular qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the Bayesian Information Restricted Diffusion (BIRD) model to theoretically understand how generative AI generalizes from limited data. It establishes a precise information-theoretic boundary separating memorization from true generalization based on mutual information. The findings reveal that successful generation requires operating precisely at this phase transition edge, providing fundamental insights into deep learning's capacity limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous, information-theoretic lens (related to complexity measures used in statistical physics) to analyze generalization failure modes in modern deep learning models, connecting concepts of phase transitions to AI performance.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental mystery of how diffusion models generalize from finite data without memorizing the training set in high-dimensional spaces.

**Main result.** It identifies an information-theoretic phase boundary determining when a model transitions between memorization and generalization, showing that optimal generation occurs near this boundary.

**Method.** The authors introduce Bayesian Information Restricted Diffusion (BIRD) models and derive theoretical criteria based on mutual information to characterize the transition point.

**Model / system.** The core system is the BIRD model, which restricts pixel observations ($C_{x,t}$) during diffusion reversal. The analysis compares this analytical framework against empirical training of standard neural architectures like UNets and DiTs.

**Key observables.** Mutual Information ($	ext{MI}$), $r^2$ correlation coefficient, critical patch scale ($L_c(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Amount of training data, time/noise level in the reverse process ($t$), and amount of information restriction (patch size $L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical criterion relies on mild assumptions about the underlying data distribution; local approximations are used to model complex architectures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing the transition boundary between memorization and generalization, and show quantitative comparisons of analytical models against early-training results for various datasets.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the problem (generalization mystery) to introducing the BIRD model framework. It then develops the information-theoretic criterion for the phase transition, validates this theory by comparing it to empirical training data across multiple architectures, and concludes by showing that optimal generation tracks this theoretical boundary.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

How diffusion models circumvent the curse of dimensionality to learn complex distributions over high dimensional spaces from a finite training set, instead of memorizing it, remains a fundamental mystery. To address this, we introduce analytically tractable Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models, in which each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data. A BIRD model time-reverses diffusion by inferring which past training sample produced its current restricted observation using the Bayesian posterior. This model class generalizes existing analytical diffusion models that use spatially local information restriction. We show that spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models \textit{early in training}, across different architectures such as UNets and DiTs. Under minimal assumptions on the data distribution, we identify an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization in the joint space of amount of training data, time in the reverse generative process, and amount of information restriction: a BIRD model memorizes when the mutual information between its restricted noisy observations and the training data exceeds the log number of training points, and it generalizes otherwise. Experiments across a range of datasets confirm our theoretically predicted location for the transition. We find that generation proceeds near the edge of memorization: both spatially local BIRD models and early-training diffusion models track the memorization-generalization phase boundary by increasingly restricting information over time. Overall, our results reveal a fundamental role for information restriction in generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

</details>

### [Renormalization flows for 1D mixed states and a quantum Goursat lemma](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08568v1)

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops an algebraic framework to analyze how physical systems evolve under renormalization group coarse-graining. It shows that the resulting fixed points are mathematically described by finite $*$-quantum hypergroups, generalizing classical classification theorems like Goursat's Lemma to the context of $C^*$-Hopf algebras relevant to quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical framework connecting topological order, renormalization group flow, and abstract algebra (Hopf algebras), which is crucial for understanding the structure of ground states in strongly correlated quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide an algebraic description for renormalization flows applied to 1D mixed states, culminating in a quantum generalization of Goursat's lemma.

**Main result.** Renormalization fixed points are characterized by finite $*$-quantum hypergroups, and the structure of these hypergroups within tensor products is classified analogously to classical group theory via a quantum Goursat's Lemma.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced algebraic techniques involving $C^*$-Hopf algebras, completely positive maps, and coarse-graining procedures derived from renormalization group theory.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on boundary states of non-chiral two-dimensional topologically ordered models, modeled using matrix product density operators (MPDOs) describing 1D mixed quantum spin chains.

**Key observables.** Renormalization fixed points, finite $*$-quantum hypergroups, and the structure constants derived from the Hopf algebra representations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is restricted to finite-dimensional $C^*$-Hopf algebras; convergence depends on the noise channels applied during coarse-graining.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the renormalization flow can be rigorously described by completely positive maps and that convergent trajectories lead to idempotent states defining fixed points.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from characterizing the RG flow dynamics using $C^*$-Hopf algebras (Section 4), specializing the classification for finite group algebras using classical Goursat's Lemma (Section 5), and finally generalizing this structural result to arbitrary $C^*$-Hopf algebras (Theorem 6.1).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Renormalization provides a framework for relating microscopic models of physical systems to effective descriptions at larger length scales. This procedure is studied for the boundary states of non-chiral two-dimensional topologically ordered models. The initial data consist of renormalization fixed points built from representations of finite-dimensional $C^*$-Hopf algebras, which are then perturbed by uniform on-site noise quantum channels and repeatedly coarse-grained. The resulting flows admit an intrinsic algebraic description in terms of completely positive maps on the $C^*$-Hopf algebra or, equivalently, positive linear functionals on its enveloping $C^*$-Hopf algebra. Their iteration is governed by convolution powers, and convergent trajectories yield new matrix product density operator fixed points, described by finite $*$-quantum hypergroups. This provides a concrete physical interpretation of such structures. For finite group algebras and their duals, we provide explicit classifications via Goursat's lemma for groups. Finally, we formulate and prove a quantum generalization of Goursat's lemma for finite-dimensional $C^*$-Hopf algebras, a result of independent interest, which gives an explicit structural description of all convergent renormalization trajectories.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops and optimizes fast quantum gates for fluxonium qubits using the Landau-Zener-Stückelberg-Majorana (LZSM) protocol. It rigorously models open system dynamics by incorporating multi-level effects and dissipation via a Floquet-Born-Markov master equation. The key finding is that minimizing gate error requires careful tuning of driving parameters to suppress leakage, leading to high-fidelity gates in the strong driving regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the practical limitations of superconducting qubits by providing rigorous theoretical tools (FBM master equation) to analyze gate errors beyond simple RWA, which is crucial for scaling quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is optimizing fast, high-fidelity quantum gates for low-frequency qubits while overcoming limitations imposed by standard resonant driving and accounting for multi-level structure and dissipation.

**Main result.** LZSM protocols provide a superior method for achieving fast gate operations in fluxonium circuits. The fidelity optimization shows that leakage errors dominate the error budget, suggesting mitigation through specific driving frequency choices.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced theoretical techniques including Landau-Zener-Stückelberg-Majorana (LZSM) driving protocols, Floquet theory, and deriving time-dependent master equations to model open-system dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting fluxonium circuit. The analysis moves from the full Hamiltonian to effective two-level systems using techniques like bare truncation and the Floquet-Schrieffer-Wolf (FSW) Hamiltonian, culminating in a Floquet-Born-Markov master equation for open dynamics.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($ar{F}$), transition probability ($P_{01}$), leakage rate ($L$), and total gate time ($t_g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving parameters include drive frequency ($\omega_d$) and amplitude ($A$); system parameters involve superconducting energies ($E_J, E_C, L_L$). The strong driving regime is of particular interest.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include the Floquet-Born-Markov (FBM) master equation framework and using a 'moderated rotating-wave approximation' to simplify the time-dependent Liouvillian super-operator.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate LZSM dynamics in ideal TLS, parameter space mapping for gate operations ($P_{01}=1/2$), and comparisons between calculated gate error (including leakage) versus optimized results across various driving parameters ($
u$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from analyzing the basic LZSM protocol in an idealized two-level system to incorporating multi-level structure via FSW Hamiltonians, and finally developing a robust formalism using the Floquet-Born-Markov master equation to quantify open-system performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum gates based on resonant Rabi oscillations are inherently slow for small-frequency qubits. They are also prone to errors due to counter-rotating terms. However, when the anharmonicity is sufficiently high, as in the fluxonium architecture, alternative manipulation protocols can outperform standard resonant driving. In this work, we implement fast, high-fidelity quantum gates based on a one-period Landau-Zener-Stückelberg-Majorana (LZSM) driving protocol. We derive analytical expressions that simplify the exploration of the parameter space while accounting for the multi-level structure of the circuit. Furthermore, we analyze the role of leakage, discussing strategies to mitigate it and identifying regimes in which it becomes the dominant source of error. Finally, to evaluate the impact of dissipation on gate fidelity, we develop a robust formalism suitable for analyzing the open-system performance of quantum gates in the strong driving regime.

</details>

**Authors:** Elliott Tammaro, Sriram Gundla, Arunabh Pratik, Shyla Rathore

**Summary.** This paper proposes a method to connect classical particle trajectories to quantum processes by matching their emitted radiation power spectra. Instead of using guidance equations like Bohmian mechanics, the authors calculate the spectrum from classical point charges and fit parameters until they reproduce known quantum spectral signatures. This offers a potentially less problematic theoretical avenue for understanding trajectory concepts in quantum physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This approach offers an alternative theoretical framework to interpret quantum dynamics by grounding it in classical electrodynamics spectra, which could provide novel insights into semiclassical approximations or hidden variables theories without invoking explicit non-locality.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the conceptual challenge of associating classical particle trajectories with quantum mechanical processes, an issue that Bohmian mechanics tackles but introduces non-locality conflicts.

**Main result.** The authors propose a method to find 'equivalent spectrum' classical trajectories whose radiated power spectra match those derived from known quantum transitions, bypassing issues associated with standard guidance equations.

**Method.** They use the Liénard-Wiechert potentials to calculate the radiation power spectrum generated by a point charge moving along an arbitrary trajectory and then fit parameters to match target quantum frequency distributions.

**Model / system.** The model involves calculating electromagnetic fields ($\mathbf{E}$ and $\mathbf{B}$) from a classical point charge trajectory, using atomic transitions as the target quantum processes for spectral matching.

**Key observables.** The power spectrum of emitted radiation, $P(\omega)$, which is proportional to $|	ilde{\mathbf{E}}(\omega)|^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters defining the family of classical trajectories being fitted.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that a classical trajectory can be characterized solely by matching its radiated spectrum to a quantum process's known spectrum, rather than deriving it from a guiding equation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of trajectory association, details the spectral calculation using Liénard-Wiechert potentials for classical trajectories, presents the 'equivalent spectrum' matching technique, and concludes by discussing future work on deriving a governing differential equation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum mechanics abandoned the classical notion of a particle trajectory, yet trajectories remain conceptually appealing for resolving foundational issues in quantum mechanics. Bohmian mechanics offers one route to associating trajectories with quantum processes, but it requires explicit non-locality to remain consistent with EPR-type experiments, placing it in tension with special relativity. We propose an alternative approach: rather than deriving trajectories from a quantum guidance equation, we search for classical trajectories whose radiated frequency spectra match those of quantum processes such as atomic transitions. Using the Liénard-Wiechert potentials, we compute the electric and magnetic fields generated by a point charge along a given trajectory, numerically solving the retarded-time and from these fields obtain the power spectrum of emitted radiation. By fitting the parameters of a chosen family of trajectories, we identify "equivalent spectrum" trajectories that reproduce a target frequency distribution, including ones of quantum mechanical origin. We discuss the implications of this method and propose future work to determine whether equivalent spectrum trajectories belong to a family governed by a differential equation, which would constitute a quantum-equivalent equation of motion.

</details>

### [Fluctuation theorems for autonomous work](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07843v1)

**Authors:** Christopher Jarzynski, Sebastian Deffner, Saar Rahav  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.07843v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper develops fluctuation theorems for work performed when a system interacts with a dedicated work source rather than an externally controlled parameter. By modeling the interaction using Hamiltonian dynamics involving a heat bath, the authors derive autonomous relations that generalize previous non-autonomous results. This provides a more fundamental understanding of energy dissipation and irreversibility in closed quantum or classical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous theoretical tools to analyze non-equilibrium processes (like work extraction) in closed or semi-closed systems by explicitly treating the energy exchange mechanism between components, which is crucial for understanding thermodynamics beyond simple external driving protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to derive fluctuation theorems for work performed in an autonomous setting, contrasting with existing results derived from non-autonomous frameworks.

**Main result.** Autonomous versions of standard fluctuation theorems for work and entropy production are obtained, showing that the theorem reduces to the non-autonomous case when the work source's inertia becomes infinite.

**Method.** The derivation uses Hamiltonian dynamics (or stochastic extensions) on a composite system ($RST$) and relies on analyzing probability distributions along deterministic trajectories to establish fluctuation relations.

**Model / system.** The model consists of a System of Interest ($S$), coupled to a reversible Work Source ($R$), which itself interacts with an explicit Heat Bath ($T$). The dynamics are governed by the total Hamiltonian $H_{RST}$.

**Key observables.** Autonomous Work ($W$), Free Energy Change ($\Delta F$), Stochastic Entropy Production ($\Delta \phi$), and Total Entropy Production ($\Sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta^{-1}$), System/Source masses ($M$), and the limit of infinite inertia for $R$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between inclusive and exclusive conventions for energy counting, and in some derivations, assumes specific initial equilibrium distributions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the conceptual difference between autonomous work (energy exchange between fluid S and flywheel R) and non-autonomous work (external parameter variation). Figure 2 shows a toy model setup coupling S to R.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the Hamiltonian framework for $RST$, deriving conditional equilibrium states, defining various forms of work and entropy production based on conventions, and finally deriving the autonomous fluctuation theorems using these definitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical fluctuation theorems for work have been obtained theoretically, and verified experimentally, within a non-autonomous framework in which work is performed on a system of interest, ${\cal S}$, by the external manipulation of a work parameter, such as a piston's position. Here we obtain fluctuation theorems within an autonomous framework in which ${\cal S}$ exchanges energy with a reversible work source, ${\cal R}$. The two subsystems, ${\cal R}$ and ${\cal S}$, interact with one another as they evolve under Hamiltonian or stochastic dynamics, without external intervention. In this setting, we must account for the back-action of ${\cal S}$ on ${\cal R}$, which is absent in the non-autonomous setting. We obtain autonomous versions of standard fluctuation theorems for work and entropy production. In each case, we argue, the autonomous fluctuation theorem reduces to its non-autonomous counterpart when ${\cal R}$'s inertia becomes infinitely large.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theory for novel quantum $f$-divergences based on 'quantum hockey stick divergences.' It unifies various results by connecting these measures to classical hypothesis testing error probabilities and establishing relationships between different forms of Rényi divergences. The work extends these powerful mathematical tools from finite dimensions to the abstract setting of general von Neumann algebras.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly mathematical, the generalization of divergence measures (like relative entropy) is fundamental for quantifying distinguishability between quantum states, which is crucial in understanding open quantum systems dynamics and process characterization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide a systematic and unified treatment of quantum $f$-divergences, particularly those derived from 'quantum hockey stick divergences.'

**Main result.** Key results include representing these divergences using Neyman-Pearson error probabilities, extending detection criteria for quantum channel reversibility, and establishing connections between regularized Rényi $\alpha$-divergences and Petz/sandwiched Rényi divergences.

**Method.** The work employs advanced mathematical tools from measure theory, functional analysis (convexity/concavity properties), and operator algebras to generalize classical $f$-divergence concepts to quantum states.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to quantum states described by density operators ($\varrho, \sigma$) on a Hilbert space, with extensions formulated for general von Neumann algebras.

**Key observables.** Quantum $f$-divergences (measured and maximal), hockey stick $f$-divergences, and their representations in terms of Neyman-Pearson error probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves a real parameter $\alpha$ for Rényi divergences, and the theory is developed across finite dimensions up to general von Neumann algebras.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mathematical derivations rely on properties like positive homogeneity, existence of derivatives (left/right), and assumptions about the underlying measure spaces ($\nu \ll \mu$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses logically from reviewing classical $f$-divergences to tackling finite-dimensional quantum divergences using hockey stick methods, extending this theory to general von Neumann algebras, and finally analyzing regularized Rényi $\alpha$-divergences.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper we give a systematic and unified treatment and extensions of various results on a new notion of quantum $f$-divergences defined from quantum hockey stick divergences, the theory of which has been developed recently in \cite{BHT_fdiv,HircheTomamichel_integral,LiuHircheCheng2025}. In particular, we consider non-normalized states and hockey stick $f$-divergences defined from more general notions of quantum hockey stick divergences, as well as a somewhat more general form of the integral representation defined in terms of an additional real parameter. We also consider the extension of the theory to general von Neumann algebras, and extend various results from \cite{HircheTomamichel_integral,LiuHircheCheng2025} to this setting. Our main results here are the representation of the hockey stick $f$-divergences in terms of Neyman-Pearson error probabilities, which was given in the finite-dimensional case in \cite{LiuHircheCheng2025}, an extension of Jen\v cová's result \cite{Jencova2023} on the detection of reversibility of a quantum channel on a pair of states in terms of the hockey stick divergences, and an extension of a result in \cite{HircheTomamichel_integral} showing that the regularized hockey stick Rényi $α$-divergences coincide with the Petz-type Rényi divergences for $α\in(0,1)$ and with the sandwiched Rényi divergences for $α>1$. Moreover, we give some partial results on the characterization of when different notions of quantum $f$-divergences give the same value on a pair of quantum states.

</details>

Dash-dotted lines show a simple heuristic fit, which capture both the small and large noise limits, i.e. the fitting function is a/S + b log(S) + d where a, b, d are free parameters, with a tending to a constant at large-p and b ∼1/p.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the performance of QAOA on the SK spin glass model using a semiclassical approximation. The research finds that while the algorithm converges towards the known Parisi value, it does not demonstrate a clear quantum advantage over classical scaling predictions. The study highlights the critical role of initial noise and parameter optimization in determining convergence rates.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides detailed insights into the limitations of quantum algorithms like QAOA when applied to complex disordered systems, offering benchmarks for understanding where quantum computation might fail or converge classically.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) provides a quantum advantage over classical methods for optimizing spin glass models.

**Main result.** The analysis suggests that QAOA does not exhibit a true quantum advantage on SK spin glasses, with convergence rates matching theoretical expectations derived from semiclassical approximations.

**Method.** The authors employ the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA) to perform a semiclassical analysis of QAOA applied to the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick model.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) spin glass model, characterized by all-to-all Gaussian random couplings. The optimization process uses the QAOA protocol depth $p$.

**Key observables.** Final expected energy $\langle E angle$, residual error $\delta = \langle H_c angle/N - E_P$, and spin fluctuations ($\sigma_z$-magnetization).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($p$), effective spin parameter ($S$), and the Parisi value ($E_P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA), which treats quantum dynamics semiclassically.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-monotonic dependence of final energy on $S$ for fixed depths, and plot the residual error $\delta(S, p)$ comparing exact quantum results to semiclassical fits across varying circuit depths $p$.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes QAOA performance by first examining the scaling behavior of the residual error $\delta$ with respect to the effective spin parameter $S$, establishing optimal intermediate regimes. It then compares these findings against established theoretical limits, concluding that convergence follows a $\log(p)/p$ pattern.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We perform a semiclassical, large-spin S, analysis of the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) on the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) model, using the truncated Wigner approximation. Fixing the QAOA angles to their previously determined optimal S=1/2 values, we observe a non-monotonic dependence of the final energy on the spin. At small S the semiclassics is dominated by noise, while the large-S limit is constrained by the exponential growth of the initial fluctuations. For a depth-p QAOA one achieves the optimal balance at S of order p, resulting in a convergence of the final energy to the Parisi value like log(p)/p. We find that the semiclassics slightly outperforms the true spin-1/2 QAOA, and thus suggest they both converge to the Parisi value in the same way. Finally, removing all the initial noise, and re-optimizing the parameters to account for that change, results in superior performance with 1/p convergence.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an advanced Functional Renormalization Group (FRG) technique to treat correlated electron models that explicitly include electron-phonon coupling. By unifying these interactions, the method allows for a comprehensive analysis of competing electronic instabilities—such as charge density waves or superconductivity—in systems like the Hubbard model on a square lattice. It represents a significant theoretical advance for understanding emergent order in strongly coupled quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter correlations, the use of advanced many-body techniques like FRG for coupled quantum systems provides methodological insights applicable to open quantum systems where coupling to a bosonic bath (phonons/environment) is crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to formulate a unified theoretical framework for correlated electron systems that treats electronic interactions and electron-phonon coupling (EPC) on equal footing when analyzing Fermi liquid instabilities.

**Main result.** The functional renormalization group (FRG) approach successfully analyzes the competition between various electronic orders (charge, spin, nematicity, superconductivity) driven by both electronic correlations and EPC in a single formalism.

**Method.** The core method is the Functional Renormalization Group (FRG), which allows for the simultaneous treatment of electronic and phononic degrees of freedom by integrating out phonons to derive retarded interactions.

**Model / system.** The primary model analyzed is the Hubbard model on a square lattice coupled to acoustic phonon bands. The Hamiltonian includes terms for electron hopping, on-site repulsion (Hubbard U), and EPC derived from interatomic force constants.

**Key observables.** Electronic order parameters related to charge, spin, nematicity, and superconducting pairing; phonon softening signatures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Hubbard interaction strength (U), the electron-phonon coupling constant (g), and lattice geometry/dispersion relations ($\omega_{n,\mathbf{q}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial treatment assumes the harmonic approximation for phonons, and some derivations simplify by ignoring the renormalization of the retarded interaction ($V_{R,\Lambda}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures (Hubbard model), schematic representations of EPC from lattice perturbations, and flow diagrams showing how the static vertex renormalizes across different coupling channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the FRG ansatz for coupled electron-phonon systems, derives the necessary retarded interactions by integrating out phonons, analyzes the renormalization flow of the two-particle vertex in various channels (P, C, D), and applies this to the Hubbard model example.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate a functional renormalization group (FRG) ansatz for correlated electron models that incorporates electronic interactions as well as electron-phonon coupling (EPC) stemming from dispersive phonon bands. Particularizing to the RG flow of the electron-electron interaction vertex, we treat phonon- and electron-mediated Fermi liquid instabilities on equal footing as we analyze tentative electronic order parameters related to charge, spin, nematicity, and superconducting pairing. We illustrate the approach at the example of Peierls-type transitions we find for the Hubbard model on the square lattice coupled to acoustic phonon bands. Our method allows to incorporate full electronic and phononic ab initio input, and thus lends itself to the analysis of electronic order from intertwined electronic interactions and EPC at a microscopically most substantiated level.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theory of Magic Gate Teleportation (MGT) protocols necessary for fault-tolerant quantum computing. It characterizes the required structure of resource states and proves that these states are highly constrained. Furthermore, it offers significant insights into simplifying the complex feedforward operations needed in these protocols by identifying conditions where they can be replaced by simpler Pauli operators.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep theoretical constraints on the resources needed for fault-tolerant quantum computation, specifically detailing how non-Clifford gates can be implemented while maintaining information privacy, which is crucial for resource optimization in physical implementations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a theoretical framework for Magic Gate Teleportation (MGT) protocols to implement non-Clifford quantum gates on arbitrary input states without revealing any information about those states.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that useful resource states for MGT must be Clifford-equivalent to diagonal states, and it provides structural insights into how these protocols can be efficiently synthesized or simplified using Pauli operators.

**Method.** The authors analyze the structure of MGT by backpropagating Pauli measurements, viewing the process as encoding the input state into a stabilizer code followed by a logical gate. They derive necessary conditions for information preservation and provide construction algorithms.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum gate teleportation using resource states ($\vert\etaangle$) to implement non-Clifford unitaries ($U_0$). The process relies on Clifford gates, Pauli Z measurements, and subsequent feedforward operations.

**Key observables.** The output state $ho_{out}$, the structure of useful resource states (e.g., being Clifford-equivalent to diagonal states), and the feasibility of replacing non-Clifford feedforward operators with Pauli operators.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The specific form of the resource state $\vert\etaangle$, the input state $ho_{in}$, and the measurement outcomes $m$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the input state $ho_{in}$ must remain recoverable (no information revealed) throughout the teleportation process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate MGT protocols, showing how non-stabilizer states can implement gates via Pauli measurements and feedforward. They also demonstrate the structural view of MGT as encoding into a stabilizer code and show examples where complex feedforward operations can be simplified to Pauli operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first defining the protocol, then deriving structural theorems constraining useful resource states (e.g., Theorem 2 for single qubits). It progresses to analyzing deterministic implementation via feedforward operators ($F_m$) and concludes by identifying conditions under which these complex operations can be simplified using only Pauli operators.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum gate teleportation is a key technique in fault-tolerant quantum computation that uses resource states to implement logical gates. Here, we develop a theory of quantum gate teleportation protocols that implement non-Clifford gates on arbitrary input states without revealing any information about them; we refer to these protocols as magic gate teleportation (MGT). We uncover a hidden structure within MGT -- after backpropagating the Pauli measurements, MGT protocols can be viewed as encoding the input state into a stabilizer code heralded by the measurement outcomes, followed by a logical non-Clifford gate. Using this structure, we construct MGT protocols for any resource state obtained by applying commuting Pauli rotations to a stabilizer state, and provide an efficient algorithm for synthesizing their circuit implementations. Conversely, we prove that useful resource states for MGT, i.e., states that can be used for non-Clifford gates through MGT protocols, are necessarily Clifford-equivalent to diagonal states; in particular, the output state distilled from the $[\![5, 1, 3]\!]$ protocol is not useful for MGT. Finally, we identify conditions under which the feedforward operators can be implemented by Pauli operators, shedding light on the paradigm of algorithmic fault tolerance and simplifying the feedforward operations needed for quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines the magnetic control over electron wave packet dynamics (GH shift and GDT) in monolayer WSe2 through a magnetic barrier. By solving an effective Hamiltonian, the authors demonstrate that external magnetic fields induce strong oscillations and spin/valley selectivity in these transport properties. This offers a promising pathway for designing highly controllable nanoscale devices for spintronics and valleytronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to selectively manipulate spin and valley degrees of freedom via external fields in a 2D material is highly relevant for developing next-generation spintronic and valleytronic devices, connecting fundamental quantum transport theory to solid-state engineering.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how an external magnetic field influences the Goos-Hänchen (GH) shift and group delay time (GDT) in monolayer WSe2 when a magnetic barrier is present.

**Main result.** The magnetic barrier strongly modulates both the GH shift and GDT, enabling selective control over the lateral shift and traversal time for individual spin and valley channels. This suggests a mechanism for tunable spatial and temporal separation of electronic wave packets.

**Method.** Transport properties are calculated by solving an effective low-energy continuum Hamiltonian and extracting the GH shift and GDT from the phase of the transmission coefficient.

**Model / system.** The system is monolayer WSe2 modeled using an effective low-energy Hamiltonian that incorporates spin-orbit coupling, Zeeman effects, and a magnetic barrier potential. The analysis treats charge carriers as Dirac-like fermions propagating through regions defined by this magnetic structure.

**Key observables.** Goos–Hänchen (GH) shift, Group Delay Time (GDT), transmission amplitudes, reflection/transmission probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength ($B$), incident energy ($E$), angle of incidence ($\phi$), and barrier width ($D$ or $d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model uses an effective continuum approach; the magnetic field is realized via ferromagnetic strips creating a defined barrier region.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of normalized group delay time on the magnetic field for various incident energies and angles, showing oscillatory behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using an effective Hamiltonian, derives transmission coefficients by solving boundary value problems across pristine and barrier regions, and finally analyzes the phase-dependent observables (GH shift and GDT) to demonstrate magnetic control.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the influence of an external magnetic field on the Goos-Hänchen (GH) shift and the group delay time (GDT) in monolayer WSe$_2$ in the presence of a magnetic barrier. The transport properties of Dirac-like carriers are obtained by solving the effective low-energy Hamiltonian and evaluating the corresponding transmission amplitudes. The GH shift and the GDT are subsequently extracted from the phase of the transmission coefficient. We systematically analyze their dependence on the magnetic field strength, incident energy, angle of incidence, and barrier width, with particular emphasis on the spin and valley degrees of freedom associated with the $K$ and $K'$ valleys. Our results show that the magnetic barrier strongly modulates both the GH shift and the GDT, leading to oscillatory behavior and pronounced spin-valley-dependent transport characteristics. Remarkably, the magnetic field enables selective control of the lateral shift and traversal time of carriers for each spin and valley channel, allowing for tunable spatial and temporal separation of electronic wave packets. This provides a mechanism for manipulating fermionic trajectories after transmission through the barrier in a highly controllable manner. Such tunability opens promising avenues for designing nanoscale devices based on spin and valley filtering, as well as for potential applications in information storage and processing within spintronic and valleytronic platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an agent-driven workflow using specialized LLMs to autoformalize advanced theorems in theoretical physics, demonstrated on Tensor Network Theory. By applying this system to the fundamental theorem of MPS and 1D topological phases, the authors showcase a powerful methodology for machine-checking complex physical mathematics. This effort establishes new computational libraries (TNLean) and highlights that controlling mathematical intent remains the key challenge in scaling such AI formalization efforts.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a blueprint for how AI can tackle foundational theoretical physics problems by automating rigorous mathematical proof construction in systems like Lean, which is highly relevant for developing formal quantum field theory or condensed matter models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is the automated, research-level formalization of complex theorems from theoretical physics, specifically focusing on Tensor Network Theory.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated an agent-driven workflow that autoformalized the fundamental theorem of matrix-product states (FT-MPS) and extended it to classify 1D symmetry-protected topological phases.

**Method.** A multi-agent LLM system coordinates specialized agents using a structured mathematical blueprint, executing formal proofs within the Lean 4 proof assistant for machine verification.

**Model / system.** The theory centers on Matrix-Product States (MPS) used to describe quantum states on one-dimensional chains. The formalism connects these states to topological invariants derived from group representations.

**Key observables.** Topological invariant $[\omega] \in H^2(G, C^	imes)$, the MPS state $|\psi_N(A)angle$, and the structure of the transfer operator $E_A$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bond dimension ($D$), chain length ($N$), and the nature of the symmetry group $G$ are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The main bottleneck identified is enforcing correct mathematical intent during autoformalization; furthermore, reliance on primary literature sources was necessary for full autonomy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate multi-layered consistency checks across literature, blueprints, and Lean code. Another figure shows the flow from FT-MPS to SPT phase classification via a virtual-layer gauge identity.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the agent workflow (Blueprint $\leftrightarrow$ Lean), presents the formalization of FT-MPS (relating tensors via gauge transformations), extends this to 1D SPT phases, and concludes with a discussion on process limitations and infrastructure release.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We build a team of specialized large language-model agents and present an agent-driven workflow for research-level formalization in theoretical physics, with the autoformalization of the fundamental theorem of matrix-product states as a demonstration. The agents, coordinated through a structured mathematical blueprint and periodic human review, orchestrated and executed the full formalization autonomously. For some statements, the agents were able to explore new proof routes that are not part of the standard literature. Along the way the agents produced extensive tensor-network and quantum-information libraries not previously available in Mathlib, Lean's mathematical library. As a physical application, the formalization also extends towards symmetry-protected topological phases in one dimension. We find that the main bottleneck in large-scale autoformalization is enforcing mathematical intent and we provide a detailed study of the full process and various subtleties involved. We release the codebase as the library \href{https://github.com/LionSR/TNLean}{TNLean}, together with a \nChapters{}-chapter \href{https://lionsr.github.io/TNLean/blueprint/}{blueprint} of the formalization effort.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents Plaquette, a novel software framework that allows researchers to calculate the true logical performance of fault-tolerant quantum computers directly from detailed physical noise models. By accepting general quantum channels derived from hardware imperfections (like leakage or heating), it moves beyond simplified Pauli approximations. This provides essential guidance for setting realistic error budgets and designing viable FTQC architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it bridges the gap between abstract QEC theory and messy, real-world open quantum system physics encountered in AMO/quantum optics experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the true logical performance of Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computers (FTQCs) when subjected to realistic, complex hardware noise that deviates from simple Pauli models.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Plaquette, a framework that provides a direct path from specifying open-system physics (as quantum channels) to calculating the logical error rates of an FTQC built on it.

**Method.** Plaquette accepts hardware noise models specified as Kraus operators or Lindblad dynamics and compiles them for various samplers: stabilizer sampling, XPauli sampler, near-Clifford samplers, and full-state simulation.

**Model / system.** The framework is designed to model physical imperfections across multiple platforms, including leakage in superconducting transmons, intermediate-state scattering in neutral atoms, and heating in trapped ions. The noise is generally modeled using CPTP quantum channels.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates, threshold surfaces, error budgets, and overhead estimates for fault tolerance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The vector of physical parameters ($\eta$) defining the hardware noise (e.g., decay rates, detunings).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework highlights that relying only on Clifford-only simulations can lead to overly optimistic and inaccurate estimates compared to full channel analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 summarizes the Plaquette workflow: specifying device physics as a channel, compiling it for sampling, and outputting logical error rates. Table I details Plaquette's capabilities across codes, error models, and platforms.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the limitations of current noise models, introduces the Plaquette framework to accept general quantum channels, validates specialized samplers (XPauli, near-Clifford) against full simulation, and demonstrates its utility on three distinct physical hardware platforms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hardware teams building fault-tolerant quantum computers (FTQCs) must decide which imperfections to suppress, and that decision requires the logical performance of the architecture under the device's actual noise. Hardware noise often departs from the stochastic Pauli models used by scalable stabilizer simulators: superconducting transmons leak out of the computational subspace, neutral atoms scatter through intermediate states, trapped ions heat as their motional modes absorb phonons, and miscalibrated controls over-rotate coherently. We present Plaquette, a theoretical framework and software suite that computes the logical performance of fault-tolerant architectures directly from the physics of such imperfections. In Plaquette, a hardware error model is specified once, as Kraus operators, Hamiltonian-Lindblad dynamics, or an experimentally reconstructed quantum channel, and is compiled automatically into the exact or approximate representation required by each of four sampler classes: stabilizer sampling for Pauli noise, the new XPauli sampler for leakage and environment sectors, near-Clifford samplers for coherent errors, and full-state simulation for exact reference calculations. We validate the XPauli and near-Clifford samplers against full-state simulation, which they can match within statistical uncertainty while Pauli twirling can fall short depending on the error model. We demonstrate the framework on three error models: leakage in superconducting qubits, intermediate-state scattering in neutral atoms, and heating in trapped ions. The size of the discrepancy between Plaquette and Clifford-only simulations varies with platform and noise process, so reliable thresholds, error budgets, and overhead estimates require the most accurate simulation available. Plaquette provides a direct path from the open-system physics of a device to the logical performance of the FTQC built on it.

</details>

**Authors:** Minbo Gao, Chenghua Liu, Guangxu Yang, Tianyi Zhang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08517v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

A valid output is a triangle {x, C(i), Fi(x)} for which the corresponding XY edge is present, i.e. Ax,C(i) = 1.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This work develops a novel matrix discrepancy technique to derive lower bounds for one-way quantum communication required for search problems. By applying this method to collision finding and triangle finding, the authors establish new, tight quantum lower bounds surpassing previous results. This advances the understanding of quantum computational limits for fundamental combinatorial tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on communication complexity, the rigorous mathematical techniques involving operator norms, PSD matrices, and concentration inequalities are highly relevant tools used in quantum information theory that can inform analyses of open quantum systems or resource theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses establishing one-way quantum communication lower bounds for search problems, which are fundamentally more complex than decision problems due to their multiple valid outputs.

**Main result.** They establish a tight $\Omega(N^{1/4})$ one-way quantum communication lower bound for collision finding and derive an $\Omega(\sqrt{\Delta V})$ quantum streaming space lower bound for triangle finding in graphs.

**Method.** The core methodology involves developing a novel technique based on matrix discrepancy to analyze the success probability of quantum protocols by bounding associated Positive Operator-Valued Measures (POVMs) as PSD packings.

**Model / system.** The analysis is set within the model of one-way quantum communication, where Alice sends a state $ho_a$ and Bob measures using a POVM $\{M(b)_z\}_{z\in Z}$. The applications involve theoretical search problems like collision finding and triangle finding in graph streams.

**Key observables.** Success probability ($	ext{psucc}$), quantum communication complexity ($k$), and streaming space requirements ($S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the input set ($N$), the number of edges/triangles, and structural parameters like $\Delta_V$ (max triangles per vertex).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on decomposing validity indicators into factors leading to PSD operators that must satisfy packing constraints; it also uses matrix concentration arguments.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of search lower bounds, develops the matrix discrepancy technique by analyzing POVMs as PSD packings, and applies this method sequentially to derive specific lower bounds for collision finding and triangle finding.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study one-way quantum communication lower bounds for search problems. Unlike decision problems, search problems can have many valid outputs, which pose a fundamental barrier to standard quantum lower-bound techniques. We overcome this by developing a novel method based on matrix discrepancy, which allows us to bound the output measurements of a quantum protocol jointly.   As applications of our method, we establish the first tight quantum lower bounds for two fundamental search problems in some natural parameter regimes: collision finding and triangle finding. For collision finding, we prove a tight $Ω(N^{1/4})$ one-way quantum communication lower bound. Previously, the best-known quantum communication lower bound for collision finding was $Ω(N^{1/12})$ due to Göös and Jain (RANDOM 2022), and no stronger bound was known even under the one-way restriction. For triangle finding in graph streams, we prove a one-pass quantum streaming space lower bound of $Ω\left(\sqrt{Δ_V}\right)$ for graphs with $m$ edges, $Θ(m)$ triangles, and constant $Δ_E$, where $Δ_V$ and $Δ_E$ denote the maximum number of triangles sharing a common vertex and edge, respectively, under the condition that $1\le Δ_V\le m^{2/3}$. This constitutes the first nontrivial quantum space lower bound in this regime, matching the classical upper bound of Jayaram and Kallaugher (RANDOM 2021) up to logarithmic factors. Notably, our method also recovers the classical lower bound of Kallaugher and Price (SODA 2017) through an entirely different argument, avoiding their Boolean-Hidden-Matching reduction that breaks down for quantum protocols.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical method to detect exciton superfluidity in electron-hole bilayers by monitoring the Anderson-Bogoliubov sound velocity. The authors show that this velocity exhibits a sharp, measurable drop precisely at the BKT transition point. By incorporating finite-temperature screening and RG analysis, they provide an experimentally accessible signature for this neutral quantum phase.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, measurable dynamical signature ($c_s(T)$ collapse) for an elusive quantum phase (exciton superfluidity), bridging complex many-body theory with potential experimental detection methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is obtaining definitive evidence of exciton superfluidity in electrically neutral electron-hole bilayers at zero magnetic field.

**Main result.** The Anderson-Bogoliubov sound velocity provides a measurable signature, showing a discontinuous drop to zero precisely at the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) transition temperature.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using path integrals, Renormalization Group (RG) techniques for phase fluctuations, and solving gap equations incorporating finite-temperature screening.

**Model / system.** The system is an exciton bilayer formed by parallel electron-hole layers. The model spans the BCS-BEC crossover regime, allowing tuning between weakly coupled and strongly coupled superfluid states.

**Key observables.** Anderson-Bogoliubov sound velocity ($c_s(T)$), phase stiffness ($J_0(T)$), compressibility ($\kappa_0(T)$), and the transition temperature ($T_{	ext{BKT}}^{	ext{RG}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Carrier density, temperature (especially near $T_{	ext{BKT}}$), and the ratio of interaction types (contact vs. dipolar).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes low-density regimes where certain contributions (like spin-wave terms) can be neglected or approximated.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of various quantities, such as the maximum gap energy ($\Delta_{\max}$), polarization differences ($\Pi_N - \Pi_A$), and most critically, the sound velocity $c_s(T)$ tracking the superfluid transition.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the diagnostic observable (sound velocity) to developing the theoretical framework (RPA screening, RG flow) necessary to predict the BKT transition in this specific neutral exciton system.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Obtaining definitive evidence of exciton superfluidity in electron-hole bilayers in zero magnetic field remains a major longstanding challenge since the condensate is electrically neutral, making its phase coherence difficult to detect directly. We show that the Anderson-Bogoliubov sound velocity provides a dynamical signature of exciton superfluidity. Across the BCS-BEC crossover, the velocity is known to discontinuously drop to zero at the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) transition. The magnitude of the drop has a strong density dependence. We compute this behavior, with the inclusion of finite-temperature screening, and determine the BKT transition using a renormalization-group approach. We further identify a temperature window which is experimentally accessible, where vortex-antivortex excitations strongly renormalize both the sound velocity and the transition temperature.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes and experimentally demonstrates a robust One-Sided Device-Independent Quantum Key Distribution (1sDI-QKD) protocol utilizing high-dimensional photonic entanglement. By leveraging quantum steering for security certification, the authors show that increasing the dimension enhances key rate robustness against noise and loss. This advances practical quantum communication by providing a scalable, device-independent method.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical realization of information-theoretically secure quantum communication by moving beyond qubit systems to higher dimensions, which inherently improves resilience against realistic noise sources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming practical limitations of Quantum Key Distribution (QKD), such as noise, losses, and device imperfections.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that increasing the dimension ($d$) enhances robustness in One-Sided Device-Independent QKD (1sDI-QKD), achieving positive key rates up to $d=7$ in principle.

**Method.** They develop a systematic security analysis using quantum steering inequalities ($eta > eta_{	ext{LHS}}$) and evaluate achievable secret key rates via reverse reconciliation, complemented by a proof-of-principle experimental setup.

**Model / system.** The protocol uses high-dimensional (HD) entanglement encoded in the transverse-spatial degree-of-freedom of photons. The security analysis is framed around certifying non-local correlations using quantum steering to rule out Local Hidden State models.

**Key observables.** Steering violation ($eta$), secret key rates ($r_\infty$), guessing probability ($P_{	ext{guess}}$), and one-sided detection efficiency ($\eta_{	ext{exp}}$ vs. $\eta_{	ext{req}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimension $d$ (up to 11), visibility ($
u$), noise/loss parameters, and the ratio of observed to required efficiencies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the fair-sampling assumption for Bob's side and explicitly accounts for Alice's no-click events to avoid loopholes. The experimental work assumes ideal components despite measured discrepancies.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare direct vs. reverse reconciliation key rates, show steering detection parameters ($\mu$) versus observed violations, and plot required vs. achieved one-sided detection efficiencies across various dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from theoretical security analysis (using quantum steering to bound key rates) to deriving the advantage of reverse reconciliation in 1sDI-QKD, culminating in a detailed description and results of a proof-of-principle experimental implementation using high-dimensional photonic entanglement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum key distribution (QKD) brings the promise of communication with information-theoretic security but is limited in practice due to its susceptibility to noise, losses, and device imperfections. To address these challenges, we propose a robust high-dimensional (HD) one-sided device-independent QKD (1sDI-QKD) protocol and present a proof-of-principle experimental implementation using photons entangled in the transverse-spatial degree-of-freedom. We develop a systematic security analysis of HD 1sDI-QKD protocols, leveraging quantum steering to certify security, and evaluate achievable secret key rates for different measurement configurations and system dimensions using reverse reconciliation. Our analysis shows that increasing the dimension enhances robustness against both noise and loss. We then demonstrate the key experimental building blocks required for implementing the protocol: (a) a high-quality source of high-dimensional photonic entanglement, and (b) a fully programmable, high-dimensional multi-outcome measurement device operating in up to dimension 11. Using these components, we obtain positive key rates for all investigated dimensions under the fair-sampling assumption, with the highest key rates achieved for dimension d=7. Finally, we discuss the steps required for a practical, loophole-free implementation of 1sDI-QKD in realistic regimes of loss and noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This work elucidates the origin of exciton optical brightness in magnetic van der Waals semiconductors like CrSBr. It demonstrates that excitonic visibility is not solely determined by local character but by a symmetry-enforced interference rule between transition dipoles. Bright states result from constructive superposition, while dark states arise from destructive cancellation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept that optical selection rules are governed by coherent superposition symmetries rather than just orbital composition is highly relevant for understanding light-matter interactions in complex quantum materials and artificial quantum emitters.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is explaining the coexistence of intense 'bright' excitons with much dimmer companion states, despite both originating from the same single-particle electronic manifold.

**Main result.** Brightness is determined by a symmetry-adapted interference rule between transition dipoles: bright states arise from constructive (sublattice-symmetric) superposition, while dark states result from destructive (sublattice-antisymmetric) cancellation.

**Method.** The study employs advanced many-body calculations, specifically the Bethe-Salpeter Equation (BSE) built upon a quasiparticle self-consistent GW framework, to analyze the bare exciton spectrum.

**Model / system.** The system is the magnetic van der Waals semiconductor CrSBr, which possesses an orthorhombic primitive cell containing two Cr atoms. The analysis treats the excitons as composite objects involving ligand-field, intersite, and ligand-assisted components.

**Key observables.** Exciton binding energies (e.g., XA near 1.34 eV), optical oscillator strengths, and the Interference Figure of Merit ($I_{\alpha}^S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sublattice symmetry (symmetric vs. antisymmetric superposition); energy separation between bright/dark partners ($	ext{meV}$ scale).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the *bare* exciton spectrum derived from BSE, which is necessary to isolate the fundamental electronic response before coupling to magnons or phonons.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows that diagonal (sublattice-symmetric) dipole additions lead to bright states, while cross (sublattice-antisymmetric) additions cause destructive cancellation leading to dark states. Figure 2 quantitatively verifies this interference mechanism.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the electronic structure via QSGcW, then uses BSE to analyze exciton character. The core argument develops by introducing the symmetry rule governing dipole interference (the $I_{\alpha}^S$ diagnostic) to distinguish bright from dark states, culminating in a detailed analysis of how this symmetry dictates optical visibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic van der Waals semiconductors such as CrSBr host an intricate exciton landscape whose physical interpretation has converged only recently. A many-body Feynman diagrammatic approach based on quasiparticle self-consistent GW with electron-hole ladder vertex corrections to the screened Coulomb interaction has established the electronic band gap, excitonic orbital character, real-space extent, binding energies, and bosonic-coupling signatures of the bright XA exciton near 1.34 eV and the higher XB manifold near 1.8 eV. These results agree well with ARPES and magneto-optical experiments and supersede the early Rydberg-like assignment of the excitons. What has remained unresolved is why these intense bright excitons coexist, within a few tens of meV, with companion states that are several orders of magnitude darker despite drawing from essentially the same single-particle transition manifold. Here we show that brightness is a band-coherent property of the excitonic eigenfunctions: bright and dark partners are sublattice-symmetric and sublattice-antisymmetric superpositions of the same ligand-field-like Bloch transitions across the two Cr atoms of the orthorhombic primitive cell. The commonly used Frenkel and Wannier-Mott labels describe what an exciton is made of, but brightness requires a symmetry-adapted interference rule between transition dipoles. Disentangling this bare excitonic structure is a prerequisite for interpreting the optical response of CrSBr once magnon, phonon, and photon couplings are included.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that strain induced by an aluminum gate can locally modify the valence band structure of a silicon two-dimensional hole gas. By analyzing Shubnikov-de Haas oscillations, the authors extract multiple cyclotron masses, proving that confinement and strain together dictate the electronic properties. This establishes a powerful method for engineering quantum material behavior using standard semiconductor fabrication techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the ability to use external fields/geometry (gate voltage/thickness) to tune fundamental electronic band structures in solid-state systems is highly relevant for designing quantum devices and controlling spin-orbit coupling effects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate how strain, locally induced by an aluminum gate, modifies the valence band structure of a two-dimensional hole gas (2DHG) in silicon.

**Main result.** The measured cyclotron masses deviate from simple heavy-hole/light-hole predictions, confirming that quantum confinement and strain combine to significantly alter the valence band structure. The technique allows for local engineering of electronic properties using standard semiconductor fabrication methods.

**Method.** The study combines transport measurements (Shubnikov-de Haas oscillations) analyzed via temperature dependence fitting (Lifshitz-Kosevich theory) with theoretical simulations (COMSOL finite-element solver) to model strain effects.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a 2DHG confined at the Si/SiO2 interface. The valence band structure is modeled using concepts like HH-LH mixing and incorporating strain tensors ($\epsilon_b$, $\epsilon_{rot}$) into the Hamiltonian formalism (Bir-Pikus context).

**Key observables.** Cyclotron masses ($m_c^* \approx 0.36 m_0$ and $0.49 m_0$), Shubnikov-de Haas oscillation frequencies/amplitudes, and simulated strain components ($\epsilon_b$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate thickness (20–100 nm) acts as the tuning parameter for strain magnitude; cryogenic temperatures ($T \approx 1.3$ K); magnetic field ($B = 9$ T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting SdH oscillations using the Lifshitz-Kosevich factor, and initial modeling assumes a framework incorporating confinement and strain effects beyond simple bulk approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $\Deltaho_{xx}$ vs $1/B$ and FFT amplitude vs frequency, showing how gate thickness controls the appearance of multiple oscillation peaks ($f_2$). Simulations map out the spatial variation of biaxial strain ($\epsilon_b$) near the gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (Si 2DHG) and the tool (gate-induced strain). It presents transport measurements showing evidence of multiple subbands via SdH oscillations. Theoretical analysis confirms that simulated strains are sufficient to modify band structure on the meV scale, linking theory to experiment.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show the effect of gate-induced strain on the valence band of a silicon (Si) metal oxide semiconductor (MOS) confined two-dimensional hole gas (2DHG). Increasing aluminum gate thickness, and thereby the strain in the channel, results in the onset of a second subband contributing to Shubnikov-de Haas oscillations. Temperature-dependent magnetotransport measurements reveal distinct cyclotron masses of $m_c^*=(0.36\pm0.04)m_0$ and $m_c^*=(0.49\pm0.02)m_0$. The measured cyclotron masses differ from those expected for an idealized heavy-hole (HH)/light-hole (LH) picture, reflecting the combined influence of quantum confinement, strain, and HH-LH mixing on the valence band.

</details>
