**Summary.** This work resolves the mystery of the thermal Hall effect above $T_c$ in cuprates by invoking the parity anomaly from $(2+1)$D QFT. It derives an exact formula linking $\kappa_{xy}/T$ directly to the independently measured pseudogap, $\Delta_{pg}(T)$. The theory is robustly confirmed by multiple numerical simulations and offers a falsifiable test for ARPES data.

**Why it may be interesting.** The paper provides a powerful example of how topological field theory concepts (parity anomaly) can yield exact, parameter-free predictions for transport properties in strongly correlated quantum materials like cuprates.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the discrepancy between theoretical predictions and experimental measurements of the thermal Hall effect ($\kappa_{xy}/T$) in cuprate superconductors within the pseudogap phase, where superconductivity has vanished.

**Main result.** It derives an exact, parameter-free formula for $\kappa_{xy}/T$ based on the parity anomaly of $(2+1)$-dimensional quantum field theory: $\kappa_{xy}/T = rac{\pi^2 k_B^2}{6h} C 	anh\left(rac{\Delta_{pg}(T)}{2k_B T}ight)$.

**Method.** The derivation uses the parity anomaly of a chiral U(1) gauge theory, calculating the induced Chern-Simons term via integrating out massive Nambu fermions.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonians for cuprates exhibiting $p+ip$ pairing. The analysis focuses on the pseudogap regime where $\Delta_{pg}(T)$ is non-zero even when the superconducting order parameter vanishes.

**Key observables.** Thermal Hall conductance ($\kappa_{xy}/T$), Pseudogap magnitude ($\Delta_{pg}(T)$), and the Chern number ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The pseudogap $\Delta_{pg}(T)$, which is directly measurable by ARPES/STM, dictates the temperature dependence via the hyperbolic tangent function.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result relies on the Coleman-Hill non-renormalization theorem protecting the one-loop calculation and assumes the applicability of the parity anomaly mechanism in this condensed matter context.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the phase diagram ($T^*$ vs $T_c$) and shows the logical flow connecting the spectroscopic gap ($\Delta_{pg}(T)$), the topological integer ($C$), and the final formula for $\kappa_{xy}/T$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical puzzle, develops the theoretical framework using parity anomaly calculations in a chiral U(1) gauge theory, derives the exact functional form linking $\kappa_{xy}/T$ to $\Delta_{pg}(T)$, and validates this result through multiple numerical tests (DMRG, Wilson loops).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A large negative thermal Hall signal has been reported across multiple cuprate families in the pseudogap phase where the superconducting order parameter has vanished, with a magnitude that no existing microscopic theory reproduces without free parameters. Competing proposals based on chiral phonons, spinons, or loop currents each require undetermined coupling constants and do not predict the temperature dependence in terms of an independently measured spectroscopic gap. We show that the parity anomaly of $(2+1)$-dimensional quantum field theory resolves this long-standing puzzle: the preformed-pair pseudogap $Δ_{\rm pg}(T)$ enters the parity-odd fermion determinant identically to a condensate mass, yielding the exact parameter-free formula $κ_{xy}/T = (π^2 k_B^2/6h)\,C\,\tanh[Δ_{\rm pg}(T)/(2k_BT)]$, where $C$ is the Chern number of the chiral pairing channel and $Δ_{\rm pg}(T)$ is directly measurable by ARPES or STM. Coleman-Hill non-renormalization protects the result against higher-loop corrections, and two independent numerical tests, Wilson-loop flux threading and DMRG on $p+ip$ cylinders, confirm the anomaly correlation length to $0.2\%$ accuracy with no power-law finite-size corrections. The theory predicts thermal Hall onset at $T^*$ rather than $T_c$, provides a falsifiable logarithmic-derivative test against ARPES data, and yields a concrete quantitative target for magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report highly precise measurements of the $g$-factor ratio for a single $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion by employing two independent experimental setups: a cryogenic RF Paul trap and a room-temperature Penning trap. By comparing results from these dual platforms, they achieved an unprecedented reduction in measurement uncertainty, providing stringent tests of atomic theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a state-of-the-art example of precision measurement in trapped ions, directly testing fundamental atomic structure constants ($g$-factors) using complementary quantum interrogation methods. The comparison between two vastly different physical platforms (RF vs. Penning) is particularly valuable for validating results.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to achieve a high-precision measurement of the ratio of Landé g-factors between the $3^2D_{5/2}$ and $4^2S_{1/2}$ states in a single trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion.

**Main result.** The measurements, performed using two distinct traps (Penning and RF), yielded a highly precise ratio of approximately 0.599488813, reducing the uncertainty by over 40-fold compared to prior work.

**Method.** The experiment employed dual-platform spectroscopy: Rabi/Ramsey measurements in an RF Paul trap and frequency ratios derived from multiple transitions in a Penning trap.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a single trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion. The measurement probes the energy levels of the $S_{1/2}$ and $D_{5/2}$ manifolds, utilizing techniques like Doppler cooling and resolved-sideband cooling.

**Key observables.** The ratio of Landé g-factors: $\frac{g_{3^2D_{5/2}}}{g_{4^2S_{1/2}}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Penning trap field ($\sim 0.91 	ext{ T}$ axial); RF trap field ($0.7 	ext{ mT}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Systematic shifts from various sources (e.g., AC Stark, linear Zeeman drift) were estimated to be smaller than the statistical uncertainty in both measurement platforms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the spectroscopy techniques used in both the Penning and RF traps. Figure 2 shows repeated measurements confirming the final reported value for the g-factor ratio using the Penning trap setup.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental methodology, comparing results from two complementary trapping environments (Penning vs. RF). It presents the derived ratio values, analyzes systematic error mitigation techniques specific to each trap, and concludes by stating the combined high-precision result.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report precision measurements of the ratio of Landé $g$ factors between the $3^2D_{5/2}$ and $4^2S_{1/2}$ states of a single trapped $^{40}\text{Ca}^+$ ion. The measurements are performed in two distinct ion trap apparatus: a cryogenic surface electrode radiofrequency Paul trap and a room-temperature permanent magnet Penning trap. The Penning trap measurements yield a ratio of $0.599~488~813~3(2)$, which represents a more than 40-fold uncertainty reduction compared to previous work. The radiofrequency trap measurement yields a concurring value of $0.599~488~813(6)$. We estimate that systematic shifts for each system are well below the respective statistical uncertainty.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose using heterostructuring—specifically intercalating wide-band-gap insulators into nickelates—as a novel, disorder-free way to achieve electron doping. Theoretical calculations predict that this process drives the material into an optimal superconducting state, yielding high critical temperatures for these complex oxides.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental questions in strongly correlated materials physics by proposing a chemically clean method to tune carrier concentration, which is crucial for understanding unconventional superconductivity mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Electron-doping of nickelate superconductors remains an uncharted territory, and conventional substitutional doping methods have proven insufficient.

**Main result.** The intercalation of wide-band-gap insulators creates a disorder-free route to electron-doped nickelates, predicting high-$T_c$ $d_{x^2-y^2}$-wave superconductivity exceeding 50 K in $	ext{La}_2	ext{NiO}_4:	ext{La}_2	ext{AlO}_4$.

**Method.** The study relies heavily on first-principles calculations, including DFT and advanced many-body techniques like DMFT, D$\Gamma$A, and FLEX.

**Model / system.** The focus is on Ruddlesden–Popper nickelates ($	ext{R}_{n+1}	ext{Ni}_n	ext{O}_{3n+1}$) and their engineered heterostructures formed by intercalating insulating layers like $	ext{LaXO}_3$ into $	ext{La}_2	ext{NiO}_4$.

**Key observables.** Electron doping level (charge transfer), $d_{x^2-y^2}$-wave superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), and orbital occupations ($	ext{Ni}-t_{2g}$ and $	ext{Ni}-e_g$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T_c > 50 	ext{ K}$ for the engineered heterostructures; ambient pressure stability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The superconducting properties are predicted based on theoretical models (D$\Gamma$A) and assume structural/thermodynamic stability of the resulting superlattices.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the intercalation mechanism, showing charge transfer into $	ext{Ni}-3d$ orbitals, and present band structures confirming orbital shifts in the heterostructures.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (electron doping difficulty), proposes the solution (heterostructuring via intercalation), details the theoretical framework using multiple advanced calculations, presents results showing successful electron doping and high $T_c$, and confirms structural stability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Despite enormous expenditures in the research field, the electron-doped side of nickelate superconductors remains uncharted territory. Substituting the trivalent rare-earth cations by a tetravalent one hitherto failed. Here, we demonstrate by first-principles calculations a disorder-free route to electron dope Ruddlesden-Popper nickelates. When intercalating wide-band-gap insulating layers such as La$X$O$_3$ ($X$=Al, Ga, Sc) into La$_2$NiO$_4$, the extra (LaO)$^+$ layers act as electron donors, releasing carriers into the Ni-3$d$ orbitals. This electron doping puts La$_2$NiO$_4$:La$_2$AlO$_4$ naturally in the optimal region for $d_{x^2-y^2}$-wave superconductivity with T$_c$ exceeding 50 K. The same concept also allows us to electron dope La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$, the superconductor in the limelight.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops and applies a GPU-accelerated simulation framework to model hole spin double quantum dots within silicon FinFETs. By combining self-consistent Poisson/Schrödinger solvers with Configuration Interaction theory, the authors accurately calculate exchange coupling. This work is crucial for designing and optimizing solid-state platforms for scalable two-qubit quantum logic gates.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical blueprint for realizing solid-state qubits by simulating complex many-body interactions (exchange coupling) in realistic semiconductor nanostructures, which is central to quantum computing hardware development.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize exchange coupling effects within double quantum dot (DQD) systems hosted in FinFET-like structures for the purpose of implementing two-qubit quantum logic gates.

**Main result.** The simulation framework successfully reproduces the magneto-electrostatic behavior required for controlling qubits, allowing determination of optimal operating conditions for quantum logic gates like CZ and SWAP.

**Method.** The methodology involves a self-consistent Schrödinger-Poisson (SP) solver coupled with a Configuration Interaction (CI) method applied to a two-particle Hamiltonian derived from single-particle states.

**Model / system.** The system is a hole spin DQD confined in a 5-gate silicon FinFET, modeled using a Luttinger-Kohn $6	imes 6$ k.p Hamiltonian that accounts for strain and magnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Exchange coupling constant (J), gate-induced confined electronic states, electrostatic potential, and the energy spectrum of singlet/triplet states as a function of detuning.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate biases (detuning $\varepsilon_{	ext{VP}}$), magnetic field orientation, and device geometry parameters ($L_c$, $h_S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation assumes hole wavefunctions do not penetrate oxide layers (Dirichlet boundary conditions) and uses a reduced basis set while maintaining accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the simulated Si FinFET device geometry, the self-consistent loop between Schrödinger and Poisson equations yielding the DQD potential, and the construction of the two-particle Hamiltonian via Slater determinants.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure detailing the physical platform (FinFET/DQD), outlining the multi-step simulation methodology (SP solver $ightarrow$ single-particle states $ightarrow$ CI diagonalization), presenting results on magneto-electrostatic behavior, and concluding with the determination of optimal gate operating conditions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

By leveraging a GPU-accelerated Schrödinger-Poisson (SP) solver, we characterize exchange coupling in a hole spin double-qubit device involving a double quantum dot (DQD) system formed inside a 5-gate silicon fin field-effect transistor (FinFET) similar to real experimental structures. The self-consistent SP simulations rely on a finite difference discretization of the 3D volume and on a Luttinger-Kohn 6x6 kp Hamiltonian accounting for magnetic fields and strain distribution. They return the gate-induced confined electronic states and the corresponding electrostatic potential hosting the DQD. These quantities serve as inputs to a two-particle Hamiltonian that is constructed from single-particle Slater determinants through the configuration interaction (CI) method. By diagonalizing this two-particle Hamiltonian, the eigenstates and eigenenergies of the DQD system are obtained, together with their exchange coupling. We show that our simulation framework, using a reduced number of basis states, is capable of reproducing the magneto-electrostatic behavior of the devices of interest, as predicted from theory and observed experimentally. We finally leverage our approach to determine the optimal operating conditions of a two-qubit quantum logic gate implemented in a Si FinFET structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes a minimal model of assembly formation via reversible coagulation and fragmentation, treating assemblies as ordered binary trees. It establishes that the system reaches an equilibrium characterized by a Zipf-like distribution and features a crossover length scale $l_c$ dictated by finite volume effects. The paper provides rigorous thermodynamic descriptions for this neutral baseline, allowing researchers to measure deviations caused by selection or external driving forces.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on chemical kinetics/statistical mechanics, the concept of 'neutral baseline' for measuring selection mechanisms in complex systems is highly relevant to open quantum systems where environmental coupling or external driving fields are introduced.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates equilibrium properties, finite-size fluctuations, and scaling laws within a mean-field reaction network where species are complex assemblies built via reversible coagulation and fragmentation.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a crossover between deterministic (Zipf-like) and fluctuation-dominated regimes controlled by a length scale $l_c \sim \ln V$, and the equilibrium state admits a clear thermodynamic description.

**Method.** The analysis combines combinatorial counting (Catalan numbers), detailed balance conditions, generating functions for mass conservation, and asymptotic scaling theory to analyze entropy differences between statistical and thermodynamic ensembles.

**Model / system.** The model is a closed pool of identical atoms undergoing reversible coagulation ($x+y ightleftharpoons x(y)$) into ordered binary tree assemblies. The network dynamics are assumed to be dilute, loop-free, and atom-conserving.

**Key observables.** Diversity ($\langle D_l angle$), Shannon Entropy ($s_{Sh}$), Thermodynamic Entropy ($S_{th}^{	ext{pool}}$), and various assembly-weighted observables $A^{(i)}(V)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite volume $V$, crossover length $l_c \sim \ln V$, kinetic ratio $r = k_F/k_K$, bond energy ($\epsilon_B$), and chemical potential.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is dilute, reversible, loop-free, and the equilibrium state is governed by mass conservation constraints in finite volumes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the binary tree structure of assemblies (up to $l=5$) and show the scaling behavior of diversity ($\langle D_l angle$) relative to the volume-dependent crossover scale $l_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the combinatorial nature of the species space, deriving the equilibrium concentrations via detailed balance, establishing the finite-size crossover governed by $l_c$, and finally comparing different entropy definitions (Shannon vs. Thermodynamic) to analyze scaling laws.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a mean-field reaction network whose species are assemblies built from identical atoms by reversible coagulation and fragmentation. Each assembly is an ordered binary tree, so the number of species of a given length grows combinatorially, as the Catalan numbers. The model nonetheless admits an explicit equilibrium and tractable stochastic dynamics. A finite volume $V$ sets a crossover length $l_c \sim \ln V$ that splits the equilibrium into two sectors. Below $l_c$ each assembly occurs in many copies and the rank-frequency distribution is Zipf-like; above $l_c$ individual species are rare and fluctuation-dominated. The statistical weight of the rare sector decays slowly with volume, controlling the finite-size scaling of diversity, Shannon entropy, and other assembly-weighted observables. The equilibrium also admits a transparent grand-canonical description in terms of a bond energy and an atomic chemical potential. Together these results make the model a controlled neutral baseline against which selection and driving in richer assembly networks can be measured.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes that the strange metal state in cuprates arises from itinerant fermions scattering off abundant, low-energy Charge Density Fluctuations (CDF). Using the Shrinking Fermi Liquid theory, the authors show this mechanism consistently explains linear-T resistivity and other anomalous transport properties by incorporating CDFs alongside phonons and paramagnons.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed coupling of fermionic quasiparticles to specific bosonic fluctuations (CDF) provides a concrete mechanism for emergent non-Fermi liquid behavior in strongly correlated materials, which is highly relevant to understanding quantum critical points.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the anomalous 'Strange Metal' (SM) state observed in slightly overdoped cuprates, characterized by resistivity linear in temperature, which defies standard Fermi Liquid theory.

**Main result.** The SM behavior is successfully described by interpreting transport properties as scattering between itinerant fermions and low-energy Charge Density Fluctuations (CDF), leading to a consistent model for various anomalous measurements.

**Method.** The authors combine theoretical modeling (Shrinking Fermi Liquid theory) with analysis of experimental data from RIXS and EELS spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The system is the cuprate superconductor, modeled by considering itinerant fermions interacting with low-energy bosonic excitations, specifically Charge Density Fluctuations (CDF), phonons, and spin paramagnons.

**Key observables.** T-linear resistivity ($ho \propto T$), specific heat coefficient ($\gamma(T)$), Seebeck coefficient ($S/T$), and magnetoresistance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low energy scale of CDF ($\omega_{	ext{CDF}} \sim 5-10$ meV) and the finite energy scale $M/\gamma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption for low-temperature behavior is that the CDF dissipation parameter ($\gamma$) grows logarithmically as temperature decreases when superconductivity is suppressed.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 shows RIXS and EELS spectra of Bi2212, decomposing signals to identify elastic peaks, CDFs, phonons, and paramagnon continua.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the SM problem, identifies CDF as the key low-energy scatterer via experimental characterization (RIXS/EELS), develops the Shrinking Fermi Liquid theory framework for transport and optical properties, and finally applies this model to explain multiple anomalous thermodynamic and transport measurements across different doping regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We interpret the strange metal (SM) properties of slightly overdoped cuprates in terms of the recently proposed Shrinking Fermi Liquid theory. This is based on the pervading presence in the cuprate phase diagram of charge density fluctuations (CDF), which have been identified and characterized in RIXS. These fluctuations are abundant and have a low energy due to the proximity of the charge density wave quantum critical point hidden under the superconducting dome of cuprates, but have a short range and non-critical character with a finite energy M/γ~10 meV as measured above Tc. Here M~ ξ^-2 is determined by the short correlation length ξ, while γencodes the Landau damping ruling the lifetime of the charge fluctuations. Besides these low energy CDF, cuprates also display phonons and a broad continuum of particle-hole excitations, mostly due to spin paramagnons arising from their strongly correlated character. With these experimentally characterized ingredients we show that above Tc the SM properties in transport are well described in terms of fermionic Landau quasiparticles scattering with CDF and phonons. The optical properties can instead be interpreted by the combined effect of low energy CDF determining the temperature dependence, and of the paramagnon continuum determining a linear in frequency scattering rate. Remarkably, the combined effect of these simple ingredients also induces ω/T scaling properties for frequencies larger than M/γ. When superconductivity is suppressed by strong magnetic fields the SM properties extend down to a few Kelvin. By assuming that the CDF dissipation parameter γgrows logarithmically by lowering T, we account for all anomalous transport and thermodynamic properties of cuprates (specific heat, Seebeck, heat transport, resistivity, and magnetoresistance) thereby providing a consistent scenario for the SM phase of cuprates.

</details>

### [Chiral Tube Algebras I: Topological Defect Lines, Twisted Modules, and Finite Gauging](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07786v1)

**Authors:** Nathan Benjamin, Ho Tat Lam, Conghuan Luo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.07786v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

The middle figure is the interpretation of a TDL as a defect that twists the boundary condition and defines the defect Hilbert space HL. The right figure illustrates how a TDL maps between different defect Hilbert spaces. </div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'Chiral Tube Algebras' to create a unified mathematical framework bridging Chiral Algebras (local symmetries) and Topological Defect Lines (topological symmetries) within 2D CFTs. It shows how this new structure naturally describes the behavior of chiral algebras when subjected to processes like finite gauging or interaction with defects. This advances the understanding of symmetry in topological quantum field theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the concept of generalizing symmetry structures from local operators to defect lines is crucial for understanding boundary conditions and topological phases in condensed matter systems, which can inform models used in AMO physics or quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to unify two complementary symmetry concepts in 2D Conformal Field Theories (CFTs): Chiral Algebras and symmetries associated with Topological Defect Lines (TDLs).

**Main result.** It introduces 'Chiral Tube Algebras' as a generalized framework that incorporates TDL effects, allowing for the description of chiral algebras under finite gauging.

**Method.** The authors use advanced algebraic techniques involving fusion categories, module theory, and generalizations of vertex operator algebras to construct and analyze these new algebras.

**Model / system.** The theoretical setting is 2D Conformal Field Theories (CFTs), which are studied using mathematical tools like chiral currents, defect Hilbert spaces ($\mathcal{H}_L$), and tensor categories.

**Key observables.** Irreducible modules of the algebras; fusion rules ($N^k_{ij}$); mapping between local and defect Hilbert spaces.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality (2D) and the nature of the symmetry (local vs. topological/non-invertible).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes unitary compact CFTs when dealing with chiral algebras, though the framework is designed to extend beyond these limits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how a TDL can be interpreted as an operator acting on the local Hilbert space or as a defect defining a twisted defect Hilbert space ($\mathcal{H}_L$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the distinct roles of chiral algebras and TDLs, then proposing Chiral Tube Algebras to unify them, illustrating this unification with known examples (Kac-Moody, W algebras), and concluding with a roadmap for future work on non-local currents.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chiral algebras and topological defect lines (TDLs) represent two complementary notions of symmetry in 2d conformal field theories. In this paper, we introduce chiral tube algebras to unify and extend these two notions. Chiral tube algebras generalize chiral algebras in two ways. First, they extend the action of chiral algebras beyond the local Hilbert space to include defect Hilbert spaces twisted by TDLs. Second, they allow for non-local chiral currents attached by TDLs and thus can map between different defect Hilbert spaces, analogous to the tube algebras of TDLs. Since local chiral currents can become non-local after finite gauging, chiral tube algebras provide a natural framework for describing the image of chiral algebras under such gauging. We illustrate this framework through a variety of examples that generalize familiar chiral algebras, including Kac-Moody algebras, $\mathcal{W}$ algebras, superconformal algebras, and their orbifolds/bosonizations. We construct their irreducible modules, which are isomorphic to twisted modules of the corresponding chiral algebras, and use them to organize local and defect Hilbert spaces. In a subsequent paper, we will study chiral tube algebras generated by non-local chiral currents with fractional spins, which have no counterparts in chiral algebras.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Procrustes tomography as an efficient method to characterize noisy quantum channels, addressing the computational bottleneck of quantum process tomography. By reformulating the problem as a simple least-squares minimization using input and output states, it outperforms established techniques under realistic noise conditions like uneven sampling or state preparation errors. This makes high-fidelity characterization more accessible for NISQ devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a computationally tractable and robust framework for characterizing open quantum systems on near-term hardware by simplifying the complex mathematical requirements of full process tomography.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum process tomography, which characterizes noisy quantum channels, is computationally expensive, especially for NISQ devices.

**Main result.** Procrustes tomography is presented as an efficient alternative that matches or exceeds the performance of established methods like Choi reconstruction and linear inversion tomography.

**Method.** The method formulates channel reconstruction by minimizing the Frobenius norm between measured output states (B) and input states (A), solving for $\Lambda$ via $\Lambda = B A^+$.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on characterizing noisy quantum channels ($\Lambda$) acting on qubits, often modeled using dynamics governed by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Infidelity ($1 - \langle F_{E} angle$), Process matrix ($\Lambda$), Choi-matrix ($ho_{	ext{Choi}}$), and measurement probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of independent measurement samples ($N$), relaxation times ($T_1, T_2$), and the degree of state preparation error.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares methods under conditions of finite-size sampling effects and imperfect input state preparation, requiring specific corrections for accurate results.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare infidelity vs. measurement shots ($N$) for different reconstruction techniques (Procrustes vs. Choi vs. Linear Inversion) under scenarios involving uneven state sampling or input state preparation errors.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, reviews established tomography methods, develops and details Procrustes tomography, and then rigorously compares its performance against competitors using various realistic noise models (uneven sampling, state preparation errors).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

What is the most expensive part of quantum device characterization? Clearly, the answer is quantum process tomography. However, especially for noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) computers, a comprehensive understanding of the noisy quantum dynamics is essential in interpreting the computational output. In this work, we introduce an efficient method -- Procrustes tomography -- that outperforms established methods in a number of aspects. After a pedagogical and constructive introduction, we demonstrate the utility of the method for representative examples of noisy quantum channels.

</details>

(a) The hider independently prepares the two-party quantum states τb1,2, τb1,3, and τb2,3 from the ensemble T , where each bit b1,2, b1,3, b2,3 ∈{0, 1} is chosen uniformly at random, and distributes the states to the corresponding pairs of parties (A1, A2), (A1, A3), and (A2, A3), respectively. (b) To hide a bit x ∈{0, 1}, the hider broadcasts the bit string ⃗c = (c1,2, c1,3, c2,3) to the parties A1, A2, and A3, where c1,2 = b1,2 ⊕x, c1,3 = b1,2 ⊕b1,3 and c2,3 = b1,2 ⊕b2,3.</figcaption>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a $(2, m)$-threshold quantum data-hiding scheme to securely hide one classical bit among multiple parties. It proves that while any pair can perfectly recover the secret bit through joint measurement, local operations and classical communication reveal negligible information. The construction is notable because it only requires separable states, enhancing its practical feasibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete cryptographic primitive (quantum data hiding) that is physically realizable using only separable states, making it highly relevant for quantum communication protocols in AMO settings where entanglement generation might be difficult.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a $(2, m)$-threshold quantum data-hiding scheme for concealing one classical bit among $m$ parties.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the hidden bit can be perfectly recovered by any pair of parties via joint measurement, while local operations and classical communication (LOCC) reveal only an arbitrarily small amount of information.

**Method.** The scheme is constructed using a building block ensemble based on orthogonal PPT states shared between pairs of parties, leveraging bounds derived from multipartite state discrimination theory.

**Model / system.** The model involves $m$ parties sharing quantum states in a tensor product Hilbert space. The security relies on the difference between joint measurements and LOCC measurements on these multipartite states.

**Key observables.** Discrimination probabilities, specifically $p_L(E)$ (LOCC) versus $p_{	ext{PPT}}(E)$, which quantifies the information leakage about the hidden bit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The threshold parameter is $(2, m)$, meaning any pair of parties can succeed in recovery. The security relies on $\epsilon > 0$ being arbitrarily small.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The scheme is shown to be implementable using only separable states of low-dimensional quantum systems, avoiding the need for entanglement.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the subsystems shared between pairs of parties for $m=3$. Figure 2 details the state preparation and broadcast bits for the $m=3$ case.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework for multipartite quantum state discrimination, deriving bounds using PPT properties. It then constructs the $(2, m)$-threshold scheme by defining the shared states ($\vec{	au}_b$) and the classical broadcast bits ($\vec{c}$) necessary to achieve the desired security gap.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider multiparty quantum state discrimination and present a multiparty quantum data-hiding scheme for one classical bit to be shared among multiple parties. In the proposed scheme, any pair of parties can collaborate to perfectly recover the hidden bit through a joint measurement, whereas measurements based on local operations and classical communication(LOCC) performed even by all parties reveal only an arbitrarily small amount of information. We further provide bounds on the optimal LOCC discrimination of multiparty quantum states. The proposed scheme can be implemented using only separable states of low-dimensional quantum systems, enhancing its practical feasibility.

</details>

**Summary.** The research introduces a novel platform using stacked 1D $	ext{MoS}_2$ nanoribbons to create Moiré Quantum Dots (MQDs). By controlling the overlap angle and size, the authors demonstrate that the resulting exciton physics is highly tunable. This work advances the study of moiré phenomena by confining them to discrete quantum dot junctions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimental realization of highly confined, tunable moiré systems, allowing for the study of quantum phenomena in a controlled, quasi-zero-dimensional geometry relevant to solid-state quantum emitters.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To study Moiré physics in a confined geometry, moving beyond the limitations of extended 2D-2D twisted bilayers which lack intrinsic lateral confinement.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated that precise overlapping of 1D $	ext{MoS}_2$ nanoribbons forms Moiré quantum dots (MQDs) whose exciton physics is tunable by controlling the overlap angle and size.

**Method.** The study involves synthesizing and deterministically stacking monolayer $	ext{MoS}_2$ nanoribbons using CVD growth followed by controlled transfer and annealing onto a substrate.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Moiré Quantum Dots (MQDs) formed at the intersection of stacked 1D $	ext{MoS}_2$ nanoribbons, creating a quasi-0D quantum dot structure with tunable moiré potentials.

**Key observables.** Angle-dependent exciton emission (enhanced at $22^\circ$), PL redshift dependent on size, and temperature-dependent fast radiative channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), Moiré dot size, cryogenic temperature regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is treated as a quasi-0D quantum-dot system due to the 1D building blocks; edge-mediated lattice reconstruction deepens the local Moiré potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the fabrication process (SEM images of NRs, OM image of stacked MQDs) and atomic schematics confirming the stacking geometry and resulting Moiré pattern.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure detailing synthesis (CVD growth of 1D NRs), assembly (controlled stacking/transfer), and characterization using PL and structural microscopy to observe angle-dependent electronic properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted atomically thin layers have attracted much attention for Moiré potential and correlated quantum phenomena. However, existing Moiré superlattices have largely been limited to extensive wavefunction without lateral confinement. Here we introduce a new platform where 1D nanoribbons of 2D MoS2 grown by vapor deposition can be easily superposed at various angles from stacking and transferring, to form Moiré quantum dots at their intersections with unique exciton physics. Angle-dependent Moiré intersections show enhanced exciton emission at commensurate angle 22 deg, which demonstrates faster relaxation at the cryogenic temperature. A size-dependent study further exhibits a reduced exciton energy and soften out-of-plane interlayer coupling for smaller Moiré areas. Our results reveal exciton physics turnability via precise overlapping of 1D nanoribbons.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum Dense Network Coding (DNC), a protocol demonstrating that quantum resources allow for significantly more efficient information transmission than classical methods. It proves this advantage is robust, requires shared entanglement and quantum links, and establishes a foundation for a new, secure quantum key growing scheme.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract coding theory, the rigorous analysis of entanglement requirements and noise robustness provides general theoretical tools applicable to characterizing resource limitations in complex open quantum systems or communication channels.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is demonstrating how quantum resources can enable communication protocols, specifically 'quantum dense network coding' (DNC), to achieve superior efficiency compared to classical methods.

**Main result.** DNC transmits function outputs using provably half the qubits per sender than classically required, an advantage that requires both shared entanglement and quantum communication. This framework also yields a novel, information-theoretically secure key growing protocol.

**Method.** The authors analyze distributed computation success probabilities in various network models (Classical, Quantum, CE, QE) using MAC formalism and Weyl operators to quantify the resource advantage.

**Model / system.** The study uses abstract quantum communication models involving multiaccess networks computing functions like addition modulo $2^n$. The core mechanism relies on shared maximally entangled states and generalized Bell measurements.

**Key observables.** Success probability ($P_f^S$), communication advantage (quantified by success probability ratios), and the achievable key distillation rate ($R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The advantage scales exponentially with the number of senders ($n$); security is governed by error parameters $\epsilon$ and soundness/completeness bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum advantage critically depends on the availability of both shared entanglement and quantum communication channels; asymptotic rate calculations assume product states ($ho_{AnBn} = ho^{\otimes n}_{AB}$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates four types of multiaccess networks. Figure 2 plots the achievable success probability bounds, showing a clear quantum advantage over classical limits. Figure 3 relates to key growing rate calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the communication efficiency problem (DNC) and proving its resource-dependent advantage using specific protocols ($\oplus_{2}^{2n}$), to establishing security definitions for distributed computation, and finally deriving asymptotic achievable rates for quantum key growth.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central problem in quantum information theory is understanding how quantum resources can be used to communicate information more efficiently than classical resources. We introduce quantum dense network coding -- a protocol that transmits the output of a non-Boolean function to a receiver using provably half as many qubits as bits for each sender by not transmitting the entirety of the function inputs. We show this advantage requires both shared entanglement and quantum communication, is robust to noise, and the gap in success probability between quantum and classical communication can be amplified exponentially in the number of senders. Finally, we show that dense network coding gives rise to a novel, information-theoretically secure, quantum cryptographic protocol, which we call measurement-device-independent quantum key growing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel parallel decoding strategy for QEC in distributed quantum computers by integrating Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) locally into the Belief Propagation and Ordered Statistics Decoding pipeline. The method significantly reduces computational complexity while maintaining high logical accuracy, proving that local compression is effective where global compression fails.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation, the techniques of dimensionality reduction via SVD applied to structured matrices (like parity checks) are relevant for analyzing complex correlation structures in many-body systems or simulating highly entangled states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing efficient, scalable decoding algorithms for Quantum Error Correction (QEC) when implemented on distributed quantum computing hardware.

**Main result.** Applying Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) as a *local* preprocessing filter significantly improves the computational complexity and maintains high logical accuracy compared to global or non-optimized decoding methods.

**Method.** The approach combines Belief Propagation (BP) for initial error estimation with Ordered Statistics Decoding (OSD), introducing SVD locally on sub-regions of the lattice to compress the error vector basis before final decoding.

**Model / system.** The work models quantum computation using stabilizer codes, specifically focusing on Surface Codes implemented across a distributed network of Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) connected by quantum links.

**Key observables.** Logical Accuracy (%) over 1000 trials; Computational Complexity ($O(m^3 + (n/r)^3)$); Execution Time (seconds).

**Important parameters / regimes.** SVD Energy Threshold ($\epsilon$); Physical Error Rate ($	ext{perr}$); Number of QPUs ($\#$ QPU); Code parameters $[[n, k, d]]$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that local SVD compression retains sufficient topological information for accurate decoding, unlike global compression which fails due to loss of inter-qubit correlations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the planar code structure and circuits for syndrome extraction. Comparisons show performance vs. energy threshold (Table 2) and scalability vs. number of QPUs (Table 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces QEC decoding bottlenecks using BP/OSD on surface codes. It then proposes the novel local SVD pre-processing step, analyzes its complexity gains over centralized methods, and finally validates performance and scalability across varying error rates and node counts.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A novel parallel approach is proposed for QEC decoding based on Belief Propagation with Ordered Statistics Decoding. The main idea is to pre-process the error vectors obtained from Belief Propagation by applying Singular Value Decomposition locally to sub-regions of the lattice. The proposed approach is applied to distributed quantum computers and evaluated in terms of complexity, accuracy, and scalability.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive framework for compiling quantum circuits onto error-corrected silicon spin qubit architectures using the 'snakes on a plane' model. It proposes and compares two novel routing algorithms—shortest-path and rotation-based—to solve the complex scheduling problem. The findings guide researchers by showing that the optimal algorithm choice depends entirely on whether the target hardware is sparse or highly dense.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial algorithmic insights into scaling quantum computation by directly addressing the complex interplay between circuit compilation, physical connectivity constraints, and fault tolerance in solid-state qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing efficient compilation and routing techniques for quantum circuits onto error-corrected silicon spin qubit platforms, which must account for hardware constraints.

**Main result.** The authors propose two novel routing algorithms—shortest-path and rotation-based—showing that their performance depends critically on the underlying architecture's density (sparse vs. high-density).

**Method.** The work formalizes physical qubit routing as a graph path-finding problem, comparing explicit path planning against dynamic rotational scheduling techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is based on silicon spin qubits implemented using the 'snakes on a plane' model, which utilizes a 2D surface code for error correction and involves shuttling logical qubits along lattice edges.

**Key observables.** Mean Timesteps and Mean Movements required to execute quantum operations; scalability of runtime with increasing qubit density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit density (sparse vs. high-density), defect probability ($p_{	ext{defect}}$), and the type of initial mapping strategy used.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The architecture is modeled as a 2D latticework supporting surface code operations, and routing failures are handled via 'snake surgery' teleportation protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 'snakes on a plane' architecture, the snake surgery protocol, graph formalization of routing challenges (collisions/defects), and performance comparisons showing path vs. rotation algorithm scaling.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and problem gap; it then details the graph formalization and error handling protocols; finally, it compares shortest-path and rotation algorithms across various architectural settings to determine optimal routing strategies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Silicon spin qubits have emerged as a promising qubit technology due to their favorable scaling and fabrication properties. However, efficiently compiling quantum circuits onto spin qubit platforms remains challenging, particularly when accounting for hardware constraints and the high sensitivity to static defects. Existing compilation approaches for spin qubits either largely ignore error correction, despite its critical role for large-scale quantum computation, or focus on low-level schedule constructions, missing a high-level compilation and routing for logical, error-corrected algorithms. To address this gap, we introduce a compilation framework for spin qubits based on the recent snakes on a plane model, which utilizes a 2D surface code and qubit teleportation to mitigate errors. Building on this model, we propose shortest-path and rotation-based algorithms as two novel classes of qubit-routing techniques, along with additional defect-handling and initial-mapping strategies. We evaluate both algorithms across diverse architectural settings and problem sizes, demonstrating that shortest-path methods excel in sparse, low-defect scenarios, while rotation-based approaches perform better in high-density environments. The implementation is publicly available on GitHub as open source.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that nonlocal electronic correlations in 1D materials can spontaneously generate topological order by inducing Charge-Density-Wave (CDW) or Bond-Order-Wave (BOW) states. Using advanced cluster diagrammatic techniques, they map these interacting many-body phases onto effective Hamiltonians resembling the SSH and Rice-Mele models, confirming their nontrivial topological nature via edge state signatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical link between interaction-driven symmetry breaking in correlated electron models and the emergence of robust topological invariants, which is highly relevant for understanding novel quantum phases in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to establish a mechanism by which nonlocal electronic correlations can induce topological phases in low-dimensional systems, starting from an initially trivial noninteracting band structure.

**Main result.** Nonlocal interactions drive the system into Bond-Order-Wave (BOW) and Charge-Density-Wave (CDW) phases, both of which possess nontrivial topological character, evidenced by localized edge states.

**Method.** The research employs an advanced cluster-diagrammatic many-body approach (D-TRILEX framework) applied to the extended Hubbard model, mapping the interacting system onto effective noninteracting Hamiltonians for analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is modeled by the one-dimensional extended Hubbard model, characterized by hopping ($t$), local repulsion ($U$), and nearest-neighbor nonlocal repulsion ($V$). The analysis focuses on the emergence of BOW and CDW phases.

**Key observables.** BOW order parameter ($	ext{O}_{	ext{BOW}}$), staggered charge density ($	ext{O}_{	ext{CDW}}$), effective Hamiltonian parameters ($u_k, v_k, w_k$), and edge-localization weight $|\phi_j^{	ext{edge}}|^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlocal interaction strength ($V$) relative to local repulsion ($U$); temperature ($T=0.1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The single-site D-TRILEX approach fails for symmetry-broken phases due to the nonlocal nature of the order parameters, necessitating a dimer/cluster formulation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing coexisting CDW+BOW regions; energy spectra demonstrate the evolution from bulk states to SSH-like edge modes (BOW) and finally to RM-like gapped states (CDW).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the model and using cluster methods to identify symmetry-broken phases. It then maps these interacting states onto effective single-particle Hamiltonians, analyzing the topological signatures (edge states) associated with both CDW and BOW order parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate that electronic correlations in low-dimensional systems can induce topological phases starting from a topologically trivial noninteracting band structure. Using an advanced cluster-diagrammatic many-body approach applied to the one-dimensional extended Hubbard model, we show that tuning the nonlocal Coulomb interaction drives the emergence of bond-order-wave (BOW) and charge-density-wave (CDW) phases. Despite being interaction-driven and symmetry-broken, these states admit an effective low-energy single-particle description. In particular, the BOW phase maps onto an effective Su-Schrieffer-Heeger model, while the CDW phase, with subleading bond-order correlations, corresponds to a Rice-Mele model. Both phases exhibit a nontrivial topological character, manifested by the presence of localized edge states. Our results establish a mechanism by which nonlocal electronic correlations generate emergent topology in correlated systems.

</details>

### [Works on My QPU: Reproducibility in Quantum Computing Research](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08348v1)

**Authors:** Dominik Köster, Maja Franz, Benjamin Zec, Nicole Hoess, Ralf Ramsauer, Wolfgang Mauerer  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08348v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

The results for the final reproducibility evaluation are summarised in Tab. II.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the reproducibility crisis in quantum computing research by systematically auditing thousands of published papers. It finds that code artifacts are scarce and often fail to run due to incomplete environment specifications, rather than just missing code. The authors recommend adopting declarative tools to enforce rigorous, reproducible computational environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on software best practices rather than physical Hamiltonians, the discussion of complex dependency management and required environmental rigor is highly relevant to simulating or analyzing open quantum systems that rely on specific numerical libraries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The reproducibility of published results in quantum computing research is severely compromised by complex, evolving software stacks.

**Main result.** Reproducibility is not consistently achieved; only a small fraction of papers provide executable code artifacts with complete environment specifications.

**Method.** The authors conducted a combined manual and automated large-scale analysis across thousands of QC papers using a five-criterion framework (code availability, environment specification, etc.).

**Model / system.** The focus is on the software workflow surrounding quantum computing algorithms, which involve logical schedules transpiled to physical QPU circuits.

**Key observables.** Code availability rate (~25%), execution success rate among available code (low), and completeness of machine-readable environment specifications.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis uses a five-question framework: Code Availability, Environment Specification, Documentation, Hardware Description, and Executability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that reproducibility requires capturing the full execution context, necessitating declarative environment specification tools like Nix.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 details the pipeline from a large initial corpus (4966 papers) down to the final set of manually validated NISQ-era papers (127).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem, presents the methodology involving both manual and automated screening against five criteria, quantifies the failure rates across these metrics, and concludes with practical recommendations for improvement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing research increasingly depends on complex software stacks, yet the reproducibility of published results does not receive the priority and longevity mandated by recommendations of large international scientific bodies and best practices in software-centric systems research. In this paper, we present a combined manual and automated large-scale analysis of the reproducibility landscape in quantum computing research, quantify shortcomings, and derive actionable steps forward.   We manually evaluate a curated sample of 127 papers using a five-question framework that covers code availability, environment specification, documentation, hardware description, and executability. To place these findings in a broader context, we conduct an automated large-scale screening of nearly 5000 quantum computing papers for the same reproducibility indicators. Our manual analysis reveals that only 24.4% of the sampled papers provide code artefacts, and among those, 64.5% fail to execute successfully in a clean environment. This assessment is corroborated by a large-scale automated analysis that yields a consistent code availability rate of 26.8%. Further, it shows that approximately one-third of the papers with accessible code lack machine-readable environment specifications.   The results in this paper indicate that reproducibility is not yet consistently achieved in quantum computing research. In response, we outline a set of practical recommendations that address the observed failure modes and illustrate how reproducibility can be improved in practice.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper creates a formal taxonomy to audit the geometric assumptions made when using quantum representations (like projectors or subspaces) in machine learning. It proves that various complex quantum scoring methods are mathematically equivalent to simpler, traceable Hermitian projector overlaps. The work emphasizes that the utility of any quantum ML approach depends entirely on whether its inherent mathematical invariances match the physical symmetries of the problem being solved.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO dynamics, the rigorous geometric analysis of quantum information structures (projectors, density matrices) provides deep mathematical tools for understanding how physical constraints manifest as computational invariances in QML applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how machine learning models implicitly make 'invariance decisions' when replacing raw data vectors with geometric representations like projectors, subspaces, or density operators.

**Main result.** It develops a formal taxonomy showing that the choice of representation dictates the imposed invariance, proving key equivalences between quantum fidelity kernels and Hermitian projector traces.

**Method.** The authors develop a mathematical framework—a 'real-to-Hermitian taxonomy'—to audit these geometric lifts by comparing kernel methods (Grassmann/Flag) against variational return probability scoring mechanisms.

**Model / system.** The focus is on Quantum Machine Learning (QML) representations, analyzing geometries such as the Grassmannian manifold and complex projective space ($\mathbb{CP}^{D-1}$) using projectors ($P$), density operators ($ho$), and learned anchor matrices ($A_	heta$).

**Key observables.** Invariance properties (e.g., block-gauge invariance), kernel values, QVR return probabilities ($p_	heta(x)$), and trace consistency between different scoring methods.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the Hilbert space ($D$), the rank of projectors ($r$), and the number of measurement shots/noise levels are key parameters in the audits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The paper explicitly states it makes no claim regarding hardware speedup or quantum advantage; all results are derived from statevector simulations using controlled, synthetic datasets as witnesses.

**Figures summary.** Tables summarize comparisons across various ML tasks (e.g., classwise anchor protocols, regression performance) and provide conceptual mappings between different geometric representations and their associated invariances.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the core problem of invariance decisions to developing mathematical proofs for specific kernels (Grassmann/Flag), establishing equivalences (Fidelity $\leftrightarrow$ Projector Trace), and concluding with a comprehensive taxonomy supported by multiple comparative audits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Machine-learning models often replace vectors by normalized directions, projectors, covariances, subspaces, ordered flags, quantum states, or density operators before any classifier is fitted. This replacement is an invariance decision: it determines which distinctions are kept and which are quotiented out. We develop a self-contained real-to-Hermitian taxonomy for auditing such representations in quantum machine learning. On the real side, we formalize Grassmann and flag projector kernels, prove positive semidefiniteness and block-gauge invariance of a weighted flag kernel, and give a same-span block-swap witness showing when whole-span Grassmann geometry must fail while ordered flags succeed. On the quantum side, we prove that a noiseless fidelity kernel is exactly the Hilbert--Schmidt inner product between the associated rank-one Hermitian projectors, and that a QVR-style return probability is exactly an overlap score between the input projector and a learned anchor operator. Rank-constrained returns correspond to complex Grassmann anchors, while mixed or multimodal class models are naturally represented by density or positive-semidefinite anchors. Controlled vector, subspace, statevector, anomaly, finite-shot, and quotient-witness experiments support the same conclusion: quantum and geometric lifts are useful when their invariances match the task, and fail correctly when discarded information is label-bearing. The paper makes no hardware-speedup or quantum-advantage claim.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically models the evolution of satellite X-ray emission intensity near ionization thresholds for Copper K-shell transitions. Using sophisticated ab initio calculations, the authors demonstrate that their methods accurately predict spectral changes by accounting for shake processes originating from specific resonant excitations in oxide compounds. This provides a powerful tool for high-resolution spectroscopy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it applies advanced quantum mechanical methods to model complex many-body dynamics (shake processes) in atomic/solid-state spectra, which shares mathematical techniques with open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how satellite X-ray emission intensity evolves near the ionization threshold for Cu K-shell transitions.

**Main result.** Standard ab initio methods successfully simulate this intensity evolution with good agreement to experiment, identifying below-threshold satellites originating from specific resonant excitations in oxide phases.

**Method.** The research employs state-of-the-art ab initio theoretical calculations using the Multiconfiguration Dirac-Fock code (MCDFGME) to model radiative and non-radiative decay processes.

**Model / system.** The system under study is Copper (Cu) atoms/ions, specifically examining K-shell transitions in $	ext{Cu(I)}$ and $	ext{Cu(II)}$ oxide phases. The theoretical framework models the transition intensity by incorporating shake probabilities derived from atomic structure calculations.

**Key observables.** Satellite intensity ($K\alpha_1$ and $K\alpha_2$), total decay width ($\Gamma_i$), shake probabilities (shake-off and shake-up), and spectral line shape modulation near thresholds.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ionization threshold energy, excitation energies, binding energies, and the relative contributions of different excitation channels ($1s	o 3d$, $1s	o 4p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on the Sudden Approximation (SA) for shake processes; solid-state effects are noted as a potential source of discrepancy with experiment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overlap integral between beam Gaussian profiles and initial level Lorentzian profiles, and show simulated synthetic spectra for Cu K$\alpha_{1,2}$ including all satellite contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by detailing theoretical models (Thomas/Roy), implementing calculations using MCDFGME to determine transition rates and shake probabilities, modeling the energy dependence via overlap integrals, and finally comparing these simulations against experimental observations for Cu oxide species.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we have investigated the evolution of satellite intensity near the ionization threshold for Cu K-shell transitions through theoretical methods. Employing standard state-of-the-art ab initio methods, we have calculated all Cu K-shell transitions and simulated the full K$α_1$ and K$α_2$ spectrum where all transition parameters, as well as shake probabilities were determined theoretically. Through these calculations we show that standard state-of-the-art ab initio methods achieve good agreement with experiment and enable us to simulate the intensity evolution near ionization thresholds within a good margin of error. Below-threshold satellite intensity was found to originate from resonant 1s$\rightarrow$3d and 1s$\rightarrow$4p excitations in Cu(I) and Cu(II) oxide phases respectively, which were included in our simulations.

</details>

**Authors:** Marcel Kühn, Bernd Rosenow

**Summary.** This work explains that the vast number of near-zero eigenvalues in neural network loss functions are not random artifacts but rather signatures of approximate continuous symmetries inherent in the network's structure. By analyzing how these symmetries are weakly broken by nonlinearities, the authors provide a deep physical understanding of low-curvature directions in ML optimization.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful analogy linking concepts from condensed matter physics—specifically Goldstone theorems related to broken continuous symmetries—to the geometry of high-dimensional machine learning loss landscapes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the mystery of near-zero eigenvalues in the Hessian matrix of a neural network's loss landscape.

**Main result.** It argues that this bulk of small eigenvalues originates from pseudo-Goldstone modes associated with continuous symmetries of the network parametrization, which are only weakly broken by nonlinearities like ReLU.

**Method.** The analysis involves studying the Hessian spectrum by constructing an adiabatic path between a perfectly symmetric linear limit and the full nonlinear model using perturbation theory and eigenvector overlap calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is deep neural networks, analyzed through their loss function's Hessian matrix. The study progresses from simple two-layer linear models to complex convolutional architectures trained on datasets like CIFAR-10.

**Key observables.** Hessian eigenvalues spectrum (large outliers vs. small bulk), overlap ($	ext{o}_i$) between eigenvectors and the symmetry subspace, and eigenvalue scaling laws ($\epsilon^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The perturbation strength $\epsilon$ controlling the transition from linear to nonlinear regimes; network depth and architecture (e.g., convolutional layers).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that symmetries are continuous and relies on comparing the Hessian at an endpoint of training rather than an exact critical point.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of eigenvalues as symmetry is broken ($\epsilon^2$ scaling) and show eigenvector overlap measurements comparing periodic vs. nonperiodic boundary conditions in CNNs.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds its argument by first establishing exact zero modes in linear networks, then perturbing this system with ReLU to generate pseudo-Goldstone modes, and finally generalizing the diagnostic tool (overlap measurement) to complex convolutional architectures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Hessian of the training loss governs the local geometry of the loss landscape, yet despite existing explanations for its largest eigenvalues, the origin of the vast multitude of vanishingly small eigenvalues remains elusive. We argue that the bulk consists of the weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of the continuous symmetries of the network parametrization. In deep linear networks these symmetries are exact: they generate flat directions and hence exact zero modes, whose eigenvectors we construct explicitly. Introducing a ReLU nonlinearity as a perturbation, we show that it breaks these symmetries weakly and explicitly. Resolving the spectrum at the level of eigenvectors, we find that the high-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace, while the bulk lies almost entirely within it. We demonstrate the mechanism in a two-layer ReLU student--teacher model and in a network trained on CIFAR-10. A convolutional example demonstrates that the same diagnostic extends beyond fully connected layers. Together, these results link the Hessian bulk to weakly broken symmetries and clarify the origin of near-zero modes.

</details>

### [Instability of the undecidable behavior of the spectral gap in 1D](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08686v1)

**Authors:** Laura Castilla-Castellano, Angelo Lucia  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08686v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `exotic spin models in light-matter` **1/5**

The dashed red and blue lines are bounds on the contribution of both HT M and µHf, respectively.</figcaption>

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the decidability of the spectral gap problem for 1D quantum spin chains, building on prior work showing it could be undecidable. The authors demonstrate that this undecidability is highly unstable, proving that even an arbitrarily small, local perturbation can simplify the system such that determining if a gap exists becomes a computationally decidable problem.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly challenges the robustness of complexity results in condensed matter theory. Showing that undecidable behavior can be eliminated by a simple local term suggests that many theoretical barriers might be artifacts of specific, non-generic constructions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem addressed is determining whether the spectral gap (gapped vs. gapless) for a 1D lattice quantum spin system is decidable, contrasting with previous results showing undecidability.

**Main result.** The authors show that the previously constructed family of Hamiltonians exhibiting undecidable behavior is extremely fragile; adding any small, local perturbation renders the spectral gap problem decidable.

**Method.** The analysis involves constructing a specific 1-local, rank-1 perturbation and proving that this perturbation disrupts the fine-tuning energy coupling responsible for linking the spectral gap to an undecidable computation.

**Model / system.** The system is a 1D lattice quantum spin chain described by nearest-neighbor Hamiltonians. The analysis focuses on perturbing a specific Hamiltonian family previously shown to encode Universal Turing Machine computations.

**Key observables.** Spectral Gap ($\Delta$), Ground State Energy ($\lambda_0(H_N)$), and the sign of the ground state energy in the thermodynamic limit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The perturbation strength $\epsilon > 0$, which is shown to be sufficient to restore decidability regardless of its smallness.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The undecidability proofs rely on boundary-induced structures (like marker symbols) that are sensitive to local perturbations, while the new result assumes a general 1D nearest-neighbor Hamiltonian structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates the final spectrum of the perturbed Hamiltonian ($H_{\epsilon}^N$), showing distinct spectral features for both gapped and gapless scenarios.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews the undecidability result from [1], then details the energy amplification mechanism used in that construction, and finally introduces and analyzes a specific small perturbation to prove decidability by checking only up to a finite critical size $w_\epsilon$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The problem of determining the existence of a spectral gap in a lattice quantum spin system was previously shown to be undecidable for one [J. Bausch et al., "Undecidability of the spectral gap in one dimension", Physical Review X 10 (2020)] or more dimensions [T. S. Cubitt et al., "Undecidability of the spectral gap", Nature 528 (2015) and Forum of Mathematics Pi, 10 (2022)]. In this work, we focus on the 1-dimensional result, showing that the constructed family with undecidable behavior is extremely sensitive to perturbations. In particular, for any $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists a 1-local, rank 1, perturbation with norm $O(\varepsilon)$, such that the spectral gap problem for the family in [J. Bausch et al., "Undecidability of the spectral gap in one dimension", Physical Review X 10 (2020)] now becomes decidable.

</details>

**Summary.** This work examines grokking and double descent in Quantum Neural Networks using a 2-qubit model. The authors show that generalization relies on specific structural changes in the circuit weights, which can be mathematically tracked via Fourier analysis. By applying weak weight regularization, they provide a robust method to stabilize these quantum circuits after the initial generalization phase.

**Why it may be interesting.** It connects complex concepts like quantum circuit optimization and phase transitions (grokking) to rigorous theoretical frameworks from machine learning stability theory, providing insights into how physical constraints (like real coefficients) dictate optimal computational states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates grokking (delayed generalization) and epoch-wise double descent dynamics within Variational Quantum Machine Learning (QML). It seeks to understand the structural changes in quantum circuits that govern this transition.

**Main result.** Grokking is observed in a 2-qubit QNN, correlating with weight-norm increases away from sparse solutions. Weak explicit weight-norm regularization successfully stabilizes the post-grokking generalization phase.

**Method.** The study uses empirical observation on a 2-qubit QNN trained on concentric circle data and analyzes weight dynamics by tracking Fourier coefficients and algorithmic stability measures.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-qubit Quantum Neural Network (QNN) parameterized over the SU(4) manifold, trained to classify data points near an optimal decision boundary. The training involves minimizing a regularized loss function.

**Key observables.** Test error, weight-norm, algorithmic stability metric ($eta_{	ext{loo}}$), and Fourier coefficients ($c_{n1, n2}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth (overparameterization), learning rate, weight decay ($\lambda$), and the data margin ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares idealized mathematical constraints (e.g., purely real Fourier coefficients for step functions) with empirical training dynamics; some results rely on interpreting weight coefficient structures as indicators of generalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate characteristic grokking loss curves, the evolution of phase profiles in Fourier space across training stages, and comparisons of final test error distributions with and without regularization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from initial empirical observation of grokking/double descent to detailed analysis using algorithmic stability theory. It then uses mathematical tools (Fourier coefficients) to characterize the structural changes underlying generalization, culminating in proposing weight-norm regularization to stabilize the final state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Grokking, the delayed transition from memorization to generalization, is a fundamental phenomenon in gradient-based learning, yet its dynamics within variational quantum machine learning (QML) remain largely unexamined. In this work, we report the empirical observation of both the grokking transition and epoch-wise double descent in a two-qubit quantum neural network (QNN) under a complete parameterization of the SU(4) manifold. We demonstrate that overparameterization via increased circuit depth improves the probability of successful generalization. Notably, these architectures frequently exhibit an epoch-wise double descent in test error, degrading at a critical epoch before recovering into a generalizing state. Crucially, we identify a generalization decay in late-stage training, where the test error increases significantly despite a stagnant training loss. Bridging this behavior with algorithmic stability theory, our analysis reveals that this decay correlates with an unconstrained increase of the weight-norm, drifting away from sparse, phase-aligned harmonic solutions toward overfitted solutions in the Hilbert space. We analyze the underlying temporal dynamics of this transition, demonstrating how the onset of generalization is linked to optimization hyperparameters such as learning rate and weight decay. Finally, to mitigate late-stage decay, we introduce a weak explicit weight-norm regularization into the loss function. We demonstrate that this structural anchor stabilizes the post-grokking phase and permanently preserves generalization gains, providing a robust framework for training overparameterized quantum circuits.

</details>
