**Summary.** This paper investigates methods to enhance magic state generation, crucial for fault-tolerant quantum computing using bosonic systems. By applying error correction techniques designed for Gaussian states to simple non-Gaussian inputs like Fock or cat states, the authors demonstrate a significant improvement in resulting state quality. This research suggests that resource requirements can be substantially lowered by moving beyond purely Gaussian assumptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses resource optimization in quantum computation by showing that incorporating structured non-Gaussian resources can outperform standard Gaussian assumptions used in current theoretical models for bosonic QEC.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to improve the quality of magic states, which are essential resources for achieving fault-tolerant universal quantum computation in bosonic systems.

**Main result.** Applying error correction techniques designed for Gaussian states to simple non-Gaussian input states (like cat or Fock states) significantly boosts magic state fidelity, potentially reducing distillation resource requirements by a factor of three.

**Method.** The authors utilize the framework of Gottesman--Kitaev--Preskill (GKP) codes to encode logical information and then apply error correction protocols to non-Gaussian input states to generate high-fidelity magic states via distillation.

**Model / system.** The work is set in bosonic quantum computation, employing GKP codes within an infinite-dimensional bosonic Hilbert space. The analysis focuses on superpositions of coherent or Fock states as the input resources for error correction.

**Key observables.** Magic state fidelity (purity), required distillation cost (resource reduction factor), and Bloch vector coefficients characterizing the logical state output.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The improvement factor achieved in resource requirements (e.g., a factor of 3); the specific structure of non-Gaussian input states (superpositions of coherent/Fock states).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on applying established error correction frameworks to novel, simple non-Gaussian inputs; it assumes that avoiding projection near stabilizer states is key to improvement.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Bloch sphere and the stabilizer octahedron formed by Pauli eigenstates. Figure 2 depicts the GKP error-correction gadget, showing how an arbitrary input state is projected into the logical code space.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon existing knowledge of gate teleportation and magic state distillation in bosonic systems. It introduces the methodology for using non-Gaussian states within the GKP framework to calculate improved fidelity metrics compared to purely Gaussian inputs, concluding with an analysis of the physical origin of these improvements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gate teleportation, together with magic state distillation, is a promising route towards fault-tolerant, universal computation. In the context of bosonic quantum computation, Baragiola et al. PRL 123(20).200502 (2019) showed that within the framework of Gottesman--Kitaev--Preskill codes, encoded magic states suitable for distillation can be produced by error correcting Gaussian states, such as the vacuum. Here, we show that applying the same framework to simple non-Gaussian input states can significantly improve the quality of the magic states obtained, reducing the overall resources for the complete distillation procedure. We focus on superpositions of coherent states or Fock states, showing that many can lead to improvements in the generation of high-quality encoded magic states, which in some cases reduces the resources required for magic state distillation by about a factor $3$. We also investigate the primary source of these improvements and find that, unlike what was previously conjectured, the suitability of input states is not fully explained by symmetry arguments. Instead, the best states seem to avoid projection near stabilizer states as a result of the error correction procedure.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses advanced machine learning interatomic potentials to simulate massive amorphous indium oxide structures. It reveals that structural features like edge-sharing polyhedra and peroxide-like O-O bonds are responsible for the material's high electron mobility and intrinsic n-type doping. The findings provide a detailed structure-property roadmap for improving its use in energy devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the use of machine learning potentials trained on first principles to simulate large disordered systems falls directly into the realm of ML-physics applied to complex materials, which is relevant for understanding emergent properties in disordered quantum matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to resolve the structural motifs responsible for the high electron mobility and the existence of peroxide-like bonds in amorphous indium oxide (a-In2O3).

**Main result.** The research establishes a comprehensive structure-property picture, showing that extended InOx chains and O-O peroxide-like bonds provide the basis for high electron mobility and intrinsic n-type doping.

**Method.** A MACE-based machine-learned interatomic potential was developed and used to simulate large amorphous supercells (up to 5120 atoms) via first-principles molecular dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is amorphous indium oxide (a-In2O3), a high-electron-mobility semiconductor crucial for thin-film transistors. The simulations model the structural disorder and defect chemistry inherent in the amorphous state.

**Key observables.** X-ray structure factors, partial radial distribution functions (RDFs), vibrational spectra (Raman/IR), localized in-gap electronic states near the conduction band minimum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mean O-O bond length of 1.5 Å; supercell sizes up to 5120 atoms; energy differences between amorphous and crystalline phases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of a MACE potential trained on first-principles data assumes the accuracy of the underlying DFT functionals (SCAN meta-GGA).

**Figures summary.** Figures likely compare calculated X-ray structure factors to experimental data, show RDFs confirming bond lengths, and display Raman spectra highlighting a distinct feature near 850 cm-1 associated with O-O bonds.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first developing the large-scale ML potential, then using it for structural characterization (RDFs, structure factors), followed by electronic analysis to identify defect states, and finally correlating these structural/electronic findings with measurable optical signatures like Raman scattering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Amorphous indium oxide (a-In$_2$O$_3$) is a high-electron-mobility semiconductor of central importance in thin-film transistors and a promising photoanode for solar-driven water oxidation. Despite sustained experimental and computational investigations, the structural motifs underlying its unusual transport properties and the existence of O-O peroxide-like bonds within its network have remained unresolved. Here we develop a MACE-based machine-learned interatomic potential trained on first-principles molecular dynamics trajectories and use it to generate and analyze amorphous structures containing up to 5120 atoms, two orders of magnitude larger than those adopted in typical ab initio studies. We find X-ray structure factors in excellent quantitative agreement with experiment and we confirm that In$_2$O$_3$ is a poor glass former, with the likely presence of quasi-crystalline regions in amorphous samples. Our large-scale structural analysis reveals extended chains of edge-sharing InO$_k$ polyhedra providing a concrete structural basis for the high electron mobility of a-In$_2$O$_3$. Our results strongly support the formation of O-O peroxide-like bonds in the amorphous network, with a mean length of 1.5 Å. We show that these bonds introduce localized in-gap states near the conduction band minimum, acting as a source of intrinsic n-type self-doping and enhancing sub-gap optical absorption. These effects are detectable via a distinct Raman feature near 850 cm$^{-1}$ that is absent in the IR spectrum. Overall, our results establish a comprehensive structure-property picture of a-In$_2$O$_3$, provide directly testable experimental predictions, and suggest that controlled amorphization is a viable strategy for improving the photoelectrochemical activity of a-In$_2$O$_3$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a quantitative theory showing that universal quantum state purification requires a specific amount of 'magic' resource. It proves exact linear laws connecting this required magic (measured by mana or robustness) directly to the achievable fidelity gain beyond what is possible classically. This establishes a fundamental, measurable resource constraint for fault-tolerant quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The quantitative link between fundamental resource measures (magic/nonstabilizerness) and achievable performance metrics like fidelity is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols, providing rigorous theoretical limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a quantitative resource law governing quantum state purification, aiming to determine the minimum 'magic' (nonstabilizerness) required to achieve high fidelity from noisy copies.

**Main result.** It proves exact linear laws relating the required mana (for odd dimensions) and robustness (for multi-qubit systems) to the achievable fidelity gain over the unpurified state.

**Method.** The analysis uses resource theories, specifically defining 'mana' and 'robustness,' and employs advanced mathematical techniques involving Semidefinite Programming formulations and structural decomposition of stabilizer states.

**Model / system.** The model involves purifying a quantum state $|\psiangle$ corrupted by depolarizing noise ($	ext{D}_\delta$) using $n=2$ noisy copies. The analysis covers both odd-dimensional qudit systems and general multi-qubit systems.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($f$), Success Probability ($p$), Mana ($	ext{M}$), Robustness ($	ext{R}$), and the fidelity gain ($f - \lambda_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of copies ($n=2$), Noise parameter ($\delta$), System dimension ($d$ or qubit count).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of depolarizing noise and focuses on probabilistic purification protocols modeled by CPTN maps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how magic resources govern purification performance, showing linear growth of mana and robustness bounds with the fidelity gain ($f - \lambda_0$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem using resource measures (mana/robustness) for two-copy purification. It then proves the exact linear law for odd dimensions (mana) and derives analogous bounding laws for multi-qubit systems (robustness), culminating in explicit bounds derived from stabilizer state analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum state purification aims to recover higher-fidelity quantum states from multiple noisy copies and is a fundamental primitive for quantum information processing. Magic resources enable operations beyond classically simulable dynamics and are central to universal fault-tolerant quantum computation. Recent no-go results show that classically simulable operations cannot achieve a nontrivial universal fidelity gain. This motivates a quantitative theory of the magic required for purification at prescribed success probability and target fidelity. For universal purification with two input copies, we prove an exact linear mana law in odd dimensions and a two-sided linear robustness law for multi-qubit systems, which becomes exact for a single qubit. We also identify an explicit successful purification map that makes the tradeoff transparent. These results establish universal purification as a task obeying a quantitative magic-fidelity law and link magic resources to error mitigation and fault-tolerant quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) for predicting complex chaotic systems. It rigorously tests this approach against classical methods by analyzing the stability and conditioning of the learned feature space as complexity increases. The results demonstrate that QRC maintains superior, stable performance metrics compared to its classical counterparts.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, quantifiable diagnostic tool—the condition number analysis—to validate the utility of high-dimensional feature spaces generated by quantum circuits in physical modeling tasks like chaotic forecasting.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to use Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) for forecasting chaotic dynamical systems while rigorously diagnosing whether the high dimensionality of the quantum feature space provides a genuine advantage over classical methods.

**Main result.** On two chaotic test systems, QRC maintained a stable prediction error and superior feature conditioning compared to matched classical reservoirs as system size grew. This confirms that the quantum architecture's structure yields better stability in the learned feature space.

**Method.** The authors implement QRC using fixed quantum circuits for feature generation ($\phi_q(x_t)$) and train a linear readout via ridge regression. They employ a matched-complexity scaling protocol to ensure fair comparison with classical Echo State Networks (ESN).

**Model / system.** The methods are tested on chaotic dynamical systems, specifically the Lorenz-96 model and shallow-water fluid equations (SWE) simulated on large grids.

**Key observables.** Prediction Mean Squared Error (MSE), the condition number ($\kappa$) of the feature Gram matrix ($F^	op F + \alpha I$), and stability across increasing system/reservoir dimensions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The comparison is made by growing both the prediction problem size ($K$) and the quantum reservoir dimension ($q$) together, ensuring $K=q$ for matched complexity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes noiseless statevector evaluation; the paper also notes that the condition number bound controls conditioning but not the inherent bias of the ridge estimator.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare QRC vs. ESN test MSE (QRC flat, ESN oscillates) and plot the feature-Gram condition number ($\kappa$): $\kappa_{	ext{QRC}}$ saturates near 1 while $\kappa_{	ext{ESN}}$ grows significantly with dimension.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces QRC for chaotic forecasting. It then details the diagnostic protocol, comparing its spectral properties (condition number) against classical ESNs across varying dimensions using L96 and SWE models. Finally, it presents results showing superior stability and conditioning for the quantum approach.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum reservoir computing uses a fixed quantum circuit as a feature generator and trains only a simple linear readout on top of it. This makes it cheap to train and free of the optimisation problems that affect many quantum machine-learning models. A natural worry is that the very large feature space the circuit produces might inflate apparent performance without adding anything real. This paper provides two things. First, it gives a complete, reproducible recipe for one such reservoir applied to forecasting chaotic systems, including how data is fed in, how the circuit is built, and how the readout is trained. Second, it gives a way to tell whether the reservoir's high dimension is actually doing useful work. We grow the size of the prediction problem and the size of the quantum reservoir together, so that extra capacity cannot be the explanation for any improvement, and we track a single stability number that measures how well behaved the readout fit is. On two chaotic test systems, a spatiotemporal chain and a shallow-water fluid model, the quantum reservoir keeps a flat, stable error as both sizes grow, while a matched classical reservoir does not. We report where the classical baseline is in fact stronger, so the comparison is honest. The result is a clean specification plus a diagnostic that other groups can apply to any reservoir whose features have a known scale.

</details>

Naturally, the larger the number of terms becomes, the longer it will take to process each block of circuit instructions. With p-mass truncation, the situation is different. Here, the state vector is truncated after each block (as opposed to top-k truncation, where it is allowed to grow freely up to a point). However, if the...</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper presents an efficient classical simulation method for quantum circuits, particularly those with peaked output distributions. It achieves efficiency by using a sparse representation of the state vector and implementing controlled truncation via top-k or p-mass limits. This allows users to trade off computational resources against desired accuracy when exact simulation is infeasible.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, resource-aware numerical technique for simulating quantum dynamics that goes beyond standard tensor network methods by directly managing state vector sparsity based on physical probability concentration, which is relevant for understanding complex many-body states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To simulate quantum circuits efficiently when the state vector size grows exponentially, which exceeds classical computational memory.

**Main result.** The authors developed an open-source implementation that successfully simulates peaked circuits by combining sparse representation with controlled truncation (top-k or p-mass). This allows resource management while retaining high fidelity for specific circuit types.

**Method.** The simulation uses a sparse state vector representation and applies unitary operations in blocks, followed by mandatory truncation based on either the maximum number of non-zero terms ($k$) or the minimum retained probability mass ($p$).

**Model / system.** The model is general quantum circuits applied to an $n$-qubit system. The focus is on 'peaked circuits' where the output probability distribution is highly concentrated, allowing for significant state vector sparsity.

**Key observables.** Fidelity (approximated by retained total probability mass) and computational resources (runtime/memory usage controlled by $k$ or $p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The truncation parameters ($k$ for top-k, $p$ for p-mass), the number of qubits ($n$), and the structure of the unitary gates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the observable quantities are dominated by a small subset of basis states, allowing for effective truncation. The method can fail if probability mass is spread widely despite having a clear peak.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling of simulation time with $k$ (linear scaling) and show how the number of active terms grows during simulation under different truncation regimes ($k$ vs. $p$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the problem of state vector growth, detailing the sparse representation, proposing the combined top-k/p-mass truncation strategy, describing the optimized implementation (vectorization, GPU acceleration), and finally analyzing performance scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a class of quantum circuits known as peaked circuits, the goal is to predict the most probable bit string at the output of the circuit. Since these circuits are designed to have a sharp peak in their output distribution, in principle it should be possible to simulate them using a truncated state vector with a limited number of terms, or a fraction of the total probability mass. This approximate simulation can be carried out on a classical computer with a sparse representation that stores only the nonzero amplitudes of the state vector, in contrast to the dense representations that are common in most quantum simulators. For efficiency, all operations on the state vector should be vectorized to the furthest possible extent and, if available, hardware acceleration can also be used. This work describes how these requirements were met in an open-source implementation, and discusses its performance and limitations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides an exact solution for the free energy of the Ising model on random regular graphs using advanced analytic combinatorics. It rigorously proves that the system undergoes a second-order phase transition at a specific critical temperature, confirming mean-field critical exponents. The methodology involves mapping the partition function to counting weighted edge-bicolored graphs in the thermodynamic limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical tools developed—analytic combinatorics applied to large random structures—are highly relevant for analyzing complex quantum many-body systems where exact enumeration is intractable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to find an exact solution for the free energy of the ferromagnetic Ising model defined on a random regular graph ensemble.

**Main result.** The model exhibits a second-order phase transition at a critical temperature $T_c$, and the critical exponents ($\alpha=0, eta_{mag}=1/2, \gamma=1, \delta=3$) match those of the mean-field universality class.

**Method.** The authors use analytic combinatorics by expressing the partition function as a generating function for labeled edge-bicolored graphs and calculating its asymptotic enumeration in the thermodynamic limit.

**Model / system.** The system is the Ising model ($	ext{Z}_2$ spin system) defined on a random $k$-regular graph ensemble. The Hamiltonian includes ferromagnetic coupling ($J>0$) and an external magnetic field ($h$).

**Key observables.** Free energy in the thermodynamic limit, spontaneous magnetization $m_0(eta)$, and critical exponents ($\alpha, eta_{mag}, \gamma, \delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature $eta$, coupling constant $J$, external field $h$, and graph degree $k$. The critical point is defined by $eta_{	ext{tree}}^c = rac{1}{2J} \log\left(rac{k}{k-2}ight)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation yields an *annealed* free energy, and the analysis relies on identifying a unique global maximum of auxiliary functions derived from asymptotic enumeration.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows magnetization vs. external field for fixed $eta$. Figure 2 illustrates spontaneous magnetization ($m_0$) versus inverse temperature ($eta$), clearly marking the second-order phase transition at $eta_{	ext{tree}}^c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the partition function and using high-temperature expansions to deriving an identity involving subgraphs. It then uses asymptotic enumeration techniques (saddle point approximation) on these graph counts to determine the free energy in the thermodynamic limit, culminating in the calculation of critical exponents.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give an exact solution of the ferromagnetic Ising model on a random regular graph ensemble via analytic combinatorics. Expressing the partition function as the generating function of labeled edge-bicolored graphs, we obtain the free energy in the thermodynamic limit from the asymptotic enumeration of these graphs. A simple analysis of the resulting formula reveals a second-order phase transition with critical exponents of the mean-field universality class.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel Continuous-Variable Quantum Secret Sharing protocol leveraging a MIMO architecture operating in the THz band to overcome the limitations of point-to-point QKD. By decomposing the channel via beamforming and analyzing both Gaussian and passive modulation schemes, it derives theoretical benchmarks showing significant improvements in key rate and transmission distance for multi-user quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work pushes quantum communication into high-frequency regimes (THz) using advanced MIMO techniques, which is crucial for realizing practical quantum networks in challenging wireless environments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the limitation of Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) being point-to-point by proposing a multi-user Quantum Secret Sharing (QSS) protocol.

**Main result.** The proposed MIMO architecture significantly improves both the achievable Secret Key Rate (SKR) and transmission distance compared to conventional SISO schemes in the THz band.

**Method.** It utilizes transmit-receive beamforming to decompose the MIMO channel into parallel SISO subchannels, analyzing eight protocol variants under Gaussian collective attacks.

**Model / system.** The system is a Continuous-Variable (CV) QSS protocol operating over an atmospheric channel using a Multiple-Input Multiple-Output (MIMO) architecture in the Terahertz (THz) band. It compares Gaussian Modulation (GMCS) and Passive Modulation (PMCS).

**Key observables.** Secret Key Rate (SKR), transmission distance, and minimum net secure key bits shared per MIMO-QSS round.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Antenna configurations ($32 	imes 32$ or $1024 	imes 1024$), atmospheric channel parameters, and finite-size error correction probabilities ($\epsilon_{	ext{cor}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Simulation results assume ideal conditions like perfect Channel State Information (CSI), phase synchronization, and beamforming; the analysis also incorporates thermal noise for physical realism.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the GMCS MIMO THz-QSS protocol with AM/PM modulators. Figure 2 illustrates the data partitioning process into parameter estimation, SKR calculation, and key generation stages across SISO subchannels.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the need for multi-user QSS over MIMO channels. It details the transmission procedure involving beamforming, modulation (GMCS/PMCS), detection (homodyne/heterodyne), and subsequent classical post-processing steps to derive SKR expressions. Finally, it derives formulas for composable finite-size SKRs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Although quantum key distribution (QKD) enables information-theoretically secure key distribution, it is mainly designed for point-to-point communication and cannot directly support multi-user collaborative scenarios. To address this limitation, quantum secret sharing (QSS) has been proposed to enable secure multiparty key sharing. However, most existing QSS protocols rely on a single-input single-output (SISO) channel, which limits the achievable secret key rate (SKR) and transmission distance. This paper proposes a continuous-variable (CV) QSS protocol based on a multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) architecture operating in the terahertz (THz) band. In the proposed scheme, transmit-receive beamforming decomposes the MIMO channel into multiple parallel SISO subchannels, thereby improving both the SKR and transmission distance. We describe the QSS transmission procedure and derive the SKR expressions for eight protocol variants under Gaussian collective attacks. Specifically, Gaussian modulation and passive modulation are considered at the transmitter, while homodyne and heterodyne detection are considered at the receiver. Both asymptotic and composable finite-size SKR formulas are derived to characterize the ideal upper-bound performance and the achievable performance under finite resources, respectively. Simulation results show that, under ideal assumptions including perfect channel state information, perfect phase synchronization, and ideal beamforming, the Gaussian-modulation protocol with a 32 x 32 antenna configuration and the passive-modulation protocol with a 1024 x 1024 antenna configuration achieve transmission distances of 14.99 m and 160 m in the atmospheric channel, respectively. These results provide an idealized theoretical benchmark for evaluating the potential performance gains of MIMO-assisted THz CV-QSS in indoor and short-range outdoor wireless networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Equivariant Quantum Clustering (EQC), a novel framework designed to perform clustering on highly sensitive data while rigorously maintaining differential privacy. It achieves state-of-the-art performance by combining symmetry-aware parameter sharing in quantum circuits with formal DP guarantees across multiple heterogeneous datasets.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the primary focus is data privacy/ML, the use of symmetry constraints ($p4m$) within a quantum circuit architecture provides an interesting case study in applying group theory principles to variational quantum algorithms for structured regularization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is balancing high analytical utility with stringent data confidentiality when clustering sensitive, heterogeneous datasets.

**Main result.** The proposed framework achieves superior clustering accuracy (e.g., 79.3% on NSL-KDD) while significantly reducing privacy leakage compared to classical and quantum baselines under strict differential privacy guarantees.

**Method.** It integrates symmetry-aware equivariant quantum circuits with rigorous differential privacy mechanisms across a multi-stage pipeline for secure data analysis.

**Model / system.** The model is the Equivariant Quantum Clustering (EQC) framework, utilizing parameterized quantum circuits operating on qubits arranged in a $2	imes4$ grid topology to enforce $p4m$ symmetry constraints.

**Key observables.** Clustering accuracy, success rate of membership inference attacks (MIA), model inversion reconstruction error (MSE), and attribute inference error rate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Privacy budget ($\epsilon=1.0, \delta=10^{-5}$), noise standard deviation ($\sigma$), circuit depth ($L=4$), and optimization hyperparameters (e.g., $a=0.1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed performance gains are primarily attributed to parameter efficiency via equivariance constraints and DP composition, rather than unique quantum mechanical effects requiring fault-tolerant hardware.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate EQC's superior MIA resistance (lowest rate) and model inversion robustness compared to baselines; Figure 10 shows gradual accuracy degradation under noise for EQC versus faster drops for competitors.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the problem setup, introduces the five-stage EQC pipeline (preprocessing $ightarrow$ quantum encoding $ightarrow$ equivariant circuit $ightarrow$ kernel computation $ightarrow$ spectral clustering), presents results across multiple datasets, and concludes by analyzing performance trade-offs against various attacks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Privacy-preserving clustering is critical for analyzing sensitive data in healthcare, cybersecurity, and enterprise applications, where maintaining data confidentiality must be balanced with analytical performance. This paper presents Equivariant Quantum Clustering (EQC), a parameter-efficient framework that integrates symmetry-aware quantum circuits with differential privacy to improve the privacy-utility tradeoff. EQC employs p4m equivariant parameter sharing to reduce circuit complexity while preserving informative feature representations. The framework is evaluated on three privacy-sensitive datasets: NSL-KDD, CERT Insider Threat v6.2, and a synthetic MIMIC-III clinical dataset. On the NSL-KDD benchmark, EQC achieves 79.3% clustering accuracy while reducing membership inference attack success to 38.3% under a privacy budget of ε = 1.0 and δ = 10^-5, outperforming representative classical and quantum baselines. Ablation studies indicate that the performance gains primarily arise from parameter-efficient circuit design combined with differential privacy. The results demonstrate that EQC provides a practical quantum-ready framework for secure and privacy-preserving clustering across heterogeneous sensitive datasets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a novel quantum simulation technique, ADAPT-SSVQE, designed to solve the intractable problem of diagonalizing large nuclear shell model Hamiltonians. By mapping the fermionic Hamiltonian to qubits and using an adaptive variational approach, the authors can simultaneously find multiple excited states for small nucleon systems with high accuracy. This demonstrates a powerful pathway toward simulating complex quantum many-body physics beyond classical computational limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on nuclear physics, the core methodology—using advanced quantum algorithms like ADAPT-SSVQE to tackle exponentially hard many-body problems while enforcing symmetries—is directly transferable to simulating strongly correlated electron systems or complex molecular Hamiltonians in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the exponential dimensionality of the nuclear shell model Hamiltonian diagonalization, which exceeds classical computational capacity.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a quantum simulation approach that simultaneously extracts multiple orthogonal eigenstates for two and three nucleons with spectroscopic accuracy relative to exact diagonalization.

**Method.** They employ an ADAPT-SSVQE algorithm, combining the Subspace Search VQE with the ADAPT ansatz, applied after mapping the fermionic Hamiltonian using the Jordan-Wigner transformation.

**Model / system.** The system is based on the nuclear shell model Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_{	ext{eff}}$) describing protons and neutrons interacting via a residual interaction (e.g., JUN45). The problem is mapped onto qubits while preserving fermionic anti-commutation relations.

**Key observables.** Simultaneous convergence of multiple mutually orthogonal eigenstates, restoration of total angular momentum ($\hat{J}^2$) symmetry, and energy eigenvalues compared to exact diagonalization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The simulation was benchmarked for two and three identical nucleons in a $j = 9/2$ orbital within a 10-qubit active space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation addressed is the exponential scaling of the Hilbert space; the method assumes quantum computation can scale beyond classical limits, though circuit depth remains a concern for NISQ devices.

**Figures summary.** Figures track energy convergence and relative error over simulation iterations for two and three nucleons. They also show the growth of CNOT gate count, illustrating the increasing complexity of the required quantum circuits.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework by mapping the fermionic Hamiltonian to qubits (JW transformation). It then introduces and implements the ADAPT-SSVQE algorithm for simultaneous state extraction, culminating in benchmarking results for 2 and 3 nucleons showing convergence and symmetry restoration.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The nuclear shell model is a general way of expressing the many-body nuclear Hamiltonian and deciphering the underlying nuclear structure. In today's era of modern and high-power computation, the primary limitation of the nuclear shell model is the enormous dimensionality of its Hilbert space, which far exceeds available storage capacity and prevents the diagonalization of the full Hamiltonian matrix in that space. Quantum computing offers a scalable solution to bypass this curse of dimensionality. In this work, we introduce a single-run quantum simulation capable of obtaining multiple shell-model eigenstates simultaneously. The nuclear Hamiltonian is transformed from a bit to a qubit basis using the Jordan-Wigner transformation, explicitly preserving fermionic anti-commutation. We employ a Subspace Search Variational Quantum Eigensolver (SSVQE) along with an Adaptive Derivative-Assembled Pseudo-Trotter (ADAPT) ansatz to construct the quantum circuit required to solve the shell-model problem. The ADAPT-SSVQE algorithm uses a symmetry-preserving single and double-excitation operator pool and optimizes a weighted energy sum to obtain the simultaneous convergence of all eigenstates within a targeted MJ subspace, eliminating the need for post-processing efforts to extract excited spectra. We benchmark this approach by solving the problem for two and three identical nucleons in a j = 9/2 orbital, successfully extracting five and ten mutually orthogonal states, respectively, within a 10-qubit active space. The algorithm achieves spectroscopic accuracy, in simulation, relative to exact diagonalization and intrinsically restores total angular momentum (\hat{J}^2) symmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a new pathway for ultra-low power magnetic switching by utilizing interfacial chirality in vdW heterostructures. By generating two unconventional spin-orbit torques, the system achieves field-free magnetization reversal with minimal energy loss. This establishes interfacial chirality as a powerful design handle for next-generation spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter spintronics rather than fundamental quantum dynamics, the concept of using symmetry breaking (chirality) to engineer novel, highly controllable quantum phenomena at interfaces is relevant to understanding emergent behavior in complex quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to demonstrate highly energy-efficient, magnetic-field-free switching of perpendicular magnetization using interfacial chirality effects in all van der Waals (vdW) heterostructures.

**Main result.** Magnetic-field-free switching was achieved with ultralow current densities ($\sim 10^6 	ext{ A/cm}^2$) by exploiting two unconventional spin-orbit torques originating from the interface's chirality. Replacing one material allowed this effect to persist up to room temperature.

**Method.** The study combines experimental transport measurements (measuring $R_{xy}$ vs. writing current) with detailed symmetry analysis of the resulting spin-orbit torque terms.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an all vdW heterostructure, specifically $	ext{Fe}_3	ext{GeTe}_2/	ext{Co}_{1/3}	ext{TaS}_2$. The key physics relies on the structural chirality at the interface, which generates unique torques beyond what bulk symmetry predicts.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall resistance ($R_{xy}$), critical switching current density ($J_{sw}$), and power dissipation ($rac{J_{sw}^2}{\sigma}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultralow current densities ($\sim 10^6 	ext{ A/cm}^2$) and minimal power dissipation ($< 10^{15} 	ext{ W/m}^3$); switching observed across a temperature range including room temperature with material substitution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed phenomena are uniquely attributed to the breaking of mirror plane symmetries (interfacial chirality) leading to unconventional spin-orbit torques.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device structure, temperature dependence of resistance kinks and anomalous Hall effect, and crucially, hysteresis loops showing magnetization switching occurring solely under an applied current with zero external magnetic field. The symmetry analysis details the three-fold dependence of the torque on writing current direction.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system (vdW heterostructure), theoretically derives unconventional spin-orbit torques based on interfacial chirality, and then presents experimental evidence showing robust, low-power magnetic-field-free switching across various temperatures and material variations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chirality, a central concept across many scientific disciplines, continues to inspire the discovery of novel physical phenomena. In condensed matter physics, structural chirality - defined by the absence of mirror plane symmetries - has primarily been explored in bulk materials. However, new chiral phenomena can emerge uniquely at the interface, distinct from their bulk counterparts, when a chiral material forms a heterostructure. Here, we demonstrate that all van-der-Waals (vdW) heterostructure composed of the chiral Co1/3TaS2 and the achiral vdW ferromagnet Fe3GeTe2 exhibits two distinct and unconventional spin-orbit torques originating from the interfacial chirality. These torques enable magnetic-field-free switching of perpendicular magnetization with ultralow current density ~ 10^6 A/cm^2 and minimal power dissipation < 10^15 W/m^3. Moreover, by replacing Fe3GeTe2 with a similar vdW ferromagnet, Fe3GaTe2, but of higher Curie temperature, we achieved the magnetic-field-free switching at room temperature in the Fe3GaTe2/Co1/3TaS2 vdW heterostructure. Our findings establish interfacial chirality as a powerful new handle for spintronic control, opening a new pathway to explore chirality-induced phenomena beyond the bulk symmetry constraints - and paving the way toward highly efficient, low-power spintronic devices based on all-vdW heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper surveys how quantum computing can be applied to solve computationally hard problems arising from Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) in particle collider physics. It outlines algorithms for tasks like event generation and amplitude calculation, contrasting the potential advantages of QC against current classical limitations. While acknowledging that NISQ hardware is a hurdle, it positions QC as a necessary tool to achieve the required precision for future high-luminosity experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** It frames high-energy physics calculations as an application area for quantum computation, suggesting that fundamental quantum principles (superposition, entanglement) are key to overcoming classical computational barriers in QFT simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The theoretical predictions for particle interactions, particularly within QCD, are struggling to keep pace with the unprecedented data collection capabilities of colliders like the HL-LHC.

**Main result.** Quantum computing offers a promising framework to tackle computationally intensive tasks in high-energy physics, though current hardware limitations restrict immediate full utilization.

**Method.** The paper surveys various quantum algorithms proposed for simulating QCD processes, emphasizing hybrid quantum-classical approaches due to NISQ constraints.

**Model / system.** The focus is on Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) describing particle interactions at colliders. The theoretical bottleneck limits the ability to reduce uncertainties below 2%-4% required by future experiments.

**Key observables.** Projected experimental uncertainties for Higgs boson couplings ($\sim 1.5\%–1.8\%$); theoretical uncertainties for gluon and top quark couplings ($2.1\%$ and $3.1\%$, respectively).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The current hardware is in the Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era, contrasting with the long-term goal of Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computing (FTQC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Classical simulations may be intrinsically inefficient for genuinely quantum features like interference and entanglement; short-term focus must remain on hybrid methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper provides a concise overview, detailing the potential applications of QC in event generation, parton shower simulation, amplitude evaluation, integration, and optimization problems relevant to collider physics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computing has emerged as a promising framework for addressing computationally demanding problems in collider physics. In recent years, a growing number of quantum algorithms have been proposed for applications ranging from event generation and parton shower simulation to the evaluation of scattering amplitudes, loop and phase-space integration, and optimization problems relevant to experimental analysis. We provide a concise overview of the main ideas behind these developments, with emphasis on the potential advantages of quantum approaches in comparison with classical methods, as well as on the current limitations imposed by noisy intermediate-scale quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a powerful 'twofold universality' result for integrals involving large-$N$ melonic random tensors. It shows that these complex tensor integrals simplify significantly at large $N$, depending only on simple polynomial functions of the graph structure, regardless of the specific details of the interaction or dimension. This provides a universal framework for calculating correlation functions in high-dimensional statistical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of universal behavior emerging from complex combinatorial structures (tensors/graphs) at large $N$ is highly relevant in statistical mechanics, particularly when studying critical phenomena or random matrix theories generalized to higher dimensions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors investigate constructing a measure exhibiting 'twofold universality' for integrating large-$N$ random tensors, which are generalizations of matrix integrals.

**Main result.** They prove that these tensor integrals simplify dramatically at large $N$, becoming independent of intricate combinatorics and the dimensionality $D$ (for $D\geq 3$), depending only on 'melonic polynomials'.

**Method.** The analysis involves computing connected correlation functions using Wick contractions ($\pi$) over graph structures, establishing uniqueness theorems for maximal contractions, and comparing cumulants across different theoretical models.

**Model / system.** The model is based on random tensors $T_{a_1,\ldots,a_D}$ integrated with respect to a Gaussian measure. The complexity arises from the need to contract indices using graph structures called 'melonic graphs' ($B$), which are analogues of matrix traces.

**Key observables.** Melonic polynomials (functions indexed by $n$ related to vertex counts), and the large-$N$ limit of tensor cumulants $\langle B_1 \dots B_n angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large $N$, dimensionality $D\geq 3$, and coupling constants ($g_i$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the tensors are 'melonic' (satisfying a recursive construction) and relies on proving convergence of ratios of cumulants to establish universality.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate graph structures, such as gluings of $2p$-agons analogous to matrix integrals, and show diagrams related to critical loci and two-point functions for the quartic tensor model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem (tensor integration), establishing tools like maximal Wick contractions ($\pi_{	ext{max}}$) via graph theory, proving universality theorems (Theorem 4.2 and 5.1), and finally applying these results to confirm the 'twofold universality' of the large-$N$ limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a measure that exhibits two aspects of a new type of universality and dramatically simplifies the integration of tensors $T_{a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_D} \in \mathbb{C}$ ($a_1,\ldots,a_D=1,\ldots,N$) at large $N$. In contrast to matrix integration, in which matrix traces canonically yield the integrand, tensors need additional information (equivalent to a $D$-coloured graph $B$) to contract their indices and form a tensor trace $B(T)$. We show that, whenever each $B_1,\ldots, B_n$ can be obtained by a recursive construction known as melonicity, then the leading order in $N$ of the integral of $ {B_1}(T) {B_2}(T) \cdots {B_n}(T) $ is independent of the -- often intricate -- combinatorics of the traces $B_i$, but also, to our surprise, independent of $D$ as far as $D\geq 3$. Instead, at large $N$, these integrals are some functions (indexed by $n$) of the number of vertices $2p_i$ of $B_i$ which we call melonic polynomials. Melonic traces cumulants with respect to any ('interacting') measure \[ \exp\Big\{-N^{D-1} \sum_{i=1}^m g_i {B_i}(T)\Big\} \mathrm{d}μ_0(T) \quad (g_1,\ldots,g_m \in \mathbb{R}, \mathrm{d}μ_0(T) =\text{the tensor Gaussian}) \] with each $B_i$ melonic, can be computed with our universal measure that replaces each $B_i$ by a canonical trace depending only on $p_i$. We prove that any two melonic tensor models are indistinguishable at large-$N$, independently of the number of tensor indices (first universality aspect), and of the fine-grainedness of their interactions (second universality), being a sufficient condition that the couplings (the parameters $g_i$ above) agree and their respective traces are monomials with the same degree in $T$.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that self-attention neural networks can generate highly accurate variational wavefunctions for the 2D homogeneous electron gas. By applying this method to large systems (up to N=169), they achieve energies competitive with state-of-the-art DMC and accurately recover complex collective excitations like plasmons and rotons, proving scalability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work pushes the boundary of computational quantum chemistry by applying advanced machine learning techniques (self-attention) to solve fundamental problems in strongly correlated electron systems, offering a scalable alternative to traditional high-accuracy methods like DMC.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is determining if self-attention wavefunctions can maintain high accuracy and scalability when applied to large quantum systems, surpassing traditional variational ansatze.

**Main result.** The method successfully yields systematically lower energies than state-of-the-art DMC for up to 169 particles in the 2D HEG, demonstrating convergence towards the thermodynamic limit.

**Method.** Variational Monte Carlo (VMC) is employed using a self-attention neural network architecture to generate highly expressive variational wavefunctions ($\psi_	heta$).

**Model / system.** The study models the two-dimensional homogeneous electron gas (HEG) of spin-polarized electrons, governed by a standard Coulomb interaction Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy ($E$), momentum distribution $n(\mathbf{k})$, static structure factor $S(q)$, and collective mode dispersion $\epsilon(q)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System sizes up to $N=169$ particles, interaction strength controlled by the dimensionless density parameter $r_s$ (e.g., $r_s=30$ or $r_s=40$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The wavefunction is constructed as an antisymmetric fermionic determinant using generalized orbitals derived from the self-attention mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the self-attention architecture, momentum occupation $n(k)$ showing metallic behavior, and structure factor $S(q)$ revealing plasmon and roton features in the liquid phase.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the self-attention approach for wavefunctions, applies it to the HEG Hamiltonian, calculates ground state energies via VMC, and then analyzes correlated observables like $S(q)$ and $\epsilon(q)$ across varying system sizes to prove convergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Self-attention neural networks provide powerful variational wavefunctions that surpass the expressivity of traditional variational ansatze. This expressivity, however, comes with increased computational complexity, raising a pressing question about scalability -- can such wavefunctions retain their accuracy at large system sizes? We apply self-attention wavefunctions to the two-dimensional homogeneous electron gas for up to N=169 particles, obtaining energies systematically lower than state-of-the-art DMC. Direct access to the ground state wavefunction further lets us recover the full collective-mode dispersion of the liquid phase, from the small-q plasmon branch to a roton-like minimum near q=2k_F. Observables at N=91 and N=169 are in near-perfect agreement, indicating convergence to the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces FrozenLGP, an adaptive technique designed to make graph partitioning robust for QAOA on limited quantum hardware. It overcomes the failure of standard divide-and-conquer methods on dense graphs by identifying and 'freezing' critical vertices using max-flow computations. This process preserves the problem's energy structure by folding interactions into local bias terms, enabling full coverage and improved noise resilience.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations (qubit connectivity and depth) in quantum algorithms by providing a systematic, mathematically rigorous method to adapt problem decomposition for NISQ devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing robust methods for solving combinatorial optimization problems, like MaxCut, using QAOA on near-term quantum devices when the problem graph is too large or highly connected for standard decomposition techniques.

**Main result.** The proposed FrozenLGP framework achieves 100% decomposition coverage across tested graphs by adaptively freezing obstructing vertices and folding their interactions into linear bias terms in the Hamiltonian, significantly outperforming baseline methods on dense instances.

**Method.** FrozenLGP identifies minimum-vertex cuts using max-flow to find obstructing nodes; these nodes are classically frozen, and their energetic contributions are preserved by updating local bias fields (Ising field) of neighboring active qubits.

**Model / system.** The model is QAOA applied to combinatorial optimization problems mapped onto an Ising Hamiltonian ($H_P$). The physical system involves near-term quantum hardware constraints regarding qubit capacity and gate depth.

**Key observables.** Decomposition Coverage (aiming for 100%), Approximation Quality ($\langle H_P angle$), Circuit Depth, and Number of two-qubit entangling gates (CNOTs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph size (up to 10,000 vertices), connectivity density, and the budget parameter ($B_f$) controlling the freezing process.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption addressed is that standard divide-and-conquer QAOA fails when small vertex separators do not exist; FrozenLGP overcomes this by enforcing partitionability via freezing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mechanism: showing how standard LGP partitions graphs, how failure occurs on dense graphs, and finally demonstrating FrozenLGP identifying and freezing a crossing node while folding interactions into bias terms to maintain circuit validity.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of DC-QAOA, proposes FrozenLGP as an adaptive framework using min-cut/max-flow for vertex identification, details the Hamiltonian modification via bias folding, validates performance through coverage metrics and noise simulations, and concludes with resource comparisons against monolithic QAOA.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Divide-and-conquer variants of the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) provide a promising route for executing combinatorial optimization problems beyond the qubit capacity of near-term quantum devices. However, existing approaches rely on the existence of small vertex separators and fail entirely on dense or highly connected graphs where such decompositions do not exist. We introduce Frozen Large Graph Partitioning (FrozenLGP), an adaptive decomposition framework that transforms partitionability from an assumption into an enforceable property. When standard partitioning fails, FrozenLGP identifies the minimum set of obstructing vertices through a minimum-vertex-cut computation based on max-flow and classically freezes their spin assignments. The energetic contributions of the removed interactions are rigorously preserved by folding them into linear bias terms in the Ising Hamiltonian of neighboring active qubits. Across graph sizes up to 10,000 vertices and multiple topology families, FrozenLGP achieves 100\% decomposition coverage, compared with 4.6\% for the standard divide-and-conquer baseline on high-connectivity instances. End-to-end MaxCut experiments demonstrate that FrozenLGP preserves approximation quality on instances already solvable by conventional divide-and-conquer QAOA while extending applicability to previously unsupported graphs, and outperforming alternative full-coverage decomposition strategies. Noise simulations further show improved robustness arising from reduced entangling-gate requirements. These results establish FrozenLGP as a topology-robust front end for distributed QAOA on near-term quantum hardware.

</details>

### [An Effective Quantum Hoare Logic for Hybrid Quantum Programs with Unbounded Loops](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08548v1)

**Authors:** Christophe Chareton, Jad Issa, Romain Péchoux  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08548v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel quantum Hoare logic designed to statically analyze hybrid quantum programs containing unbounded loops. The core innovation is the use of Integer Hybrid Path-Sums (IHPS) to symbolically track both quantum states and classical integer counts. This allows for the first semi-automated resource estimation tool for these complex, industrially relevant quantum computing models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides essential formal tools for analyzing complex, real-world hybrid quantum algorithms that are necessary before deploying such systems on actual hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a static analysis solution for hybrid quantum-classical algorithms that incorporate complex control structures like unbounded loops.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Integer Hybrid Path-Sums (IHPS) and a corresponding non-branching, effective quantum Hoare logic capable of providing both functional correctness guarantees and resource consumption estimation for such programs.

**Method.** They extend path-sums to IHPS to symbolically represent infinite-dimensional Classical-Quantum states, enabling the formulation of an invariant-based loop analysis rule within a novel quantum Hoare logic framework.

**Model / system.** The focus is on hybrid quantum-classical algorithms running on theoretical models (like $	ext{HQbricks}^\infty$), involving sequences of unitary operations combined with classical control flow and unbounded integer loops.

**Key observables.** Functional correctness, Almost-Sure Termination (AST), and expected resource consumption (e.g., expected number of gates or loop iterations).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis handles the complexity introduced by unbounded while loops and the interaction between quantum superpositions and classical integer counters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous QHLs are insufficient due to lack of expressiveness for hybridization or handling unbounded recursion; the solution relies on IHPS to manage this complexity.

**Figures summary.** One figure illustrates the circuit diagram for a repeat-until-success (RUS) unitary synthesis program, which is used as an example in the analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces IHPS as the core representation, develops the quantum Hoare logic based on transformations of these sums, proves soundness via induction over program structure, and details loop handling using invariant rules.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While quantum hardware remains limited, hybrid quantum-classical algorithms with complex control structures, including unbounded loops, are emerging, posing new challenges for quantum program analysis, including the accurate estimation of the resource consumption of a given program. Meanwhile, precise analysis techniques such as symbolic execution have largely left out hybridization and unbounded recursion. On the other hand, current quantum Hoare logics that generally support them are lacking in expressiveness and miss out on efficient computational equational reasoning that could be implemented in a semi-automated tool. This leaves a gap awaiting to be filled. In this work, we answer this challenge with the first semi-automated static analysis solution combining effective functional verification and resource (termination or cost) estimation for hybrid quantum programs with unbounded loops. Towards that end, we introduce integer hybrid path-sums (IHPS), extending path-sums to handle unbounded while loops, as a representation of possible executions of a program. A generic strategy for determining termination and expected resource consumption via loop invariants is also proposed and illustrated on several examples. Finally, the solution is implemented as a semi-automatic Haskell program. This work is the first step toward the design of a complete static resource analysis tool for hybrid quantum programs, essential for the development of real-world quantum computing.

</details>

**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08102v1>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel, scalable quantum approach using decomposition methods to solve the computationally hard Maximal Covering Location Problem (MCLP) for satellite constellation design. By partitioning the large problem into smaller subproblems solved via QAOA and then merging the results, the authors overcome the limitations of current quantum hardware on massive instances. The framework shows promise by maintaining high coverage performance while achieving better scalability than classical or standard quantum methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on optimization rather than fundamental physics, the methodology—applying divide-and-conquer techniques to map a complex combinatorial problem onto NISQ devices—is highly relevant for developing scalable quantum algorithms for physical simulations in condensed matter or AMO contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is optimizing satellite constellation design by solving the NP-hard Maximal Covering Location Problem (MCLP) to maximize ground coverage.

**Main result.** A decomposition-based QAOA framework demonstrates better scalability and competitive coverage performance for large MCLP instances compared to standard quantum or classical solvers.

**Method.** The method uses spectral bisection on a co-observation graph to partition the large MCLP into smaller subproblems, which are then solved independently using QAOA circuits before being combined via quantum reconstruction strategies (GSR/QSR).

**Model / system.** The system is an Earth observation satellite constellation modeled by orbital slots and ground targets. The problem is formulated as an Ising Hamiltonian derived from the MCLP constraints, which are then mapped onto a QUBO representation.

**Key observables.** Coverage Ratio (Approximation ratio $ho$), Runtime ($T$), and Optimality Gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of orbital slots ($J$), number of satellites ($N$), maximum subgraph size ($q_{	ext{max}}$), and circuit depth ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that decomposing the problem into smaller, hardware-tractable subproblems mitigates the exponential scaling issues faced by standard QAOA on large instances. The use of GSR is shown to outperform QSR.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decomposition framework flow; Figure 12 compares approximation ratios ($ho$) between IBM hardware and OpenQAOA simulator, showing noise effects; Table VIII summarizes mean runtimes with 95% Confidence Intervals for various visibility matrix instances.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes MCLP as an intractable problem. It then introduces the decomposition-based QAOA framework, detailing graph construction via spectral bisection and solving subproblems using QAOA. Finally, it compares the performance of different merging strategies (GSR vs QSR) on both simulated and actual quantum hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

An increase in earth observation missions has increased the demand of efficient design and optimization of satellite constellations. Maximizing coverage of the target while effectively utilizing the limited orbital resources is one of the critical design challenges for complex combinatorial optimization problems. The maximal covering location problem (MCLP), serves as a base for orbital coverage modeling, is NP-hard and computationally intractable for large-constellation instances. Using heuristics, metaheuristics, and mixed-integer linear programming, classical solvers have achieved optimal or near-optimal results, yet their scalability is limited as the problem size increases. Quantum computing advancements, including the quantum approximate optimization algorithms, offer a potential solution to NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems. Current quantum hardware limitations, such as low qubit counts and circuit depth, restrict solutions for small-scale instance problems. To address this challenge, this paper proposes a scalable quantum optimization framework for MCLP in satellite constellation design. A decomposition-based quantum methodology is proposed, in which large MCLP instances are partitioned into subgraphs by classical decomposition, optimized independently via quantum optimization circuits, and combined using quantum reconstruction strategies. Computational results across different constellation sizes reveal better scalability in less time while maintaining competitive coverage performance compared to classical solvers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents efficient methods to synthesize complex multi-controlled gates in qudit quantum computing. It significantly improves the theoretical upper bounds on gate counts required for these operations, achieving polynomial scaling in the number of qubits ($O(n^2)$). The results are vital for advancing hardware implementations utilizing higher-dimensional quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides concrete, optimized circuit decompositions for high-dimensional quantum operations, which is crucial for designing practical algorithms on qudit architectures that move beyond simple qubit models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently synthesizing quantum circuits for multi-controlled gates, particularly $(n-1)$-controlled unitaries, in qudit systems.

**Main result.** It achieves improved gate count bounds, reducing the required CINC gates for general $(n-1)$-controlled unitaries to $O(n^2)$ and special unitaries to $O(n)$.

**Method.** The authors develop novel circuit synthesis schemes by decomposing complex controlled operations into products of fundamental building blocks like CINC gates and single-qudit gates.

**Model / system.** The model is based on qudit systems ($d$-level quantum computing) operating in a Hilbert space $\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}_d$. The focus is on constructing circuits for controlled unitaries, isometries, and quantum channels.

**Key observables.** Gate counts (CINC, GCX), circuit complexity bounds, and theoretical lower bounds on gate requirements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qudits ($n$), the dimension $d$, and the control structure (e.g., $(n-1)$-controlled).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on decomposing unitaries into specific controlled gates and assumes that classical control after measurement is allowed for channel synthesis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantum circuits for multi-controlled qudit gates, including representations for $C_{1,2}^{2}(U)$ using both CINC/single-qudit gates and $	ext{SUM}$ gates. Other figures detail circuit structures for general $(n-1)$-controlled operations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing efficient decompositions for controlled unitaries (e.g., $C_{1,2}^{2}(U)$), then generalizing these results to arbitrary $(n-1)$-controlled gates with improved complexity bounds, and finally deriving theoretical lower bounds on the required gate counts.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Circuit synthesis of multi-controlled gates is crucial for qudit ($d$-level) quantum computing. This paper presents efficient synthesis schemes that reduce the elementary gate count for multi-controlled single-qudit gates. For synthesizing general $(n-1)$-controlled unitaries on $n$ qudits, we reduce the controlled-increment (CINC) and generalized controlled-$X$ (GCX) gate counts to $O(n^2)$, improving upon existing $O(n^{2+\log_2 d})$ CINC and $O(n^3)$ GCX bounds. For $(n-1)$-controlled special unitaries, this complexity is further reduced to $O(n)$. By utilizing the proposed circuit, we present qudit-based circuit constructions for isometries and quantum channels from $n$ to $m$ qudits. When specialized to general $n$-qudit unitaries, our construction requires fewer CINC gates than previous results. Moreover, for the first time, we present a circuit synthesis scheme for single-controlled gates using SUM gates and single-qudit gates when $d$ is prime. This enables all CINC-based circuits for various quantum operations to be converted into SUM-gate circuits while preserving the same asymptotic complexity. Finally, we establish a theoretical lower bound on the number of SUM and CINC gates required to synthesize general $n$-qudit unitaries.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops NuQuLib, a comprehensive framework to translate realistic nuclear Hamiltonians from Chiral EFT into qubit circuits suitable for quantum computing. It systematically benchmarks three key quantum algorithms by estimating their resource costs (T-gates) for solving the many-body Schrödinger equation in atomic nuclei.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges advanced theoretical nuclear structure physics (ChEFT, shell model) with the practical requirements of near-term quantum computation, providing a crucial benchmark for future hardware development in fundamental physics simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish a systematic framework for benchmarking quantum algorithms designed to solve complex many-body problems arising in nuclear physics.

**Main result.** The authors provide the NuQuLib software stack, which quantifies scaling trends and resource requirements (T-gate counts) for applying three major eigenvalue algorithms (QPE, QKrylov, ODMD) to realistic nuclear Hamiltonians.

**Method.** A unified workflow maps second-quantized nuclear interactions (from Chiral EFT) into qubit Hamiltonians, allowing systematic comparison of quantum algorithm resource needs across different model spaces and interaction types.

**Model / system.** The system is the atomic nucleus, modeled by realistic Hamiltonians derived from Chiral Effective Field Theory ($\chi$EFT), incorporating two-body (NN) and three-body (3N) interactions. Calculations span both no-core and valence-space formulations.

**Key observables.** Resource requirements quantified in terms of T-gate counts and system size, alongside the energy eigenvalues ($E_i$) derived from solving $H|\Psi_iangle = E_i|\Psi_iangle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity scales significantly with interaction type (e.g., scaling as $N^4$ for NN and $N^6$ for 3N matrix elements).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on mapping fermionic operators to qubit Hamiltonians, necessitating approximations or truncations due to the rapid growth of matrix elements.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the entire NuQuLib workflow: starting from a nuclear interaction (ChEFT) $ightarrow$ Hamiltonian Encoding $ightarrow$ Circuit generation for quantum algorithms. Figure 2 shows scaling of non-zero matrix elements based on model space truncation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by detailing the physical inputs (ChEFT Hamiltonians, second quantization). It then presents the core contribution: a workflow to map these into qubit circuits and estimates resources for specific quantum algorithms ($	ext{QPE}$, $	ext{QKrylov}$, $	ext{ODMD}$), concluding with resource scaling analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a framework for benchmarking quantum algorithms for nuclear many-body systems based on realistic nuclear Hamiltonians such as chiral effective field theory. To this effect we introduce a workflow that maps nuclear interactions in second quantization formalism to qubit Hamiltonians. This enables the systematic construction of benchmark instances spanning no-core and valence-space formulations with two-body (NN) and selected three-body (3N) interactions. Then, we proceed to provide resource estimates for three representative eigenvalue algorithms: Quantum Phase Estimation, Quantum Krylov methods, and Observable Dynamic Mode Decomposition. We compare their resource requirements in terms of T-gate counts and system size, and examine the impact of model-space choices and many-body interactions. The primitives included in our analysis are Trotterization, Qubitization, and Quantum Singular Value Transformation. Our results quantify scaling trends across algorithms and problem classes, and provide a basis for consistent comparisons of quantum approaches to nuclear many-body problems. The implementation is provided by the NuQuLib software stack.

</details>
